# Engineering log

Production-readiness work, newest entry last. Each entry follows Discover → Reproduce → Root cause → Fix → Regression test → Verify → Document.

Status words are exact:

- **VERIFIED** — the named command or real entry point was run after the change and its result observed.
- **FIXED** — the change is made; the stated verification has not been run yet.
- **OPEN** — reproduced or confirmed, not fixed.
- **UNVERIFIED** — a claim nobody has checked. Never evidence.

Severity: P0 data loss, security or crash; P1 a broken documented entry point or behavior; P2 a degraded gate, tool or misleading result; P3 cosmetic or documentation.

Environment for local results unless an entry says otherwise: Linux x86_64 workstation, Wayland session, Rust toolchain from `rust-toolchain.toml`, build container `peersus-ui-build`, target directory on disk (`target/ui`). Worktree: `main` at `f74adb8` plus uncommitted development changes; a result recorded here does not transfer to another revision.

## Baseline (2026-11-10)

| Fact | Value | How measured |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Application workspace Rust | 131 923 lines in 12 crates plus `xtask` | line count of `*.rs`, build output excluded |
| Local extension sources | 14 772 lines in 4 directories under `plugins/` | same |
| Largest source files | `peersus-ui/src/view.rs` 11 670, `peersus-ui/src/lib.rs` 8 557, `peersus-app/src/runtime.rs` 7 972 | same |

| Strict gate at start | **red**, at the first step | `./check.sh` in the build container |
| Strict gate after E-001…E-003 | **green**: fmt, metadata, check, clippy (`-D warnings`), test, audit; 652 s wall; every test binary 0 failed (largest: interface 630, owner 628, transport 228, core 114, broker 91) | same command, exit 0; six script tests skipped here (E-002) |

## Entries

### E-001 — P2 — Strict gate red at formatting — VERIFIED

- **Discover/Reproduce:** `./check.sh` stopped at `cargo fmt --all -- --check` with one diff in `crates/peersus-net/src/relay_policy.rs`.
- **Root cause:** an uncommitted edit was never formatted.
- **Fix:** `rustfmt --edition 2024` on that one file; no semantic change.
- **Verify:** `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exits 0.

### E-002 — P2 — Metadata gate cannot run on a workstation with an enforced commit identity — VERIFIED (with a stated limit)

- **Discover/Reproduce:** `python3 scripts/test-deploy.py` and `scripts/test-extension-source.py` fail in fixture setup. Both make a throwaway commit under a fixture identity; `test-deploy.py` then pushes it unsigned to a local bare repository. The workstation's commit policy refuses a foreign identity and an unsigned push.
- **Root cause:** the tests assume nothing constrains `git commit` and `git push` on the machine that runs them.
- **Decision:** the policy is the owner's control. It is neither overridden nor routed around (no other identity source, no direct binary, no signing with a personal key from a test).
- **Fix:** each fixture skips with an explicit reason only when the refusal carries the policy's own marker; every other failure still fails. Hosted CI has no such policy and runs all of them.
- **Verify:** `test-deploy.py` 7 run, 1 skipped; `test-extension-source.py` 5 run, 5 skipped; both exit 0.
- **Limit:** on this workstation those six tests do not execute. Their coverage here is **UNVERIFIED**; it exists only on a machine without the policy.

### E-003 — P1 — Extension source directory renamed, consumers left behind — FIXED

- **Discover:** local extension sources now live in `plugins/`; the application branch still pointed at `plugin/`.
- **Reproduce:** `scripts/package-upload-wasm.py` (`cargo xtask upload-wasm`), `scripts/prepare-extension-branch.py` and `scripts/deploy-plugin.py` resolved a directory that no longer exists. `.gitignore` ignored only `/plugin/`, so `git status` listed `plugins/` — four source trees and their build output — as untracked and one `git add` away from the application branch, which must never carry extension source.
- **Root cause:** a directory rename without migrating its consumers.
- **Fix:** `.gitignore` ignores `/plugins/`; the three scripts, the export test fixtures and the build hints in `crates/peersus-plugin-runtime/src/tests.rs` use `plugins/`. `deploy-plugin.py`'s usage text named a script that does not exist (`scripts/deploy.py`); it now names itself.
- **Not changed on purpose:** `scripts/check-extension-source.py` stages a submission under `plugin/<name>/` inside a *disposable SDK copy*. That layout belongs to the submission contract documented for the `extension` branch, not to the local checkout.
- **Verify so far:** `test-prepare-extension-branch.py` 7 passed. `cargo xtask upload-wasm` and a real `deploy-plugin.py` run are **UNVERIFIED**.
- **Still stale:** `docs/development.md` and `docs/release-readiness.md` cite `plugin/…` paths (tracked under "Documentation").

### E-004 — P2 — `workspace_member` in the submission validator cannot be satisfied — OPEN

- **Discover:** `scripts/check-extension-source.py` accepts `workspace_member` only when it matches `plugin/<name>` and is also listed in the SDK's workspace members.
- **Evidence:** the application workspace declares no `plugin/…` member (its members are `crates/…` and `xtask`). The two published submissions that use the key (`clipboard`, `upload`) write `crates/peersus-<name>`, which the pattern refuses and which the workspace no longer contains either. The `extension` branch README still describes the key with a `crates/…` example. No test covers the branch.
- **Consequence:** the overlay branch of the validator is unreachable; those two submissions cannot pass the current validator, and the documentation describes a path that does not work. Independent of the `plugin/` → `plugins/` rename.
- **Not reproduced by execution:** the validator needs a fixture commit, which this workstation refuses (E-002). The conclusion is from reading the pattern, the workspace manifest and the two `submission.toml` files.
- **Fix:** pending a contract decision — see "Decisions for the owner".

### E-005 — P2 — The `demo` feature was outside every gate and never run as a window — VERIFIED (Linux)

- **Discover:** the gate's `check`, `clippy` and `test` steps ran without the feature; the demo module, its tests, the argument gate test and the `replay_bench` example were compiled by no gate step. `--demo` had never been opened as a real window.
- **Reproduce:** `cargo test -p peersus-ui --features demo demo` had to be run by hand to learn that its five tests pass.
- **Fix:**
  - A `headless` feature (`peersus-ui/src/headless.rs`) opens the demo's real root component on the toolkit's own headless runner (`freya-testing`, same pinned version, optional dependency): real layout, events and CPU drawing, no display. Animations are brought to rest so that a screen is asserted and drawn as it settles.
  - Six screen tests (`src/headless/tests.rs`): every screen at the desktop and the upright width shows its own words and draws a distinct picture; the demo marker is on the screen; pressing each tab changes the screen at both widths; a refused command changes nothing shown; one screen drawn twice gives identical bytes; refused arguments open nothing.
  - `examples/demo_shots.rs` writes a PNG of every screen and Settings tab for before/after review.
  - The gate compiles, lints and tests the feature build (`xtask`: `check`, `clippy`, `test`). The test step runs library tests only: an integration test would make Cargo build the program with the feature on where the ordinary development build is expected.
- **Verify:**
  - `cargo test -p peersus-ui --features headless --lib -- demo headless::` → 11 passed.
  - `cargo clippy -p peersus-ui --all-targets --features headless -- -D warnings` → clean.
  - The real program, `peersus-ui --demo --demo-page=settings`, on a private X server (Xvfb, debug build): the window mapped, drew the Settings screen (captured and read; same layout as the headless picture), stayed alive, used 1 CPU tick in 10 idle seconds and 188 MB resident. That is a debug build on a software-rendered X server: evidence that the entry point works, not a performance figure.
- **Limits:** not opened on Wayland, Windows or macOS. The headless runner has no GPU surface, window system or frame pacing. It asks the system for a clipboard handle when it starts (the toolkit does); nothing reads or writes it.
- **Dependency note:** `freya-testing 0.5.0-rc.13` is the one package added to the lockfile; it is reachable only through the `headless` feature.

### E-006 — P3 — Screenshot environment hooks are read by product builds — OPEN

- **Discover:** `PEERSUS_PAGE`, `PEERSUS_MENU` and `PEERSUS_OFFER` are read unconditionally in `crates/peersus-ui/src/lib.rs`. `PEERSUS_OFFER=1` draws a synthetic incoming offer over a real window; a non-empty `PEERSUS_PAGE` skips the first-run name screen.
- **Consumer:** `store-demo.sh` sets `PEERSUS_PAGE` on an ordinary build, so that one has a real user. No consumer of the other two was found in scripts, docs or CI.
- **Risk:** local environment only (whoever sets it already controls the process); nothing the owner holds changes. Low, but it is test scaffolding in the product path now that the `demo` feature exists.

### E-007 — Performance baseline and measuring tools — VERIFIED (Linux, synthetic)

- **Tooling:** `./dev.sh bench` (`cargo xtask bench`, see `docs/development.md` "Measuring"). The demo can change its snapshot (`--demo-replay=HZ`) and measure itself (`--demo-probe=SECONDS`, `peersus-ui/src/probe.rs`); `demo_window` opens it without building the product program with the feature. Kept runs: `benchmark-results/<name>.json` (untracked).
- **Verify:** the whole chain ran end to end three times; six repeated 3-second probes ended with exit 0 and a report each. `cargo clippy --all-targets --features headless -D warnings` clean for the interface; `xtask` clippy clean, 8 tests pass.
- **Defect found while building it:** the first version ended the probe with `process::exit` from its own thread; one of two runs panicked on the main thread in the text shaper while exit handlers ran under a frame being drawn. The probe now asks the event loop to end (the toolkit's own exit event), and the bench refuses a report from a window that then ended badly.
- **Second defect of the probe, found later by a core dump:** on a screen that was still redrawing, the window did not end when asked: the run took five seconds longer, and twice ended in a segmentation fault. A core taken during the wait showed the main thread inside the probe's own plugin, waiting for the probe's lock, which the probe's thread held through its five-second wait (a guard living to the end of an `if let`). The lock is now let go before the wait; a window that does not end when asked is said on standard error and ends the run with exit code 2 instead of 0. The fault was the tool's, on every run of a redrawing screen; no product code was involved. Reports written before the fix are unaffected (the report was written first).
- **Baseline** (release, commit `f74adb859e7e` plus the uncommitted work of this log, workstation, private in-memory X server, software OpenGL — `llvmpipe` —, fixture of 16 devices and 128 transfers, 10 s per window run):

| What | Median | p95 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `View::of` (snapshot → what the screens show) | 18 µs | 19 µs |
| `view::refresh`, same / changed snapshot | 21 µs / 21 µs | 23 µs / 23 µs |
| Snapshot JSON encode / decode | 41 µs / 120 µs | 45 µs / 131 µs |
| One screen built, laid out and drawn from nothing, headless CPU (five screens) | 157–194 ms | 166–222 ms |
| Encrypted loopback transfer, 16 MiB | 136 MiB/s | — |
| History update at 1 / 100 / 1000 records | 0.02 / 0.07 / 0.48 ms | — |

| Window run | First frame | Redraws/s | CPU of one core | Resident | Whole frame median / p95 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| quiet (Send, nothing moving) | 253 ms | 0.1 | 0.2 % | 151 MB | — (1 frame) |
| still (Transfers, rows going, snapshot unchanged) | 227 ms | 11.4 | 18.6 % | 152 MB | 3.8 / 5.1 ms |
| replay (Transfers, snapshot changing at 30 Hz) | 247 ms | 161 | 277 % | 154 MB | 5.1 / 7.7 ms |

  Of the first frame, about 180–200 ms pass before the window exists and 42–48 ms are the frame's own work. Start-up CPU 240–260 ms; 28 threads; no disk bytes read or written in any run. In a frame, presenting takes 3.4–4.1 ms and rendering 0.4 ms: on this software driver the present dominates, which says nothing of a GPU.
- **Limits:** one machine, one display server, software rendering, no frame pacing; no owner process, extension, network or saved state; allocations not counted (see "Decisions for the owner"); nothing measured on a GPU surface, Wayland, Android, Windows or macOS. Run-to-run spread seen between three baselines: first frame 224–253 ms, replay redraws 161–197/s. A change smaller than that is not a result.

### E-008 — P2 — The Peers screen kept redrawing while nothing changed — FIXED, VERIFIED by measurement (Linux, synthetic)

- **Discover:** 4-second probes of each screen on a snapshot that never changes (same build and display as E-007, default fixture of 3 devices and 4 transfers):

| Screen | Redraws/s | CPU of one core |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Send | 0.2 | 0.4 % |
| Transfers, no transfer or one finished | 0.2 | 0.3 % |
| Settings | 3.7 | 8.9 % |
| Receive | 22 | 31 % |
| Transfers, one transfer moving | 35 | 44–48 % |
| Peers | 227 | 336 % |

- **First reading, WRONG:** from the per-second averages above it was concluded that Peers, Receive and a Transfers list with a moving row never come to rest. A per-second average over four seconds cannot tell a short burst from a steady redraw.
- **Corrected by measurement** (same program, total frames after the first one, 3-second run against 12-second run; taken while another build loaded the machine, so the rates are lower than above):

| Screen | Frames in 3 s | Frames in 12 s | Reading |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Transfers, one transfer moving | 61 | 65 | one burst, then at rest |
| Receive | 51 | 50 | one burst, then at rest |
| Peers | 458 | 1483 | never at rest |

- **Reading:** Receive and Transfers draw about 50–110 frames right after the first one and then stop; it is a cost of opening the screen, not of leaving it open. Settings does the same on a smaller scale (12 frames in 12 s, none in the second half). Only Peers redrew without end. Inferred from source and not proven by an experiment: the burst of Receive and Transfers is the progress bar's opening transition (500 ms, from its value to the same value), drawn as often as the CPU allows on a display that does not pace frames. In the E-007 table, the "still" row is this burst averaged over ten seconds; see the re-taken figures below.
- **Cause, Peers, first guess, INCOMPLETE:** the pairing circle ran the sparks of its fuse unconditionally (`use_sparks(reduced, true)`). Passing whether a code is on show changed nothing measurable: 2317 frames in 12 s, 190 a second in the second half.
- **Cause, Peers, found by the measurement above failing:** the fuse itself. Its animation was started when the page was made and ran for the life of a code (five minutes), code or no code; the toolkit's animation loop asks for a redraw on every tick whether or not anything reads the value. So every opening of Peers redrew the whole window for five minutes.
- **Fix:** `PairingSection` uses the same two hooks as the Send circle (`use_fuse`, `use_sparks`), and both burn only while this device's code is on show: the fuse from what the code has left at that moment (`made_at`), so that hiding and showing the code again neither restarts it nor leaves it running unseen. Reduced motion is as before (fuse whole, sparks still).
- **Verify** (same display and build profile as E-007, default fixture, 12-second probe, idle machine):

| Peers | Frames after the first | In the second half | CPU time | CPU of one core |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| before | 2225 | (not reported then) | 33.2 s | 277 % |
| after the sparks only | 2317 | 1142 | — | 288 % |
| after the fuse too | 2 | 0 | 0.034 s | 0.3 % |

- **Not validated:** a code actually on show (the demo's commands are refused, so no code can be made in it): that the fuse and sparks still burn then, from the right place, is read from the hooks' rules and from the Send circle using the same ones, and has not been seen. To be looked at on a real device with the pairing of Phase 5. Nothing measured on a paced display or a phone.
- **Tool defect found by this, FIXED:** the bench's `window_still` figure was the opening burst averaged over the run; it said nothing of a window at rest. The probe now reports the second half of the run on its own (`steady.settled`). Re-taken baseline: quiet 1 frame, settled 0; still 82 frames, settled 0 (13.6 % of a core over the run, all of it in the burst); replay at 30 Hz 174 redraws a second, settled 164, 297 % of a core (unpaced).
- **Left as it is:** the progress bar's 500 ms opening transition to its own value (in the vendored component). It is bounded and ends; changing it is a patch to third-party code for a cost not yet measured on a paced display.

### E-009 — P1 — One subscriber that stopped reading cost every subscriber its events (A-01) — FIXED (broker), VERIFIED by test

- **Discover:** a published event was delivered to all subscribers or to none: one subscriber with a full queue (64 deliveries) made the publish fail for the application and every other extension subscribed to the same event, for as long as it did not read. The application discards that failure on purpose (an event is a notice; a `list` method says what is true), so the events were lost for everyone with no sign. This contradicts the isolation rule: one extension must not be able to stop what others receive.
- **Reproduce:** `hub::tests::a_subscriber_that_does_not_read_costs_nobody_else_an_event`, written first: failed on the publish after the 64th (`QueueFull`), with a second subscriber that had read everything.
- **Fix:** `peersus-plugin/src/hub.rs`, publish: each subscriber is delivered to on its own; one without room misses that event alone, stays subscribed and receives again once it reads. The slot reserved for a pending call's result is still never given to an event. A missed event is traced in development builds. The contract is stated in `docs/api/service-messages.md`.
- **Verify:** the new test, `a_publisher_that_does_not_read_its_own_events_is_not_refused_either` and the adjusted `a_flood_of_events_cannot_starve_a_reply_slot` pass; the crate's 101 tests pass; clippy clean.
- **Contract change:** a publisher is no longer told `QueueFull` when a subscriber is behind. No caller in the workspace acted on it (the application ignored it; a guest's link reports a refusal only for calls). The earlier test asserted the all-or-nothing rule and was replaced, not weakened: it now asserts who misses an event and who does not.
- **Considered and not done:** disconnecting a subscriber whose queue is full. An extension that is suspended for a legitimate reason (a phone in the background) would be restarted and, after repeats, stopped for the person; missing notices it can ask for again is the smaller harm.
- **Remaining from A-01 (now P2, with A-04):** a control-frame write to an extension that never reads has no time limit. It is bounded (its queue holds 64, calls to it expire at their deadline, nobody else waits on it), but the application goes on showing that extension as connected. A liveness rule belongs with the per-extension limits of A-04.
- **Not validated:** on a phone, or with a real extension made to stop reading.

### E-010 — P2 — A refused inbound stream left a record behind for good (A-05) — FIXED, VERIFIED by test

- **Discover:** `peersus-app/src/runtime.rs`, `route_inbound`: the call was recorded as "a receive for this extension" before the stream was set aside and before the extension was told. When either step refused (32 streams already waiting, or the broker refusing the call), the record stayed: it is removed only when the call's result arrives, and a call that was never made has none. A paired, permitted device opening streams while none could be taken grew that map without bound.
- **Reproduce:** by reading the order of the three statements; no failing run of the old code was made (the function needs two live transport streams).
- **Fix:** the three steps are one helper, `keep_and_announce`: the stream is set aside, the extension is told, and only then is the call recorded; a refusal at either step leaves nothing kept and ends the device's stream at once.
- **Verify:** `runtime::tests::a_stream_that_is_refused_leaves_no_record_and_one_that_is_announced_leaves_both` (refused announcement, full waiting room tried ten times, and the accepted case).
- **Not validated:** with two real devices.

### E-011 — P1 — Android Settings showed rows for services no installed extension uses (A-02) — FIXED in source; desktop tests pass; NOT yet built for Android or seen on a phone

- **Discover:** on Android, Settings → Device always showed "Background clipboard access" and "Remote input accessibility". Each is the platform's means of serving one of the application's services (`peersus.clipboard`, `peersus.input`) to extensions; the application itself uses neither. With no extension installed they were rows about nothing, against the rule that the application without extensions has no residue of them. The common interface also carried their titles, two dedicated types and search words naming the system-level providers the Android adapter works with.
- **Fix:** one generic `link::ServiceSetting` (the service it serves, title, search words, status, open, switch) replaces the two types; `Platform.service_settings` is a list the adapter fills. `view::wants_service` — an installed extension imports that service from the application — decides whether a row is shown and indexed for search. The titles and provider words now live in the Android adapter; the common interface names no service and no provider.
- **Verify:** `view::tests::a_platform_setting_for_a_service_is_wanted_only_while_an_installed_extension_imports_it_from_the_application`; the search test now uses a made-up setting; the interface's 647 library tests pass. The local sandboxed (WASM) copies of the clipboard and control extensions, the form Android runs, import `host`/`peersus.clipboard` and `host`/`peersus.input`, so their rows return when they are installed.
- **Not validated:** the Android crate is not compiled by the desktop gate; both phones, with and without each extension, are still to be checked (Phase 5).

### E-012 — P2 — A permission of the application could be granted without words saying what it grants (A-12) — FIXED, VERIFIED by test

- **Discover:** what each permission of the application lets an extension do was written in three places: the constants beside each service, the list the application's manifest was built from, and a second list in the interface that chose the words to show. The interface's list had eight of the fourteen. For the other six (the arrangement of desktops, the two transfer permissions and the three status permissions) it fell through to "No description was given for this permission." on the consent row. Seven of the fourteen sentences (those six and the one for pointer and keyboard input) had no translation.
- **Reproduce:** by reading the committed interface list, which names eight permissions and has no arm for the other six. The new test was not run against the old code.
- **Fix:** one table, `peersus_app::runtime::HOST_CAPABILITIES` (service, permission, words). The application's manifest is built from it, and the interface asks `host_capability_description` instead of keeping a list of its own. The seven sentences that had none are translated into the eleven other languages.
- **Verify:** `view::tests::every_permission_the_application_declares_has_its_own_words_on_the_screen_in_every_language` walks the table: every permission has its own sentence, in English exactly the declared one and in every other language a different one. The earlier test of four permissions still passes.
- **Limit:** the translations were written here and not reviewed by a speaker of each language; they are consent text, so a review is worth having before a release.

### E-013 — P2 — Two clipboard tests failed once under the full gate's load — test changed; cause inferred, not reproduced

- **Discover:** one full gate run failed in `peersus-app` on `desktop_clipboard::tests::a_timed_out_owner_frees_its_place_only_when_the_thread_exits` and `two_still_blocked_threads_are_the_bound_not_two_timeouts` (627 passed, 2 failed). Both asserted that a read which timed out after 20 ms had left its owner thread inside the store.
- **Reproduce:** not reproduced. The same two tests passed five times alone and three times in the crate's whole suite right after. The file had uncommitted changes from before this work; nothing in this work touched it until the test change below.
- **Cause (read in source, not proven):** the owner thread skips a request whose caller has already given up. A thread that the scheduler starts later than the 20 ms deadline therefore never goes inside the store and exits, which is correct behavior and leaves nothing stuck; the tests assumed the thread always got there first.
- **Fix (tests only):** the fixture's gate counts reads that went inside; a helper reads until one read has timed out with its thread inside, waiting after each for the thread to be either inside or gone. The assertions on how many owners were made follow the number of reads it took. No product code changed.
- **Verify:** 40 runs of the clipboard tests in a row, the first part of them with 48 busy loops on 8 cores: 40 passed. Because the original failure was never reproduced on demand, this shows the new tests hold under load, not that the old ones would have failed under the same load.

### E-014 — P2 — The custom theme editor was shown in English in every language — FIXED, VERIFIED by test (not seen in a window in another language)

- **Discover:** `peersus-ui/src/appearance.rs` (the editor under Settings → Appearance) wrote every title, button, role name, sample text and status note as an English literal; none of them was in the translation catalog, and the file was not among the sources the catalog-coverage test reads, so nothing reported it. The rest of Settings is translated into twelve languages.
- **Reproduce:** by reading the source and searching the catalog for fifteen of its strings (none found).
- **Fix:** every text of the editor goes through the catalog; 88 messages added in the eleven translated languages. What an action came to is a value (`Note`) and not a text, so it is said in the language in use when it is shown, and a file read on another thread needs no language. The search index's details for these rows are translated too.
- **Verify:** `appearance.rs` is now one of the sources of `every_string_the_screens_translate_has_a_row_in_the_catalog`; the role names, which reach the translator as a variable the scan cannot read, have their own test (`every_role_has_one_name_and_the_name_is_a_message_of_the_catalog`). The generator refused a row without eleven cells or with a lost placeholder.
- **Not validated:** the translations were written by the model and read by nobody who speaks ten of the eleven languages; no window was opened in a language other than English.

### E-015 — P3 — Theme editor: a colour needed a second button, results were said off the screen, a saved theme could not be removed — FIXED, VERIFIED by test (desktop, synthetic)

- **Discover (read in source and in the demo's picture):** a typed colour did nothing until "Apply colour to preview" was pressed; the line that said what an action came to was the last of a panel longer than the window; the two base buttons and the element buttons did not show which was in use or what colour an element had; "Cancel preview" put the interface back but left the edited draft in the fields; a theme of the library could be loaded and replaced but never removed; a text in the colour of its own surface was accepted without a word; the colour parser took a sign for a digit (`+1` is a number to the integer parser). The three ways a palette arrives (library, pasted text, file) each repeated the same five assignments.
- **Fix:**
  - A valid colour is applied as it is typed; an invalid one shows the rule under the field. The apply button is gone.
  - The result of an action is said under the title. A warning names the first text that cannot be read on what it is drawn on (contrast below 4.5, five pairs that are always drawn together); it warns and does not refuse.
  - Each element's button carries a square of its colour; the base in use is the filled button.
  - "Discard changes" puts the draft back to the saved palette as well as the interface.
  - A saved theme has a "Remove" button, asked for twice. New optional field `forget_theme` of the settings patch (`peersus-core`); the palette in use is untouched, and a name that is not there is not an error.
  - One path for every palette that arrives (`Work::adopt`) and one for every edit (`Work::change`).
- **Compatibility:** a window newer than the owner it talks to gets the removal refused whole (the owner denies fields it does not know) and says "The theme could not be removed."; no other patch carries the field. Preferences files are unchanged in form.
- **Verify:** `forgetting_a_theme_removes_only_that_name_and_keeps_the_palette_in_use` (real store, reopened); `a_colour_typed_in_the_theme_editor_is_applied_at_once_and_an_unreadable_one_is_named` drives the real component tree with no display (type, warning, reset, discard); unit tests for the parser, the role names and the contrast rule on both built-in palettes. Before and after pictures of the panel were compared by eye.
- **Not validated:** the library rows (load, remove, confirm) were never drawn: the demo has no saved theme and refuses every command, so pressing them on a real owner is for Phase 5. The file dialogs, the clipboard, a phone, and whether the layout is pleasant to a person are not validated either.

### E-016 — P3 — Duplicated rules (A-14) — FIXED where the rule was one rule; one left to the owner

- **Fix:** the application's import of a service is found or made in one function (`import_of`), which also removed three `expect`s; a stored API key is read back through the same type that accepts it when it is saved (`ExtensionSecret`) instead of a copy of its rule; the data handshake's time limit is the control handshake's value and no longer a second number; the alphabet of an identifier is one function (`peersus_api::name::plain`) used by the four checks that share it, each of which keeps its own further conditions (length, leading dot, `.`/`..`). The multipart field check in `file_http.rs` has a different alphabet and is left alone.
- **Left, see "Decisions for the owner":** the element-tree limit.
- **Verify:** the full gate below; no behavior was meant to change, and none of the existing tests of these checks changed.

### E-017 — Both phones, development build after E-001 … E-016 — OBSERVED (what is listed, and no more)

- **Build and install:** `./dev.sh android` built (exit 0); `./dev.sh deploy nx rog` installed the same APK on both phones and verified it byte for byte; nothing was uninstalled or cleared. Each phone had three extensions installed and running before and after.
- **Start:** both open on the Send screen; the process is the same one through everything below (no crash, no restart). Each phone lists the other, and shows it asleep when its screen is off and online when it is on.
- **E-008 (Peers at rest), CPU time of the application's process, kernel ticks of 10 ms:**

| Phone | Peers, untouched, 10–12 s | Send, untouched, 10–12 s |
| --- | --- | --- |
| NX | 16 | 35–49 |
| ROG | 3–31 | 24–47 |

  One first reading on ROG's Send screen was 623 ticks in 12 s, about 45 s after the application was opened; two readings after it were 24 and 47. Seen once, not reproduced, cause unknown.
- **E-008 with a code on show (NX), which the demo could not make:** the fuse burns (the spark is further round in a second picture taken 10 s after the first) and the sparks are drawn; 724 ticks in 10 s while it does. Pressing Pair again keeps the same code and the fuse where it was. On another screen with the code still alive: 49 ticks.
- **Open, small:** with the code scrolled off the top of the Peers screen the process still used 549 ticks in 10 s: the fuse goes on redrawing where nobody sees it, for as long as the code lives (at most five minutes).
- **E-015 (theme editor) on NX:** an emptied colour field shows the rule under it; a typed colour recolours the whole interface at once, with no button; the warning that the text cannot be read on the page appears under the title; closing Settings puts the saved colours back. **E-014 on ROG**, which is set to Romanian: the editor is in Romanian.
- **Found on the phones, fixed in E-018:** places of the bottom bar broken over two lines in Romanian with five places (the fifth is an extension's); the fourth theme button squeezed to one letter a line, or pushed off the screen; the last accent colour cut off; "Bază luminoasă" and "Discard changes" broken letter by letter.
- **Found, open:** on a phone the column of section pictures at the right edge of Settings lies over the right end of the content.
- **Not done on a phone:** saving, loading and removing a theme of the library; installing or removing an extension; permissions switched repeatedly; a restart with the phone locked; an extension that floods or dies. The extension stress cases of the test strategy remain to be run on devices.

### E-018 — P2 — Texts broke inside a word, or left the screen, where the window was narrow or the language long — FIXED, VERIFIED by test in twelve languages; phones re-checked below

- **Discover:** on the phones (E-017), and then by laying every screen out with no display at 300, 360 and 412 logical pixels across and at the desktop's size, in each of the twelve languages: 89 texts were broken inside a word. A window is as wide as the screen divided by the size chosen for the interface, so a larger interface is a narrower window: every such break is also a "scale" defect.
- **Cause:** nothing said what a text does when its box is narrower than it would like. A row of buttons gave the last one what was left, and the toolkit then broke its word to fit; a place of the bottom bar is a fixed share of the width, whatever its name is in the language in use.
- **Fix, one rule for each kind of text, in one module (`peersus-ui/src/text.rs`):** running text wraps at its spaces (unchanged); the caption of a control is one line, never broken (`text::caption`), and controls side by side are in a row that wraps, so the one that does not fit goes under the others whole; a text in a box of a fixed share of the window is made as large as fits, down to a floor, and only then cut with an ellipsis (`text::Fitted`, sized by `view::Fit`, the rule the screen titles already had, now general and tested on its own). All sizes are logical pixels, so resizing, a small phone and a larger interface are one case.
- **Applied to:** the places of the bottom bar; Pair and Connect; the theme buttons; the accent colours; the theme editor's rows; the name-and-value rows of the Settings overview, whose value (a name a person typed) is now cut at a share of the row instead of squeezing the word that says what it is.
- **Guard:** `no_text_is_broken_inside_a_word_at_any_size_in_any_language` reads the lines the toolkit actually laid out for every visible text of every screen and Settings section, at those four sizes, in twelve languages, and fails on a line that ends inside a word. It found the 89, then 11 after the first fixes, then none. The demo takes `--demo-language=CODE` so that a screen can be opened and drawn in any language.
- **What the guard does not see:** a text or control pushed outside the window or under another. A first check for that (any visible text reaching past the window's edge) reported 47 texts in its one run, of four kinds: the names in the moving strip of languages, the transfer filters (a row that pans), and the placeholders of the theme exchange field, the new-relay field and the Settings search field, which are longer than their field in some languages. They were sorted by recognising the texts, not by examining each node; the only one of them looked at on a device is the new-relay placeholder, seen earlier on NX in English cut at the edge of its field and not drawn past it. The check could not tell those from a real overflow, so it was removed and nothing guards against one. Also not seen: an extension's own pages, which the demo has none of, and widths under 300.

### E-019 — P1 — What controlling a device costs while it is being controlled — TWO CAUSES FIXED AND MEASURED; the rest measured and open

Measured while a pointer was being moved and not at rest: the remote page of NX (Android, optimized Rust in a debuggable package) moving a pointer of its own on the workstation (the person's running desktop instance: a **development build**, Wayland, GNOME), driven by `adb shell input swipe` on the page's pad, eight strokes of one second. CPU time is read from `/proc` in ticks of 10 ms; on NX a sampling profiler (`simpleperf`, 1000 samples a second, with call chains named from the unstripped library) says where it goes. Figures of the same run are compared with each other only.

- **The controlled computer (development build):** at rest the owner used 17–22 ticks in 8.5 s; while controlled 128–143, the control extension's process 22–24, Xwayland 17–20, the window 21–23 (unchanged). About 59 batches of input arrive a second, so the owner spends about 2.5 ms of CPU on each. It is not held up: a batch is carried from the extension to the platform call in about 0.6 ms and the platform call takes about 0.15 ms.
- **What that cost is not:** the eleven development trace lines of a batch cost about 105 µs of it (measured apart; none in an optimized build); naming the pointer after its device costs 21 µs for each paired device (1.2 µs optimized).
- **Cause 1, development builds only: the encrypted transport and the runtime under it were compiled unoptimized.** A probe added to the tests (`what_a_stream_of_small_messages_to_a_plugin_costs`, run on purpose: 300 messages of 48 bytes at 60 a second to an extension, both devices in one process) costs 866–933 µs of CPU a message in the development profile and 200–266 µs in the release profile. **Fix:** those libraries (never this project's code) are optimized in the development profile, as the interpreter and the toolkit already were. The same probe after: 333–366 µs. *Not measured on the real path:* the running instance is the older build; it has to be built again and restarted, and the ticks above taken again.
- **Cause 2, every build: a window made a new connection to its owner for every command**, and every sample of a finger on an extension's page is a command: a socket made, connected, checked for its owner, taken by the owner on a new task, and closed, some sixty to a hundred and twenty times a second. On NX this was 157–163 M cycles of 1969–2042 M in a nine second drag on the window's side alone, 78–84 M of them in `connect`. **Fix:** a command may ask the owner to keep its connection (`keep`), and the window then sends the next one on it. An owner that does not know the word turns the request away unread and is asked the older way, as with `quiet`; a ninth kept connection is turned away the same way; a kept connection takes no place from a command line client; the owner stopping does not wait for one. A connection the owner has closed is found before anything is written on it, so a window whose owner was replaced asks the new one, once.
- **After, same drag, same phone, two runs:** 1765–1822 M cycles where there were 1969–2042 M (about a tenth less); the window's side of the link 70–87 M where there were 157–163 M; no `connect` at all; the thread that carries the window's commands 138–159 M where there were 250–256 M. In ticks: the application 284–294 in 8.5 s where there were 324–332. Guards: four tests in `tests/runtime.rs` (one connection for forty commands and for a refusal among them; the older way against an owner that keeps none, each command once; the bound and the command line's place; an owner replaced under a window).
- **How even the pointer moves, after:** one unbroken stroke of eight seconds, three times: 56–60 batches a second reach the workstation; between two of them 17.1 ms at the median, 20.4–20.7 ms for nine in ten, 29–38 ms for ninety-nine in a hundred; in two of the three strokes two to four gaps of 80–130 ms. No like-for-like figure from before exists (the earlier strokes were one second long, and the pause between two of them looks the same in the log as a stall), so nothing is claimed about a change.
- **Where the rest goes on the phone (after, of about 1800 M):** the Java main thread that takes the touch and hands it to the native side, 630–640 M (in a debuggable package, with JNI checking on: how much of it a release package keeps was not measured); the owner's worker threads 660–690 M, spread over the transport (150–180 M), the frames to and from the extension's worker (115 M), the window's requests (72–78 M), the broker (43–72 M) and waiting; the interface thread 250 M, most of it taking each event through the window toolkit (finding what the finger is over, 44 M; rendering the page's components again, 23–45 M) and little of it drawing (20–24 M). The extension's own interpreter process is not in the profile; by ticks it used 64–69 in 8.5 s. No single remaining cause was found.
- **Not measured:** the shared pointer against a pointer of its own on the workstation (the setting is the controlled computer's; trying it moves the person's real pointer, so it was not done unasked); the compositor's share (GNOME Shell used 540–740 ticks in 8.5 s with or without a drag in these sessions, for a reason not looked for, and 172 against 231 in the first one); ROG as the controlling phone; a release package; the native build of the extension against the WASM one.
- **Found beside it and fixed:** a watch for changes was answered only when the owner's count of states passed the window's. A window that outlived its owner (the count starts again) saw changes up to ten seconds late, and an owner stopping waited that long for it. It is now answered when the count differs, and when the owner stops.
- **A mistake in the measuring, said as it happened:** after the phone's application was restarted for the new build, its remote page pointed at ROG again and not at the workstation. Two drags (sixteen strokes, pointer moves on the pad) went to ROG, which was dozing with its screen off; what it did with them was not observed. The first profile of a drag is therefore of NX controlling ROG; it agrees with the later one of NX controlling the workstation to within a few percent. The target is now read from the screen before every drag.

### E-020 — P1 — An idle phone spends most of its CPU resolving names — MEASURED on NX; cause not established; OPEN

- **Discover:** sixty seconds of the application at rest on NX, profiled: 3570 M cycles, of which 2119 M (59 %) under the transport library's own name resolver, called by its HTTPS client. Three bursts of about three seconds, about twenty-one seconds apart, each some 600–700 M cycles; between them the whole application uses about 20 M a second. One such burst fell in a nine second drag and was 436 M of its 2478 M.
- **What it is, as far as the profile and the library's source show; not established by experiment:** three quarters of it is the library's HTTPS client finding an address before it connects, and a sixth is lookups of TXT records, which is how the library finds a device's addresses by name. The frames of the library's periodic report on the network (which probes relays over HTTPS and builds a client for each probe) are in an eighth of the samples; for the rest the caller is not in the call chain, so which of the library's uses of that client it is, is not known. The resolver races the name servers with a new socket for each attempt (most of the cost is `socket`, `connect`, `sendto` and `close`, each of which Android also passes through its network daemon), and it does not remember an answer that says "none" unless it is told to, nor a lookup that failed. Which lookups fail on this network, and why, was not found: the application writes no log of it.
- **Next step, not taken:** read what the resolver is asked and what it is answered on the phone (its debug lines), then either give the endpoint a resolver that remembers negative answers, or probe fewer relays, or both, and measure the same sixty seconds again. This is the transport's configuration (`peersus-net/src/transport.rs`); nothing was changed.

### E-021 — P2 — Two polls on Android attach a thread to the Java machine for every look — MEASURED; cause read in source; OPEN

- **Discover:** the count of unnamed threads the Java machine has seen grows by three to six a second for as long as the application runs (14 465 after 44 minutes). In the profile, attaching and detaching is 3.6 % of the application's CPU at rest.
- **Cause:** the usable area of the screen is asked every 250 ms and the dark mode every 500 ms, each from a thread of its own, and each call attaches the thread, asks, and detaches it (`bridge.rs`); the Java machine makes a thread object each time.
- **Fix, not made:** these threads live as long as the window, so they can stay attached (`attach_current_thread_permanently`, which the binding's source shows attaches once and reuses that). It changes the lifecycle of threads against the Java machine, which is to be read in the binding and tried on both phones before it is installed, and it was outside what was being measured.

### E-022 — P3 — The development desktop's owner writes its trace to one file without end — OBSERVED; OPEN

- The running development instance's output goes to `owner.log`, which was 113 MB and 653 000 lines, and grows by about 100 KB for each second a device controls the computer (eleven trace lines a batch). Nothing rotates or bounds it. Development builds only: the lines are compiled out of an optimized build. Not changed.

### E-023 — P1 — Where the cost of controlling goes on both devices, a device's own pointer included — MEASURED; no single cause; nothing fixed; OPEN

Asked for by the owner: the cost while a device is controlled, with the controlled computer giving the device a pointer of its own, which is drawn by a layer the shared pointer does not have. Same devices as E-019 (NX controlling the workstation's running development instance, which is the build from before E-019's change of the development profile; GNOME on Wayland). CPU time in ticks of 10 ms over 8.5 s, read from `/proc`; 850 ticks would be one core in use throughout. Each row was measured two or three times, with the desktop at rest measured between them.

- **The controlled computer, real strokes from the phone (nine fast strokes of one second across the pad), against rest:** the owner 104–105 where it uses 6–8; the extension's interpreter process 19–20 (0 at rest); the compositor 226–238 where it uses 172–179; the display server for X clients 14–16 (1 at rest); the application's window 7–9, unchanged. Together about 185 ticks more than at rest: a little over a fifth of one core. One slow stroke of nine seconds: the owner 99–101, the interpreter 21–22, the compositor 15–20 more, the X server 2–3.
- **The layer by itself** (`what_a_moving_pointer_costs_this_desktop`, an ignored test that moves a pointer in a circle of 450 pixels, sixty batches a second, without a phone or an owner): the input device alone (a pen tablet, nothing drawn) leaves the compositor where it is at rest (186–212 against 184–211) and the X server at 0–1. The drawn pointer alone, with no input device: compositor 224–247, X server 16–19. Both, as the service makes them: 236–243 and 17. So the drawn pointer is what costs, about 40–60 ticks in the compositor and 17 in the X server: 7–9 % of one core while the pointer moves, a third of what controlling costs this computer. It is a small window that is moved, which the compositor has to compose again at each move; the shared pointer is not a window.
- **What does not change it, each tried:** the name tag beside the pointer (236–243 with it, 236–241 without); four moves in a batch instead of one (235–244); raising the window above the others only when it is shown and not at every move (230–234 against 236–243, a difference inside the spread; the trial was removed again).
- **Not measured:** the same circle with the shared pointer (it moves the person's real pointer). That the shared pointer costs the compositor little is expected and not shown here.
- **The controlling phone, same strokes, over a window of about 29 s that contains them** (the phone's counters were read slowly, so these are totals and not rates): the application 326–343 ticks where it uses 104–111 at rest; the extension's interpreter process 86–90 (12–13); the system's compositor 46 (1–2); the system's own server 190–193 (75–85), part of which is `adb` injecting the strokes and would not be there under a finger. The application and its interpreter together: about 300 ticks for nine seconds of strokes, a third of one core.
- **The phone's Java main thread, a third of the application's CPU in E-019 (632 M of 1822 M), is the system's own path for a touch**, read in the profile's call chains: each frame the system's frame callback consumes the batched touch (257 M under it), passes it through the view's input stages to the queue of the native side (a wake of the native loop by a `write`, 30 M), asks for the next frame (a call to another process, 42 M), posts a message to itself (39 M) and tells the system the event is finished (29 M). None of it is the application's code. Checking of calls between Java and native code, which only a debuggable package has, is 12 % of that thread. How much a package that is not debuggable keeps was not measured: making one is a release build, which was not asked for.
- **The development trace is not the cost on the phone:** writing its events is 31–47 M of 1765–1822 M (2–3 %). On the workstation the owner writes eleven lines for each batch (861 KB in 8.5 s of strokes, see E-022); what share of the owner's 104 ticks that is in an unoptimized build was not measured, because the running instance is the person's and was not restarted.
- **What this leaves:** no one place holds the cost. A batch of moves passes, sixty times a second, through the system's touch path, the interface, the owner, the broker, the extension in its interpreter (about 1.5 ms of CPU for each batch on the phone, 0.4 ms on the workstation), the transport, and the same on the other side, and then the drawn pointer. Fewer batches a second would lower every one of them and make the pointer less even; a shorter path for moves (not text through the interpreter for each batch) is a change of the extension contract; drawing the pointer by other means than a window is a change of how a device's own pointer is made. Each is a decision, listed under "Decisions for the owner"; none was taken.

### E-025 — P2 — Hosted automation: one scheduled update could never succeed, four workflows kept the checkout's token, and the published gate has never finished a run — FIXED locally (two); OPEN (one)

Read from the repository host's own record of workflow runs (the last sixty) and from the published branches, on 2026-11-11. Nothing was pushed, dispatched or merged.

- **A scheduled dependency update that fails every week.** `.github/dependabot.yml` asked for Cargo updates of a directory on the `extension` branch. The published `extension` branch holds nine files and no Cargo manifest (its tree was listed); extension sources live on `submission/*` branches. The host's record shows the run for that directory failed on 2026-11-09, while the three other entries succeeded. **Fix:** the entry is removed; the branch's own workflows keep their update entry, which works (three proposals for it exist). Not verified on the host: it reads the file from the default branch, so the change counts only once it is published.
- **Four workflows left the checkout's token in the working copy** (`ci.yml`, `artifacts.yml`, `docs.yml`, `pages.yml`) while they build and run the repository's code; the other four already did not. The token can only read, so this is a narrowing and not a hole closed. **Fix:** `persist-credentials: false` on each checkout; no step of these workflows uses git with credentials (read in each file). The three short jobs also get a time limit (10, 10 and 30 minutes) and the website job a named runner image instead of the moving one. All eight files and the update configuration parse after the change (a YAML parser in a throwaway environment); no workflow was run.
- **Not bounded on purpose:** the two jobs that build Skia from source (`ci.yml` `linux`, `artifacts.yml`) keep the host's default limit of six hours. No run of either has ever finished on a hosted runner, so there is no duration to bound them by; a guessed limit could stop a valid run.
- **OPEN: the published gate has never been seen green on a hosted runner.** "Quality gates" appears seven times in the record: three failures within seconds, on 2026-11-06 and 2026-11-07, and four runs skipped, as designed, because no maintainer asked for them. The three failures started no step in either job (read from each run), and the host's own note on the job says it was not started for a reason of the account, not of the workflow; a scheduled audit run failed the same way on the same days and succeeded on 2026-11-09. So the claim "the gate passes" rests on this workstation's container only. Starting a run spends runner minutes and tests the published commit, not the uncommitted work recorded here; it is the owner's to start.
- **Seen and left alone:** five dependency proposals are open as branches on the host (three single Cargo updates, one grouped Cargo update, one Gradle update). None was evaluated here.

### E-026 — Desktop release artifacts for Linux — BUILT and CHECKED as files; NOT RUN as an application

No optimized desktop artifact existed before this (the release directory was empty). Built once from the tree the gate above passed, on 2026-11-11.

- **Build:** `./build.sh desktop` in the build container: exit 0, 316 s (the renderer was already built from source for this profile). Five programs: the command line (36 MiB), the interface (44 MiB), the interpreter host (3 MiB), the store fetcher (7 MiB), the native helper (21 KiB).
- **Checked:** the command line prints its version (`0.1.0`) and its help, exit 0. Each program is position-independent with read-only relocations bound at start (`readelf`). `scripts/stage-linux.py` staged ten files (the five programs with mode 755, the desktop entry, the metadata, the icon, licence and notice); the staged desktop entry passes `desktop-file-validate` and the staged metadata passes `appstreamcli validate` (one pedantic note); the staged command line prints the same version.
- **Not done, and why:** the optimized interface and owner were not started. Started beside the person's running instance they would either attach to it or become a second owner with the device's real identity (the system keyring is not separated by a private data directory); neither is a test to make unasked. So nothing here shows the optimized build's behavior, start time or CPU: every measurement in this log is of a development build on the desktop.
- **Not built:** the Android release packages (`./build.sh android`, unsigned), the Flatpak bundle and the AppImage. An unsigned Android package cannot be installed over the installed development one without a signer, and no signer for a release exists here; the two Linux bundles were last built on 2026-11-04 (see [release readiness](release-readiness.md)) and not since. The programs are not stripped; what a distribution strips is its packaging's choice.

### E-024 — P1 — Windows: two interface link tests fail every time — OBSERVED; cause not investigated; OPEN

- **Where:** a Windows virtual machine on the workstation, idle, tests run on one thread, three runs.
- **Passed there:** the library tests of the contract crate (69), the core (113) and the plugin host (94); `cargo check -p peersus-app -p peersus-net --all-targets` exit 0; the interface's library tests 626 passed.
- **Failed, 3 of 3 runs, about 41 s each:** `one_desktop_code_delivers_eighty_paths_after_its_window_observers_are_dropped` (panics at `link.rs:3003`) and `one_native_code_precedes_descriptors_and_survives_gaps_between_eighty_choices` (panics at `link.rs:3058`). The machine was idle and the result did not vary, so load is not the cause.
- **Run later, with the sources of 2026-11-11:** the owner's integration tests (`cargo test -p peersus-app --test runtime`, one thread): 9 passed, none failed, in 26 s. They pair two owners, send files and a folder over real connections and hand files over by a code, in one process.
- **Not known:** whether the application is wrong on Windows or the two failing tests assume Unix behavior. Nothing was changed. No Windows program was started as an application: Windows remains unvalidated.

### E-027 — P1 — Android: in deep sleep the owner gets no processor time, foreground service or not — MEASURED on NX; cause is the system's; OPEN

- **Measured:** NX, screen off, the local control socket asked for a snapshot at intervals. At 60 s it answered in 0.2 s (the system reported its idle state as pending). From 129 s on, with the system in its deep idle state, every request up to 1113 s was accepted and never answered (0 bytes), and the process's processor time did not advance at all between probes (8849 and 9453 ticks, unchanged). After the phone was woken a request was answered in 0.1 s.
- **What this means:** the foreground service keeps the process alive but not running. While the phone sleeps deeply the application answers nothing: not the local socket, and, as far as this shows, no device. Whether a paired device's connection wakes it was not tested.
- **Consequence seen:** `scripts/deploy-plugin.py` fails with "the app did not answer" on a sleeping phone; wake it first.
- **Not decided:** whether to ask the person for an exemption from battery optimization, to hold a wake lock while a transfer runs, or to say plainly that a sleeping phone is unreachable. Each is the owner's decision; nothing was changed.

### E-028 — An extension that misbehaves on purpose, on one phone — OBSERVED on NX; the application stayed answering; rate limits still absent (A-04 OPEN)

The test extension (`test` 0.1.1, installed from the store, see E-029) has six buttons that each do one wrong thing. They were pressed through the local control socket (`page-event`) on NX, awake, application in front, development build with the changes up to E-030. Between presses the application's state was asked about once a second: about 190 requests in all.

- **The application:** the same process throughout; every request answered, the slowest in 0.28 s; the three other extensions running at every look; two of three paired devices online at every look.
- **Flood** (one press sends 2000 calls to a service nobody offers and 2000 events): the press is accepted and an error is listed for the extension ("could not complete the action: what it needs is not available"); the page never showed the press counted. Six presses within half a second: two were refused ("The plugin is not running"), the extension was seen restarting and was running again 3 s later. **Not established:** whether the operations were refused one by one or the extension's connection was closed, and by which limit. No temporary suspension that is about flooding was seen; what stopped it was the same handling as a crash. A-04 stays open.
- **Nonsense** (bytes that are no message, an unknown operation, an answer to a ticket nobody gave, an answer whose content is not a page): nothing changed on the page, the extension kept running and kept its state (its counter went on from where it was).
- **Never answer:** after 5.9 s the error "did not complete the action in time" was listed; the extension kept running and kept its state.
- **Spin** (a loop that never returns) and **fill memory** (a megabyte at a time without end): the worker process was replaced by a new one (seen 1.3 s after the spin; by the first look, 25 s, after the fill) and the extension answered again with its state reset. Which limit ended each was not read.
- **Leave** (the guest returns an error): restarted. Pressed twelve times, one every 2.2 s: it was restarted for the first five, then marked failed with "A plugin stopped working and was turned off", and stayed off for the 45 s it was watched. `restart-plugin` brought it back within 4 s and its page answered.
- **Seen and not changed:** after a restart caused by a crash, the page's last content stays in the state until the next event, so a counter of the former run is shown. The list of errors had 11 entries at the end; whether it has a bound was not read.
- **Not done:** a transfer, a clipboard text or a controlled pointer passing between devices while an extension misbehaved (the devices were listed as online, nothing was sent between them); the same on ROG and on the desktop; the phone locked or the application in the background; an older extension on a newer application and the reverse; permissions switched on and off repeatedly.

### E-029 — Store and automatic updates of the application and of extensions — BUILT; OBSERVED end to end on Linux and on one phone; Play path NOT observable

Asked for by the owner: the application updates itself from a signed release, with one switch for "update automatically" and one for "restart after an update", and extensions update from the store. Design and limits: [application updates](app-update.md). What was published is development-only, in the private repository, signed with development keys whose seeds are local, ignored files; no product build trusts either key.

- **Published for the test:** release `app/test` (the signed description, its signature, an archive of the five Linux programs, the Android package: build 2 of version 0.1.0); the test extension's source on the `extension` branch; releases `extension/test/v0.1.0` and `v0.1.1` (four platform archives, approval, signature, review each).
- **Linux, a development instance on build 1:** a check found build 2 with the publisher's notes. "Update now": downloading after 1.0 s, installing after 5.3 s, in place after 7.3 s; all five programs on disk had the published digests and the running programs were untouched. Turning "Restart after an update" on: restarting after 1.1 s, the owner answering again after 6.1 s, "up to date" on build 2 after 8.4 s. The images of both the owner and the window were then the published ones (digest of the running programs), the extension hosts had restarted, two of three paired devices were online.
- **Android (NX, Android 16, Stellar in shell mode, already allowed), build 1, both switches turned on:** downloading after 1.8 s, installing after 9.8 s (96 MiB), then the package was replaced by the shell worker (`versionCode` 2 to 3), the window was in front again and the application named itself build 2, 11.4 s after the switches. The folder the package waited in was gone after the restart.
- **Before that, by hand as the shell user on NX:** the same command installs a package streamed on its input and opens the window again; a package of another name is refused (`INSTALL_FAILED_INVALID_APK`).
- **Extensions:** with the new development catalog key both devices listed the test extension from its signed approval. On NX it was installed from the store (running 2 s after the command). With its automatic-update switch on, after 0.1.1 was published both devices installed and started 0.1.1 by themselves at their next refresh (the store is asked again at most every five minutes).
- **Found and fixed:**
  - On Android every start of the application failed its first look at the store and at updates ("could not be reached"), with a message over whatever the person was doing, and the store then waited out its retry delay. Cause, read in the platform's download worker: it carries one download at a time (a second is refused at once) and is ended by whichever caller finishes first; the update check added here shares that transport and starts together with the store's own refresh. Downloads from the application now take turns (`peersus-ui-android/src/store.rs`). Introduced by this work; the desktop was never affected, each download there has a helper process of its own.
  - On the desktop the test extension had been switched off by the person; after the automatic update it was enabled and running. The switch is the person's and not the old package's: a replacement now leaves a switched-off extension off, in the snapshot and in its new folder, and one that cannot be installed puts the old one back switched off. Test: `an_update_of_an_extension_the_person_switched_off_leaves_it_off`; the plugin, store and update integration tests pass (65, 12, 7). An extension the person removes and installs again is new and runs, as before.
- **Found, not changed:**
  - With no private catalog configured the same situation reads differently: the desktop says "up to date" (the listing is simply not found), the phone says it could not reach the store.
  - A submission that ships only a WebAssembly guest (the `control` submission) has its manifest under `wasm/` and none at `extension/<name>/plugin.json`, which is the only place discovery looks: such an extension would never be listed. The test extension was published with a copy of its manifest at the top to be found.
  - An archive of the unoptimized desktop programs with their symbols is 378 MiB, over the transport's 128 MiB; the published one holds stripped copies (50 MiB).
  - This workstation refuses a push of commits not signed by the owner, so the two commits on the `extension` branch were made through the host's interface with the owner's account, at the owner's word.
- **Not observed:** the Play distribution (it compiles; Play's question needs a listing); Windows and macOS (no installer exists); the refusals on Android when no provider runs or it has not allowed the application; a rollback attempt against a real release (covered by tests only); the automatic check on its six-hour clock (every check here was asked for or made at start).

### E-030 — P2 — An extension card of the store squeezed its last control and broke its text inside words — FIXED, VERIFIED by test and seen on one phone

- **Reported by the owner** from a phone: on the card of an extension installed from the store, "Automatic updates: off" was drawn one syllable a line.
- **Why E-018 missed it:** the fixture every screen is laid out from had no extension and no store listing, so no extension card was ever laid out. With two installed extensions and three listings added to the fixture, the existing layout test failed on 46 texts, at every phone width and at the desktop width in ten languages.
- **Cause:** the row of a card's controls (up to five for a store extension) was a plain row inside a footer of a fixed height.
- **A first fix that was wrong, and what it showed:** making the row wrap and its texts one-line captions made the test pass, and on the phone the last control was then an empty box. The toolkit's wrapping row moves a control down only when the width it measured for it at first is more than what is left, and a text is measured squeezed into what is left, so it is cut instead of moved. **The layout test cannot see this:** it reports a word broken over two lines, and a one-line caption is never broken, only cut. Every other row that relies on wrapping one-line captions (the theme choices, among others) passes that test whether or not it is cut at the narrowest widths; none was looked at here. OPEN: a check for captions that are cut.
- **Fix:** no row of the card holds more than two controls with words. The switch stands beside the status word; Open and Remove are a row; approving, the automatic updates (now a switch with its words beside it, like every other yes or no in Settings) and a waiting update each have a line of their own, and the footer is as tall as what it holds (a minimum height on it left a blank band under the controls, seen in the drawn picture, and was dropped). The layout test passes at every width in every language, and the card was looked at in the picture the headless window draws. "Approve" had no translation in any language (it was passed as a value, where the catalog check does not look); it has one now.
- **Seen on NX** (the build with the fix, installed and verified byte for byte): the card of the extension installed from the store shows the status word with its switch, Open and Remove, "Automatic updates" with its switch, and Restart, each whole and on its own line. Not looked at on ROG or in the desktop window, and in English only.

### E-031 — Sending and receiving, as asked for by the owner: a code that gives way to a device, the jump to Receive, an offer announced outside the window — BUILT; OBSERVED on Linux and in part on one phone

Six things were asked for. Five are in this entry; the sixth (a device that is brought near) is E-032.

- **A code gives way to a device.** With a files code on show that nobody has scanned, picking a paired device takes the code back at the owner (`withdraw-files-invitation`) and lists the files again for the hold-to-send circle. Tests: the owner refuses to take back a code that was scanned or is not the newest, a code taken back admits nobody and lets go of what waited behind it, and the page's rule gives way only to devices that are picked. **Observed on Linux** (a window of the development build on a virtual X display, driven by synthetic pointer input, attached to the running owner): two files pasted, the circle made a code (`files_code` live at the owner), a device card pressed, `files_code` gone at the owner and the circle reading "HOLD TO SEND 1 DEVICE" with both files listed, the circle held, both files offered at the phone. **Observed on NX** (the build installed and verified byte for byte, driven through `adb`): one file chosen in the system's picker, the code made, the computer's card touched, the code gone at the owner and the circle reading "HOLD TO SEND 1 DEVICE" with the file listed again, the circle held, the file offered at the computer (whose panel came up). **Limit:** a selection of more than 64 files on Android cannot be listed a second time, and its code stays. Not looked at on ROG.
- **Accepting goes to Receive.** Accepting an offer takes the window to Receive and scrolls to the list of what is being received. Test: the rule fires for a file that waited and now moves or has arrived, and for nothing else. **Observed on Linux** (same window; one file, then five at once) and **on NX** (twice; Accept, the system's folder choice, back in the application): the page was Receive with the list in view. The list is a bounded one at the end of the page, so the page ends with the list whole on the screen and its first line is not at the very top; that is as far as the page goes.
  Seen on NX on the way, OPEN (P3), not caused by this: a touch sent through `adb` at the middle of the Send tab (31 dp from the left edge, at the bottom) did nothing, eight times in eight, while one 37 dp from the edge opened the tab every time and every other tab opened wherever it was touched. Whether a finger meets the same is not known; the system's own edge gesture is the first suspect.
- **Android, an offer outside the application.** The panel drawn over other applications opens by itself where the person allowed drawing over them, at the top or the bottom as chosen, and the notification is then a quiet one that cannot cover it; without that permission, or with the screen locked, the notification with its answers is the announcement, with more room than before. **Observed on NX:** the notification when the permission is off, the panel alone when it is on, both edges, and ticks that stay where they were put. Not seen: a locked screen, the row in Settings, ROG.
- **Linux, an offer outside the window.** There was nothing. Now the owner starts a small panel (the window program with `--offer-panel`) while an offer waits and no window is in front, and stops it otherwise; see [local control](api/local-control.md#an-offer-announced-outside-the-window). A window says whether it is in front with one byte on its lease. Tests: the count of windows in front, the byte carried over the socket, a surface beside the window never counted as a window, the panel's place on a screen. **Observed on this workstation** (a Wayland session, the panel through its X server): the panel came up within a moment of an offer with the window not in front, at the bottom centre; it moved to the top, the left and the right as the edge was changed in the settings while it was up; it grew for a second file and stayed at its edge; it went when the offer ran out, and when the offer was answered. On a virtual X display, with synthetic pointer input, against the same owner: View files opened the list and the panel grew upwards from the bottom edge; Accept (with a folder saved) received both files and Cancel refused one; with a window that had the focus on that display, an offer started no panel.
  **Asked for by the owner on seeing it: round corners, and that it can be moved.** Both built. Seen on the real screen: the round corners. Seen on the virtual display: held and moved in one step it followed exactly (100 by 60 pixels asked, 100 by 60 moved, and one more pixel for one more); in twenty quick steps it ended one step short (the window is moved by what the pointer says relative to it, and a step that arrives before the last move is known is not made up for once the pointer rests); let go, it stayed where it was put and its Cancel still worked. A real hand's steps are a pixel or two; not tried with one.
  **Not observed:** a press on the panel on the real screen (synthetic input does not reach an X window under this compositor); Accept when no folder is saved, which opens the system's folder dialog from a window that has no focus; a session with no X display (the refusal is a test only); two screens (it always stands on the primary one); the window coming to the front while the panel is up. **Not implemented:** Windows and macOS announce nothing outside the window.
- **Where the announcement stands.** One preference, `notice_place`: top or bottom on a phone, and the sides as well on a desktop that has the panel. The row is in Settings only where there is a panel to place.
- **Found on the way (P2), there before this work — FIXED, VERIFIED by test and seen on the devices.** When the person at the receiving device pressed Cancel on an offer, the sender recorded "network operation failed"; and when the sender cancelled an offer nobody had answered, the receiver went on showing it until its time ran out. Seen again on NX before the fix (the computer's Cancel, the phone's record: failed, network operation failed).
  **Cause:** Cancel stops the task that holds the transfer, which lets go of its two streams with no word. A stream that is let go of is not reset: its writing half is ended cleanly and its reading half asks the other device to stop sending. The sender, waiting for an answer, read a stream that ended with no message and called it the network; the receiver, waiting for its person, was not looking at the stream at all.
  **Fix** (`peersus-net`, nothing added to what is sent): a message awaited on a stream that ends or is reset while the connection stands is a new error, "the other device closed this exchange", apart from a failure of the connection; while an offer waits it is a refusal, so the record reads cancelled. The receiver, while it waits for its person, also waits for the sender to stop listening, and ends the offer at once when it does. Once a file is moving, either side letting go is "the other device stopped the transfer" at the other (it was "interrupted or timed out"), and a receiver that cannot keep a file it took whole (one of that name in the way, a digest that does not match) says so to the sender, which read the silence as the network.
  **Tests** (two endpoints on this machine): an offer the receiver lets go of with no word is a refusal at the sender; an offer the sender takes back ends at the receiver at once; a moving file let go of by either side is stopped by it at the other, and nothing of it is left in the folder; a file that cannot be kept is said to the sender and what was there stays. Three of the five failed before the code was right, each for a reason the reading of the toolkit's source then explained.
  **Works with a device that has the older build:** the sender's half does (it reads what every build does); the receiver's half needs the new build on the receiving device.
  **Seen on the devices** (this computer and NX, both on the build with the fix): NX offered a file and the computer refused it, and NX's record read cancelled 0.2 s later (it read failed, network operation failed, before); NX offered again and took the offer back, and the computer's offer was gone from what waits by the next look (under 0.3 s) and recorded cancelled on both. Not seen: a file let go of while it moves (the tests only), and ROG.
- **Found by review of the carried panel, fixed.** A press is a pointer that goes down and comes up on the same button, wherever it went in between, and a carried panel goes with the pointer: taking hold of the panel on Accept, moving it and letting go would have accepted the file. The buttons and switches of an offer now ask whether the hold became a carry and do nothing when it did (`Carrying`, provided by the panel only; the bar in the window has none and is as before). The same change made the four pixels of an unsteady hand count for every hold, where after the first carry any movement at all moved the panel. **Seen on the virtual display:** held on Accept and moved 200 by 300 pixels in twenty steps, the panel moved 190 by 285 and the offer still waited; a plain press on Cancel where the panel then stood cancelled it on both devices. The fault itself was not reproduced on the earlier build: it is read from the toolkit's source.
- **Found by the gate.** The headless test that types a colour in the theme editor could not press "Reset selected element": the Settings page is one row taller where a display for the panel exists (the build container has one), and the test's window was as tall as the page had been. The window is taller; nothing of the application changed.

### E-032 — Devices nearby, as asked for by the owner: a paired device brought near is proposed on Send, and devices nearby are listed on Peers to pair with — BUILT, experimental, off by default; the list and the pairing OBSERVED on one phone and this workstation; the proposal on Send NOT observed

What it is, what is announced and what that means for an invitation is in [devices nearby](nearby-send.md). Linux and Android only; one switch, off by default.
