- **Asked for in two steps.** First: with files chosen, a paired device brought near becomes where they go. Then, on reading that: a list of devices nearby on Peers, at the right on a desktop and fitted to a phone, where choosing one begins pairing, so that Pair and Connect are not needed for a device that is in the room. Pair and Connect stay.
- **Built.** Rules as pure functions in `peersus-core::nearby` (the tag, the form of an offer, two rules of nearness); the radio form of an invitation and a gate of its own for what is announced in `peersus-net`; the follower and the BlueZ adapter in `peersus-app`; the Android adapter (`NearbyRadio.java` and its bridge); the switch, the proposal and the list in the interface. An offer to pair is longer than the older kind of Bluetooth announcement carries, so it is sent as the extended kind.
- **Tests.** The rules (a tag names its device for its period and the ones beside it only; near takes an unbroken strong while and gone a longer weak one; an offer that is not exactly one is nothing, a control character in a name included, which the first version let through by cutting the space around the name before looking). The follower with a radio that does what the test says (nothing with the switch off; a stranger listed only while this device looks, never when paired, never more than sixteen; a radio that cannot offer says so and still lists). The BlueZ adapter against a stand-in for BlueZ on a connection with no bus (two advertisements, the offer's with a secondary channel; taking the offer back leaves the tag). The radio form of an invitation (the routes that fit, the relay first; truncation and trailing bytes refused). The gate (announcing never takes away the code on the screen, nor the other way; used once; gone when taken back).
- **Observed, this workstation and NX** (both on the build with this, the phone's installed and verified byte for byte; the workstation's adapter is BlueZ 5.87 with extended advertising):
  - The switch in Settings on the workstation's window, and the system's question for nearby devices on NX when it was first turned on.
  - BlueZ took the offer as a second advertisement beside the tag when Peers was opened in a window in front, and let go of it 9 s after the window left Peers.
  - NX heard the workstation's offers (156 and 164 bytes of data, which the older kind of announcement cannot carry) and another phone's; the workstation heard both phones'.
  - **The list**, on NX under the Pair and Connect buttons and in a window on the workstation as a column at the right, with the names the devices gave and "not paired" under each.
  - **Pairing from the list, both ways.** A row pressed on NX: both devices showed the three numbers within a few seconds; the same number chosen on both: paired and online on both, and gone from both lists. A row pressed in the workstation's window: both showed the numbers; different numbers chosen: refused on both with the words pairing has for that, nobody paired, and both listed again.
  - To have a device that is not paired with the phone, the workstation ran a second owner with an identity made in memory (the build the integration tests use) on a data directory of its own, with a window on a virtual X display; its identity was checked against the paired devices of both real devices before anything was paired, and the pairing made with it was forgotten on the phone afterwards (the phone's paired devices were the same set after as before).
- **Measured on NX** (40 s, phone at rest on the desk, distance to the workstation not measured): a phone's offer was heard every 0.29 s (median); the workstation's every 1.29 s, which is BlueZ's own default, with silences of 2.6 s when one was missed; strengths between −58 and −76 dBm. On the workstation, the phone's tag every 0.3 s (median; nine in ten under 0.85 s; the longest of 88 was 1.5 s) at −76 dBm. From this: the longest silence that still counts as unbroken went from 1.2 s to 2 s, and the workstation now asks BlueZ for an interval of 200 to 250 ms (`MinInterval`, `MaxInterval`). **Measured again with that:** an advertisement registered without an interval was heard at the phone every 1.29 s (15 and 12 times in 20 s), one with it every 0.26 s (69 times in 20 s, the longest silence 0.76 s), and the workstation's own offer every 0.26 s (74 times in 24 s). BlueZ 5.87 takes the interval.
- **Found while measuring, on NX — FIXED in the adapter, and the fix was observed.** The phone listened with one scan for as long as its window was in front. Twenty-six minutes after the application was started the same scan heard far less: of an advertisement sent every 1.29 s, 4 and 3 in 24 s where a new scan heard 15 and 12 in 20 s; of one sent every 0.26 s, 9 in 24 s where a new scan heard 69 in 20 s. Three minutes after the start it had heard as a new scan does. The system's own source turns a scan that has run half an hour into one that hears only what other applications' scans hear; that this is what happened here is inferred from the numbers and the age of the scan, and was not read from the phone. The adapter now stops its scan and starts it anew every five minutes. **With that build**, the application in front on NX for 43 minutes and one advertisement sent from the workstation every 0.26 s, counted a minute at a time: 217 a minute on average in minutes 1 to 16 (the fewest 193), and 214 a minute in minutes 25 to 43 (the fewest 130, once; then 205), so nothing fell off at the age where the old scan had. Starting anew every five minutes left no mark in the counts.
- **Seen in that same measurement — P1 for this feature on Linux, cause FOUND, fix in E-035:** from minute 17 to minute 23 the phone heard nothing of the workstation's advertisement (111, then 0 for five minutes, then 180), while it went on hearing other devices' tags at about 150 a minute, down from about 400. Nothing was written by the workstation's Bluetooth service or kernel in those minutes. A first comparison (106 heard in 30 s without the workstation listening, 104 with) was read as clearing the listening; it was worthless, because the command that was to make the workstation listen was sent in a form the owner does not take, and it never listened. The cause is the listening: see E-035.
- **NOT observed.** The proposal on Send: it needs a device held right next to another (−55 dBm or stronger), and nobody was there to move one; the strengths above are of devices lying where they lay. So the thresholds of "brought near" are not calibrated against a hand's distance, only chosen. ROG was not driven (it appeared in both lists, as another person used it). A phone without extended advertising (it would say Bluetooth refused, and still list others). Battery cost. Windows and macOS have no adapter and show no switch.
- **Seen on the way, OPEN (P3), not caused by this:** on NX the Refuse button of a pairing request is squeezed beside the three numbers and its word breaks one syllable a line. It is the card every pairing has; on the workstation it is whole.
- **Said plainly in the document and to be weighed by the owner:** an offer is read by every radio in range, where a code on a screen is read by who sees the screen. While Peers is open with the switch on, anyone in range can begin a pairing with this device (it goes no further unless the person chooses a number), and a device can announce any name. What then stands between a person and pairing with the wrong device is the choice of one number of three. Pairing by code is unchanged and is the stronger way.

### E-033 — P2 — Four preferences were forgotten at every start — FIXED, VERIFIED by test; the failure seen on this workstation

- **Seen** while turning on the switch of E-032: set, written to the preferences file, and gone after the owner was started again.
- **Cause.** On reading the file, keys the program does not know are dropped first, so that a file of an older program is read for what it still says. What the program knows was worked out from what the default settings write, plus a list written by hand of the fields that are not written while they hold their default. Four such fields were not on the list: the switch of E-032, where an offer is announced (`notice_place`, E-031), and the two switches of the application's own update (`update_automatically`, `restart_after_update`, E-029). Each was read as unknown and dropped at every start, and the next save wrote the file without it. So a person who chose the top of the screen, or automatic updates, had the default again after a restart, on every platform.
- **Fix** (`peersus-net/src/settings.rs`): the names are asked of the type itself (what its own reading accepts), so there is no list to fall behind. A type that says nothing drops nothing.
- **Tests.** The names include every field the default writes and every one it does not; the four choices survive closing and opening the file, and a key no program has is still dropped with the rest kept. The existing tests of the file (an older file, a wrong value reset alone, an unusable file put aside) pass unchanged.
- **Not looked for:** whether anyone's saved choice of these was lost before now. It was, for anyone who made one and restarted; nothing can bring it back, and the next choice stays.
- **Seen after the fix.** On this workstation (the owner and its window stopped and started twice on the build with the fix): the switch of E-032 and `notice_place` set to the top were in the settings and in the file after each start; `notice_place` was then put back. On NX: the switch, turned on under the build without the fix, was still on after the build with the fix was installed and the application started (the first installation that day, of the build without it, had started with the switch off).

### E-034 — Three things reported by the owner on 2026-11-11: files that vanished behind a code, a phone held sideways, the menu of the gear — FIXED; what was seen is said for each

- **P2 — With a code drawn, the list of files was gone.** Files were listed on Send, the circle was touched to make a code, and while the code was drawn the page named no file; choosing a device afterwards brought the list back. **Cause:** the names under the circle were drawn from the files that wait, and making a code takes them out of that place. **Fix:** the names of what went behind the code are kept from the moment the circle is touched until the code gives way (`view::names_listed`, and the code on show carries its names); while a code is drawn they are only named, without the controls that remove one, since they are no longer the page's to change. **Test:** `view.rs`, names listed from paths and from an Android selection. **Seen** in the workstation's window on a virtual display: the names stay under the drawn code, without the remove buttons; choosing a device brings the list back with its controls, each file once. **Seen on NX:** a file chosen in the system's picker, the circle pressed, and under the drawn code the file is still named, without a remove button. Choosing a device after that was not done on the phone (it would have sent the file).
- **P2 — A phone held sideways got the desktop's window.** In landscape a phone is as wide as a small desktop window, and the form of the window was chosen by width alone: it showed the desktop's close, minimise and maximise buttons, which do nothing on a phone, and the desktop's navigation. **Fix:** a third form, `view::Form::Side`, for a held device that is wider than tall: no window buttons, the places in a rail at the left edge (76 px) in the order of the bottom bar, Settings as the sheet from the top as in portrait, pages laid out as on a desktop with a phone's margins. Which form applies is `Form::of(width, height, held)`, and `held` is the platform, never a guess from the size. **Tests:** `view.rs`, the form for each shape and platform and what each form shows. **Seen on NX**, turned by the system's setting and turned back after: the logo and the gear in the header and no window buttons; five places in a rail at the left; Send and Peers laid out beside it (Peers with its Nearby column at the right); the gear opens Settings as a sheet from the top. **Seen there and OPEN (P3):** the word of the Connect button is cut ("Conne…") on Peers; right after turning, the header was partly off the top with the page partly scrolled, and both were whole after a pull down; five places just fill the rail's height, and a sixth (one more extension with a place) was not tried. ROG was not turned.
- **P3 — The menu of the gear listed three places, and a right click also went to Settings.** The menu that opens on a right click of the gear named Overview, Extensions and About from a list written by hand beside the real one. **Fix:** the menu is `SettingsTab::ALL`, the same list Settings itself shows, so there is nothing to fall behind; and a release of the right button is no longer a press (`view::presses`), so opening the menu goes nowhere. **Seen** in the workstation's window on a virtual display: seven places in the menu, the right click opens it and navigates nowhere, a left click on a place goes to that place, a left click on the gear goes to Settings.

### E-035 — Sending to a device that is not paired by bringing it near, and how near is near set by the person — BUILT, experimental, behind the switch of E-032; between two phones NOT observed, and put off by the owner

Asked for by the owner in place of scanning a code: bring the other device near and the files go. The owner chose that no pairing is needed, that the files are sent by the bringing near itself, and that the person, not the application, sets how near is near. What it is and what it means for who may be offered a file is in [devices nearby](nearby-send.md).

- **What was built.** A device with the switch on and a window open announces, in the offer of E-032, that it takes files from a device that is not paired (the first byte of the offer is 2; a build before this one says 1 and is only listed to pair with). A second gate is made from each announced invitation (`Invitation::for_files`, `Purpose::NearbyFiles`): a device admitted by it may offer up to sixteen files on that connection and nothing else, and is not paired. On Send, with files listed and no destination chosen, a device that takes files and is brought near after the files were listed is offered them at once (`View::brought_near`); each file is then its person's to accept, as any file is.
- **The owner decides, not the window.** `queue-files` with the destination `nearby` is refused unless the owner itself holds that device as taking files and near, and what it announced is live; a tool on the control socket cannot send to a device that is merely in range. What was announced is used once and kept for another try when the attempt could not start.
- **The transfers are named when the files are taken.** The connection is admitted later than the command is answered, so the owner promises the identifier of each transfer in its answer (`NetworkHost::send_nearby`, `TransferTasks::send_as`) and makes the transfers under those identifiers once admitted; none is made when it is not. The circle on Send follows them as it follows any batch.
- **How near is near.** The owner's instruction: the application does not calibrate, the person does, by where the signal is strongest. Settings has *How near is near* under the switch: *Measure* makes the owner tell the strength of the device heard strongest (`measure-nearby`), *Set near from here* stores a threshold 8 dB under it (`nearby_near_at`, between −85 and −30 dBm), *Reset* returns to the application's −55 dBm. The row shows the threshold in use and, while measuring, what the threshold would become.
- **Measured between two phones, and what it changed.** The owner pointed out that the signal between a phone and the workstation says nothing of two phones, and held NX and ROG; how far apart is the owner's word, nobody measured it. Asked over the control socket (`measure-nearby`, both at once). Held "almost touching, a small distance": NX heard ROG's tag at −63 to −67 dBm (median −64), ROG heard NX at −51 to −59 dBm (median −56); the application's −55 dBm was never reached on NX and was on the edge on ROG. A run twenty minutes later, with ROG asleep and how the phones lay not known to who measured, gave −62 to −66 dBm on NX and nothing from ROG, whose owner did not answer while the phone slept. Then the owner held them "very close" for the purpose, both awake, on the build with the fixes below, three runs of 25 s with 124 to 137 answers each: NX told −49 dBm as the strongest (median; −53 to −49), ROG −48 dBm (−51 to −44); the 49 single readings of NX's own log in the last run were −53 to −49 dBm; and each held the other as near with −55 dBm. **Seen on NX in that position:** a file listed on Send, the circle said *hold to send, 1 device* and ROG was marked *Brought near*; the list was cleared, nothing was sent. So −55 dBm holds between these two phones only held against each other, with about 5 dB to spare, and a small distance more loses it: the person's own setting is what makes it usable farther, which is what the owner asked for. The two directions differ by 1 to 8 dB. ROG's offer was heard on NX about 5 dB weaker than its tag (median −69 against −64).
- **P2 — A strength of 127 counted as the strongest.** ROG told a strength of 127 several times; Android reports that when it has no reading. **Fix:** a strength outside −127 to 20 dBm is not a strength and is ignored (`nearby::STRENGTHS`). **Test:** core, `nearby/tests.rs`.
- **P2 — How near a stranger is was read from its offer.** The offer is the longer announcement and was heard weaker than the tag of the same device, so a device was near as a paired one would be and not near as a stranger. **Fix:** a stranger is near by the strength its tag is heard at; the offer only says that it is around and what it takes. **Test:** core, `nearby/tests.rs`.
- **P1 for this feature — The workstation was not heard while it listened.** **Seen** with a probe on this workstation's adapter (two announcements of a script and BlueZ's own scan, counted at NX; the numbers are in [devices nearby](nearby-send.md)): announcements registered before a discovery starts were heard 35 and 38 times in 8 s before the discovery, not once while it ran, 30 and 32 times after it; two registered after a discovery had started were heard throughout. The application registered its tag and its offer before it began to listen, so a workstation with its window open was silent. **Fix:** when the adapter reports that a discovery runs, both announcements are registered again (`linux.rs`). **Test:** `what_was_announced_before_a_discovery_began_is_given_again_once_it_runs`. Seen on one adapter; whether others behave so is not known, and registering again is harmless where they do not.
- **Seen after that fix:** with a window of a test instance of the workstation in front and listening, NX listed it by its name under Nearby as a device that takes files, and went on listing it; before the fix a listening workstation was not heard at all.
- **P1 for this feature — The sending window froze when the files went.** **Seen** in a window of a test instance of the workstation (a virtual display, an identity of its own, paired with nobody): the files went to the device brought near, and from that moment the window drew nothing and took no click, with its drawing thread at 100 % of a processor until it was stopped. **Cause**, read in the toolkit's source: an effect runs again whenever something it read changes, the hand-over of the listed files runs inside the effect of a device brought near, and the hand-over read the listed names and then emptied them, so the effect called itself for ever. Only one send was made because the second pass found nothing listed. A phone that sent this way would have frozen the same; that was not seen. **Fix:** the hand-over looks at the names without subscribing (`peek`, `lib.rs`). **Seen after it**, same window, same steps: the page says the files were offered to the device brought near, the circle follows the transfer, the places answer, and the thread rests at 0 %. **No test drove it** when this was written: the test with two real owners (`link.rs`) does not go through the page, and no test of the page listed files. One does since E-039, and it fails on this defect.
- **Seen: files to a device that is not paired, from the workstation's test instance to NX.** A file listed in the window; ROG, which had been near all along, was offered nothing; NX, which came back after the file was listed, was offered it within three seconds and showed *Strain-Test wants to send you a file* with Cancel and Accept; accepted on the phone, it arrived whole (300 000 bytes, same SHA-256) and nobody was paired on either side. A second file was offered the same way and cancelled from the sender; both sides recorded it as cancelled. **What this does not show:** nothing was moved. The workstation's threshold was lowered to −85 dBm by hand and NX "came back" by its window leaving and returning, so this says that the rule, the admission and the transfer work, and nothing about how near two devices have to be.
- **Put off by the owner on 2026-11-11.** The owner's judgment: a workstation and a phone say nothing of two phones, whose signal is different, so what was seen above does not test the gesture. Sending between two phones that are not paired was not observed (the two on the desk are paired with each other, and un-pairing the owner's phone was not for this work to do), nor was a real bringing near with the application's own threshold. The feature stays behind its switch, off by default, until that is done.
- **Changed with it, and said in the document:** the offer is now announced, and offers listened for, while a window is open on any page with the switch on, not only while Peers is shown; otherwise a device could be sent to only while its person looked at Peers.
- **A risk the owner's choice carries, stated:** a device counts as near by the strength it is heard at, which a transmitter can raise and nothing verifies. Listed files are offered, without a further press, to whichever device that takes files was heard strongly enough. They go encrypted to the key that device announced and its person must accept each, but they can be offered to a device the sender did not mean. What narrows it: exactly one such device, brought near after the files were listed, no destination chosen, no paired device near, sixteen files at most. Pairing first, or a code, remains for who must be sure.
- **Tests.** Core: the threshold from a measurement and its bounds. Network: what is announced admits for files by the capability made from it and never pairs by it; a connection admitted for files is never started as a pairing. Owner: a device that takes files is near by the strength the person set and one that only pairs never is; the strongest device is told only while a person measures. Interface: the rule of when files go (`view.rs`), and with two real owners that are not paired, files are refused while the device is only around, go when it is near under exactly the identifiers promised, arrive whole after being accepted, pair nobody, and a second send by the same announcement is refused (`link.rs`). **Not tested:** that a sender answered with something other than "for files" sends nothing; it is in the code (`Work::NearbyAdmitted`) and no test drives it.

### E-036 — The workstation's desktop session went down during the measuring of E-035, by a tool of the measuring, not by the application — CAUSE FOUND from the system's record; the tool is stopped

- **Seen.** At 12:25:33 on 2026-11-11 the workstation's shell shut down and took every window of the session with it, the application's among them. Found some ten minutes later, when the application's window could not be started again for want of a compositor.
- **The system's record.** At that second the system message bus said that the user had exceeded the bytes it may have waiting, and disconnected twelve of the user's connections because they could not be given a signal or a reply; the shell shut down within a tenth of a second.
- **Cause.** Two monitors of the Bluetooth service's signals had been started for the measurements (at 08:26 and at 10:08) by the tool that drives the tests, and their output was never read. A monitor nobody reads stops taking what it subscribed to, and the bus keeps it waiting, counted against the user. Both were among the connections disconnected at that second. That they filled the allowance alone is inferred from that and from nothing else having been left unread on purpose; the bus does not record who held how much.
- **Done.** Both monitors are stopped. A monitor of the bus is not to be left without a reader again: its output is read as it comes, or it is given a time to end.
- **Not done, and whose it is.** The session was not started again by who measured; that is the owner's to do, and what was open in it is gone. The application's window on this workstation stays closed until then, and what needs it is not observed until then.
- **Not known:** whether the application's own listening on that bus could be made to wait in the same way. It was not looked into and no test starves it; nothing seen points to it.

### E-037 — What the strict gate found in the tree of E-031 … E-036: two types the linter refuses, and a test that failed by the clock — FIXED

- **P3 — Two types too complex for the linter**, both written for the devices nearby: the answer of a device asked to take files, which carried the files inside its result (`network_host.rs`), and what a stand-in for BlueZ advertises, in a test (`nearby/linux.rs`). The gate stopped at each in turn. **Fix:** the files travel beside the result, as the role already does for a pairing; the stand-in's advertisement has a name. Nothing behaves differently, and the files are still let go when the asking fails.
- **P3 — A test that failed about once in sixty runs, by the time of day.** `guest_decisions_are_closed_and_disabled_in_production` passes 999 as a stage and as an outcome no vocabulary has, and then looks for `999` anywhere in the log of the child it ran. That log has seven lines, each with a time to the millionth of a second. The gate failed on it once. **Measured:** 300 runs of the child, `999` somewhere in the log of 5, each time inside a timestamp; the code itself in none. **Fix:** the unknown code is a number of nine digits, which neither a timestamp nor a process number can hold, and the test looks for that. The test still fails if the unknown code is ever written. It had not failed in the two gates recorded below; since when it reads so was not looked up (the file is not in a commit yet).

### E-038 — How files go to a device held near, changed at the owner's word: the other device has Receive open, and then nothing more is pressed; "to be paired with" and "takes files" are two things — BUILT; SEEN between a test instance of the workstation and one phone, both ways; between two phones not seen by who built it

- **What the owner said** on 2026-11-11, after trying E-035 between the two phones: the nearness is found very well, and the rest is wrong. It worked while the other phone was not on Receive, and the circle still had to be held, so bringing a device near only chose it. Wanted: one phone lists the file, the other has Receive open, and the first just sends; what is left is the confirmation in the application on the one that receives. And: being visible to be paired with and being visible and taking files are two different things, not to be mixed.
- **What it was** (E-032, E-035): one offer said both at once, on every page of an open window; a paired device held near was proposed as the destination and the circle held.
- **What it is.** An offer says one thing: to be paired with while the window shows Peers, files while it shows Receive, and nothing is announced on any other page (`nearby::Takes`, said by the window with `seek-nearby`). Each has its own gate, and what was announced for the one admits nothing for the other (`Invitations::create_nearby`, `Announced`). Listed files go at once, with nothing pressed, to the one paired device that is held near and says it takes files (`View::receiving_near`); a paired device that is near without Receive open is named on the page with what is missing (`View::near_waiting`); a destination picked by hand is never replaced. The proposal, and letting it go, are removed. Peers lists only devices that say they are there to be paired with. For a device that is not paired the rule of E-035 stands, with the same gate: it says it takes files only while its Receive is open. All of it is in [devices nearby](nearby-send.md).
- **P2, found while at it — What was announced stayed usable after the window was put away.** Read in the code, not seen used: what drives the radio stopped announcing when no window was in front, and never told the owner, so the invitation, and with it the gate for files, lived until it ran out: five minutes at most in which a device that had heard it could still offer files to a phone that had been put in a pocket. It was so since E-032 for the pairing it announced. **Fix:** it tells the owner once when the window goes behind or away, and the owner takes back what is out (`NearbyOffer { front }`). **Test:** the test with two real owners (`link.rs`): asked with no window in front, the owner announces nothing, and in front again it announces another invitation than before. That the old one then admits nobody is the gate's own test (`invitations.rs`).
- **P3 — A paired device that sent a file was called "A device".** The card of an offer named only what a sender said of itself while the offer came in, and a paired device says nothing then. **Fix:** a paired device is named as it is kept here (`View::of`); any other still by its own word. **Test:** `an_offer_is_said_to_be_from_a_paired_device_by_the_name_it_is_kept_under_here`. **Seen** on the phone and in the workstation's window.
- **P3 — A test target that did not compile, outside the gate.** `peersus-ui-android/tests/extension_services.rs` stood in for a function with a signature it no longer has. The gate leaves that crate out, so nothing said so. **Fix:** the stand-in takes what the function takes. It compiles now, and run on a workstation it ran no test at all; why it holds none there was not looked into.
- **Tests.** Core: an offer says one thing or the other and reads back so. Net: each announced thing has its gate, used once, and neither opens the other's. Owner and radio (`nearby/tests.rs`): a window open on a page that lets nobody find the device announces nothing and still hears others; a paired device is where files go only while it is near by its tag and says it takes files, at once no longer when it says another thing, 2.5 s after it falls silent, never when it is across the room, and never a device that is not paired. Two real owners (`link.rs`): nothing announced on another page, another invitation for files than for pairing, taken back when the window goes behind. View: files go to the one paired device near with Receive open and to nobody when there are two, when one was picked, or when the switch is off; who is named as waited for; Peers lists only who is there to be paired with.
- **Seen** (a test instance of the workstation in a window on a virtual display, paired with NX for it, the threshold lowered to −85 dBm by hand on whichever sent, nothing moved; the numbers and what each device showed are in [devices nearby](nearby-send.md#what-was-observed)): with NX on Send a listed file was not sent and the page named NX; with Receive opened on NX it was sent with nothing pressed, 0.4 to 4.0 s later in four runs; NX asked in the application, naming the sender; accepted, it arrived whole; cancelled, both sides said so. NX left Receive: the window stopped sending to it 2.8 to 3.3 s later, three runs, and a file listed after that stayed listed. The other way, a file picked on NX with the window on Receive was offered to the workstation 2.5 s after it was picked. What a page announces was seen from the phone: nothing on Send, to be paired with on Peers, files on Receive, and a pairing begun from the list still pairs.
- **Measured, and changed for it.** On the first build the first file went 3.2 s after Receive was opened and files could still go 6.1 s after it was left: the radio's driver asked the owner what to announce every 2 s, and what a paired device said was held for 4 s. Now it asks at every look (0.5 s; the asking is inside the process and puts nothing on the air) and holds for 2.5 s. One of four sendings still took 4.0 s; why was not looked into.
- **NOT seen:** two phones, which is what was asked for. The second phone is the owner's and is not driven, so that is the owner's to try: files listed on Send on the one, Receive open on the other, the two held together. A device really carried near at the application's own −55 dBm. A device that is not paired, with Receive as the gate, on devices. Two paired devices near at once with Receive open. Nothing drove the Send page in a test when this was written, as in E-035: the rule was tested, the page that carries it out only seen. E-039 added that test.
- **A risk that stands, stated.** That a paired device is near and has Receive open is said by two announcements nobody authenticates. Someone in range who knows a paired device's identifier can say both for it; listed files are then sent, without a press, to that paired device and to nobody else, over the connection that authenticates it, and its person is asked about each.

### E-039 — The Send page carrying out a send is driven by a test: the freeze of E-035 and the rule of E-038 are held by it — DONE; shown to fail on the defect it is for

- **What was missing.** E-035 and E-038 each ended by saying that no test drives the Send page: the rule of when files go was tested in the view, the sending between two owners in `link.rs`, and the page that joins the two only seen on devices. The freeze of E-035 was in exactly that joint.
- **Why the tests of the window could not do it.** They open the window on the demo, which is read-only and hands nothing over, by design.
- **What was built.** The window with no display can be opened on any session (`headless::Window::on`), and files can be dropped on it as a file manager drops them (`drop_files`). For tests only, a session on an owner that runs in the test's own process, with an identity of its own in the test's directory and paired with a second such owner (`Session::paired_for_test`); it is made of the helpers the tests of `link.rs` already had. Nothing of it is in a product build: it is behind `cfg(test)` and the `headless` feature.
- **What the test holds** (`files_listed_on_send_go_once_to_the_paired_device_held_near_with_receive_open_and_the_window_still_answers`). The real window on the real owner; only what the radio would say is said by the test. Near without Receive open: a dropped file is listed, nothing is sent, and the page names the device and what is missing. Receive opened: the file is sent once with nothing pressed, the page says so, and the other owner holds the offer. The window goes on answering: another page opens, and back on Send a second file goes too. Receive left: a file listed stays listed. The window runs on a thread of its own and is given two minutes, so a window that stops answering is a failure and not a test that never ends.
- **Shown to fail on the defect.** With the one line of E-035 put back as it was (the hand-over reading the names instead of looking at them), the test failed after its two minutes with *the window stopped answering*. With the fix back, the same file again byte for byte, it passed three times in a row, 1.4 s each.
- **What it is not.** No compositor, no radio, the desktop's layout only. It lists files by their paths, as a desktop does; the listing of an Android selection, which holds the files open and names them, goes through the same hand-over and is not driven by it. It says nothing of how near two devices are.
- **Gate:** it is among the tests of the window with no display, which the gate runs (`cargo xtask gate`, the step with the `headless` feature).

### E-040 — P1 on the owner's workstation — The desktop says "Identity unavailable" and no password is asked for: the keyring service cannot show its prompt, and two identity records were in it — CAUSE FOUND on the system; one message fixed; the system's part is the owner's

- **Reported** by the owner on 2026-11-11, after logging in again: the application asks for no keyring password and says *Cannot access system keyring*; after unlocking the keyring by hand it says *multiple matching identity records*.
- **What was found, each by looking.** The Secret Service on this workstation is `oo7-daemon` (0.7.0 beta, the distribution's package), not GNOME Keyring, which is not installed. The login screen's PAM configuration still names `pam_gnome_keyring.so`, which is not there, and `pam_oo7.so`, which is installed, is named nowhere: nothing unlocks a keyring at login, so every collection is locked after each one. Asked to unlock (`Unlock`, then `Prompt` on the returned prompt object, the same two calls the application makes), the service answered *CLI prompter failed: Destination does not exist*: it had chosen its terminal prompter, and no such prompter is on the bus. Its source chooses by the login session of the calling process as logind tells it; a program started by the desktop belongs to the user manager's session, whose type is `unspecified`, and that is taken for "not graphical". The same is reported upstream. Any program that needs a prompt is refused the same way; it is not this application's.
- **What the application did wrong:** it said the keyring could not be reached, when the keyring was reached and had answered. **Fix:** a `Prompt` the provider fails is its own message, *The system keyring is locked and its service did not show the password prompt* (`identity_unlock.rs`), and never an unreachable keyring or a cancel. **Test:** `a_provider_that_cannot_show_its_prompt_is_said_to_be_that_and_not_an_unreachable_keyring`, on the stand-in service the other unlock tests use; with the old mapping put back it failed with the old message, with the fix it passes. Seen on the owner's screen with the new build.
- **The two records.** With both collections unlocked by hand, two items matched the identity's attributes. One was in the collection that the service migrated from the old GNOME keyring file (all 75 of its items carry the one migration time), the other was made new two and a half hours before that migration, in the service's own collection, when the old one was not yet there to be found. The application refused to choose, which is what it is written to do. **Which is the device's:** with the owner's consent both secrets were read in memory only, a public key derived from each, and each looked for in what the test phone holds for this workstation: the migrated one is the identity the phone knows; the newer one it does not know. The owner's phone, connected later, holds the same identifier for the workstation as the test phone. Nothing secret was printed or kept.
- **Done, with the owner's consent:** the stray record was taken out of the search and not deleted: its `service` attribute was changed, so one record matches now. Undone by setting the attribute back.
- **How the stray one came to be: likely a test instance, not proved.** A profile with pairings refuses to make an identity when it finds none (`identity.rs`), so it was made by an instance whose directory had no pairings while the old keyring was not visible. A product build started on an empty temporary directory during testing does exactly that, and a temporary instance directory dated the same minute as the record is still on the workstation. A private data directory does not separate the keyring (E-023 says the same), so any product build started on an empty one while the real identity is hidden makes a second record. The guard is to test with the test-identity build only, which never touches the keyring.
- **Not done.** The system is as it was: the collection that holds the identity must be unlocked by hand after each login until the login screen is told to use `pam_oo7.so` (its own documentation gives the three lines) or the service is replaced; that is a change to `/etc` and the owner's to decide. The application cannot make a provider show a prompt it refuses to show. Whether KDE's provider behaves was not looked at.
- **Also seen, not acted on:** the real profile's `owner.log` is 150 MB; nothing rotates or bounds it.

### E-041 — The header, redrawn at the owner's word: two pieces in the top corners with the empty middle between them, a name that is written and rubbed out, and a bar that goes on its own once scrolling has decided — BUILT; SEEN on one phone, upright and on its side, and on a desktop window on a display of its own

- **What the owner said** on 2026-11-11, in turns, looking at each build: on a phone on its side the places down the left edge were pushed down by the bar; the line *THE TRANSFER PORTAL* under the name is a line of the bar that says nothing; the name could be written beside the mark and go away at the first thing done; the bar should not follow the finger and stop where the finger stops; and the bar should be `|mark) (state, Settings|`, each piece round toward the empty middle and square at the window's edge, on the desktop too.
- **What was built.**
  - *A phone on its side:* the places stand beside the header and the page, the whole short side theirs, and not under the header.
  - *The pieces:* the header is drawn as pieces (`header`, `piece` in `lib.rs`): the mark at the left, the state and Settings (and on a desktop the window's buttons) at the right, and on a desktop the places in a piece of their own between, with gaps that still move the window. The line under the name is gone from the header.
  - *Where a page is read down* (a phone, upright or on its side) the two pieces hang over the page's top corners and are no part of its layout; the page keeps their room free at its own top, inside what is scrolled (`Scrolling`). So nothing is laid out again when they go, which the form before this did and which showed as a jump.
  - *The bar goes on its own:* `view::HeaderHide` no longer counts pixels hidden. Scrolling decides: twelve pixels one way and the pieces are to go (down) or to come back (up); at the very top they are always there; a tremble decides nothing. They then slide the whole way on the motion everything else has (`motion::use_progress`), however far the finger goes.
  - *The name* (`Brand`): the mark nods and `eersus` is written beside it when the window opens; it is rubbed out at the first touch, click or key; a press on the mark writes it again and the next press anywhere rubs it out. In a window that is in front and that nobody has touched, it is written by itself after an uneven wait of 25 to 90 seconds and rubbed out 2.6 s later (`view::idle_name_after`); never with reduced motion, never in a window that is not in front. One sleeping thread per wait; nothing wakes in between.
- **Tests** (the rules, in `view.rs`): scrolling decides and the bar turns only after the turn; the top always shows it; a tremble and a position that is not a number change nothing; the name is whole letters only; the mark nods before any letter; the waits of an idle window are within their bounds, repeatable for a seed, and uneven. The pieces themselves are drawn by no test.
- **Seen** on the test phone (NX), development package: upright, at the top of Peers the two pieces and the title under them; a slow scroll down recorded at 30 frames a second showed the pieces go once (three frames) and stay gone, and come back once on scrolling up; three fresh starts with a quick short swipe each showed one hide and no return. On its side, the same, with the left piece against the places. Settings opens over the pieces and closes. The name: written at start, rubbed out at the first tap, written again by a press on the mark, rubbed out by a tap elsewhere; left alone for 110 s it was written at 7.6 s and at 42.3 s and rubbed out 2.8 s and 2.5 s later. On a desktop window of a test instance (an identity of its own, a display of its own): `|mark) (places) (state, Settings, window buttons|`, the name written and rubbed out at the first click.
- **What it costs, said plainly.** When the pieces come back in the middle of a page they are drawn over what is in its two top corners until the page moves again; the full bar before them pushed the page down instead. The owner named this cost before choosing.
- **Seen once and not again:** during the first run on the phone the window was found on Send about ninety seconds after it had been on Peers, with no press sent to it by the test. In the source only four things show Send: a press on its place, files shared from another application, a page of an extension that went away, and a window started anew; the name beside the mark was not written, so the window was not started anew. Left alone on Peers for 110 s it stayed, and a drag begun on a card's Send button did not press it. The phone is also in the owner's hand while builds are looked at, and a press by hand would be the first of the four; that is likely and was not asked. Not explained beyond that.
- **Not seen:** the owner's phone, which the last package was installed on and which was not driven. The pieces on a light theme. A desktop narrower than its places need. The offer bar over the window together with the pieces.

### E-042 — macOS built and tested in a guest for the first time — SEEN: it compiles, builds and the tests pass; the project's own build entry point fails there; no window was ever shown

- **What the owner decided** on 2026-11-11 and 2026-11-12: run a macOS 14 guest on this workstation (not Apple hardware; the licence's terms are his to take, he clicked Agree in the guest), finish its Setup Assistant himself, and have the application built and tried in it.
- **Where it is written down:** [macOS evaluation](macos-evaluation.md), section "Tried in a guest", with the commands, times and the exact errors. In short, on macOS 14.8.9 (x86-64, 4 CPUs, 8 GiB, software graphics): `cargo metadata` and `cargo check` of the crates pass; the owner and `peersus-ui` (Skia from source, 13 min 24 s, needs `ninja`) build; `cargo test -p peersus-ui --lib` passes 648 tests and the display-less window tests pass 15; the other crates' tests pass but for one compile error and six failures, all explained (below); the native helper step of `./dev.sh desktop` fails on Apple's flags; `peersus-ui` aborts at start with *No Metal-capable device found*, in the guest with and without the vGPU option.
- **Found and fixed:** a test helper (`update_install.rs`) did not compile where the process mask is 16 bits (`mode_t` on Apple targets). One line, with a comment; the Linux lint of the crate passes with it.
- **Found, not fixed:** (1) the native helper's `xtask` step: `-pie` is an unused argument under `-Werror`, and Apple's linker rejects `-Wl,-z,relro,-z,now`. The source file itself compiles (the earlier page inferred it would not; Apple's headers define `MSG_NOSIGNAL`). (2) With no Metal device the interface `panic`s and cannot unwind, so a person sees the system's crash dialog and nothing from the program. (3) Data is kept in `~/.local/share/peersus` on macOS. (4) Four tests bind `127.0.0.2`, which only Linux has by default; two tests hold many files and need more than macOS's default 256 descriptors; the program raises no limit of its own, and nobody measured whether a desktop gets near 256.
- **How it went, for the next person:** the web viewer loses every key that needs Shift, so commands went over SSH once Remote Login was on, and that had to be switched on in System Settings (the command is refused without Full Disk Access). The first copy of the sources dropped `skia/src/android` because an exclude pattern named `android/` matched at any depth; that was this session's mistake, not the project's. The VM images (89 GB) and the build output (321 GB) live inside the repository directory and must be excluded when copying it.
- **Not seen:** any window; Apple Silicon; real Metal; the keychain with a person at it; packaging; signing.

### E-043 — Every extension carries a licence and a notice the person accepts to install it; a package from outside the store has a warning that must be read to its end and held — BUILT; SEEN on a desktop window of a test instance; not seen on a phone; store and Apple/Google rules read, not yet acted on

- **What the owner asked** on 2026-11-12: a store (Google Play, Apple) will only accept the application if every extension carries its licence and notice and the person reads them; the overlay is the application's and not editable, with `Cancel` at the far left and `Accept TOS` at the right (alone at the right for one page); an extension can only ever learn `extension.tos.accepted`; the same is enforced in CI and for extensions from outside the store, which get a very plain warning that must be scrolled to the bottom and then held; the install button is one button that does both. An API-expansion item for an optional, permission-gated telemetry extension was added (see `architecture.md`, "API expansion (TODO)").
- **Built.** `peersus-api::agreement` (pages of kind licence, notice or terms; each says what it is and what changes; plain content blocks with no size, position, id or action; `https` links only; limits and validation); `Manifest.agreements`; the digest of an agreement (`peersus-core::agreements`); `ConfirmInstall` takes the digest the person accepted and, for a package from outside the store, `risk_acknowledged`; the owner refuses a package without an agreement at staging, at confirmation and in the installer itself (`Manager::install`), so the command line, the window and the store all go through it; acceptance is kept per user in `agreements.json`, recorded before installing and undone if installing fails, kept across an update that changes no word, asked again when a word changes, and forgotten when the extension is removed; a store install now stages the verified package and waits for the acceptance unless it is the one already accepted; `peersus.extension` method `tos` answers `{accepted}` to the caller only; the command line `plugin-install` prints the warning and every page and installs only with `--accept <digest> --accept-outside-store-risk`. The window: `agreement_flow` (rules, no toolkit) and `agreement_overlay` (drawing); the install button only asks, and the overlay at the root does the rest.
- **CI and tooling** (done by an agent, read and run by this session): `cargo xtask check-manifest` (the one place the rules are applied, in Rust) wired into the extension source and upload scripts, into the validate and approve workflows (without changing triggers, permissions or secrets), the pull request template and `CONTRIBUTING`; the example extensions, `templates/hello-guest` and the test catalog fixtures carry an agreement; `docs/extension-store.md` and `docs/api/manifests.md` have the section.
- **Tests.** Contract: 78 in `peersus-api`, the digest in core, the ledger (damaged, hostile and full files read as nothing accepted), the installer, the pure overlay rules (steps, buttons, size, scroll end, hold). Owner, with real processes: a bare package cannot be shown; wrong digest, none, or an unacknowledged risk are each refused and record nothing; removal forgets; a store install waits and installs only the agreement it showed; an update with the accepted agreement is not asked again; the command line shows, refuses and accepts as described. A first install by hand failed because the data directory did not exist yet when the acceptance was written; found by the command-line test and fixed (the directory is made, private, first).
- **Seen** on a desktop window of a test instance (own identity, own display), with a demo package of three pages with a list, a table, callouts and links: the warning as step 1 of 4, the button filling while held and emptying when let go early, then the pages with `Cancel` at the left and `Back`/`Next`/`Accept TOS` at the right; accepting installed it and recorded the acceptance; `Cancel` at the warning left nothing installed or recorded.
- **Limits, said plainly.** On a window as large as a desktop the warning is short and fits without scrolling, so "read to the end" is met at once there; it scrolls where the window is small (a phone) and the button waits for the end. The hold and the scroll are the window's: the owner cannot see them, and trusts the digest and the flag the window sends, which is the same trust as for every other command the window sends. Not seen: a phone (upright or on its side), a light theme, a very long agreement, an extension with a page of its own that fails to load. An extension installed before this feature keeps running and answers `false`.
- **What the two policy reads found** (kept as [Google Play](store-policy-google-play.md) and [Apple](store-policy-apple.md), primary sources read on 2026-11-12, not yet acted on; the owner decides): *Google Play* — the accessibility service `RemoteInput` ships in the shared manifest and needs an in-app disclosure, consent and a declaration, or must go to the community flavour; there is no in-app link to the privacy policy (also Apple 5.1.1(i)) and `site/privacy.html` is a draft; Skia's full licence texts are "being added" (`about.rs`) and Shizuku is uncredited; whether Google accepts WASM as an interpreter is unconfirmed. *Apple* — native extensions and the self-updater cannot ship in the Mac App Store, a store of third-party extensions risks guideline 3.2.2(i), the Ko-fi button is an outside payment link (3.1.1), the program aborts without Metal (2.1), encryption export documents are probably due, and no `.app`, signing or notarisation exists. A licence screen at launch is forbidden (2.4.5(vi)); this one is at install.
- **The macOS guest as a libvirt domain** (an agent, then checked): `vms/macos/` holds a copy of the guest's disks, `macos-sonoma.xml` is defined under `qemu:///session` (to open it in Virtual Machine Manager add a "QEMU/KVM user session" connection), and `README.md` says how to start it. The agent saw it boot to the Apple logo and its progress bar; checked afterwards by this session: a start reached SSH on the forwarded port in 60 seconds (macOS 14.8.9). `virsh shutdown` (ACPI) was not acted on by the guest at its login window within 5 minutes, and `shutdown -h now` over SSH worked; nobody looked at the console in Virtual Machine Manager. The Podman container must not run on the same disk.

### E-044 — The numbered section labels are gone; both guests are libvirt domains startable from Virtual Machine Manager; macOS tried again: the window was seen there with the software renderer, four "eighty files" tests fail by time — BUILT; SEEN on NX (Send page) and in the macOS guest (first run, Send, Receive); macOS tests run, four fail, cause only half found

- **What the owner asked** on 2026-11-12: remove `01 / OUTGOING` and `02 / INCOMING` ("decorative, says nothing"), standardise the two guests under `vms/` so they start from Virtual Machine Manager as QEMU/KVM, test on macOS, deploy to Android, and have agents research the market.
- **Labels.** Removed from the interface and the translations: the two hero labels and the `02 / OR PICK A SAVED DEVICE` prefix of the wide Send page (found by the second look, and seen on the old binary in the macOS guest; the same word without a number already existed in the catalog). `site/index.html` still numbers its sections (`01 / THE HANDOFF` …): it is the web page, not the application, and was left as it is for the owner to say.
- **Guests.** `vms/windows/windows-server.xml` is new: the Windows image is an MBR install (the first UEFI try ended at *No bootable option*), so it boots by BIOS, on SATA and `e1000e` because it was installed without virtio drivers; SSH answers on `127.0.0.1:2222`. `vms/macos/macos-sonoma.xml` now forwards `2223` (so both can run together). Both forwards are bound to `127.0.0.1` only (seen with `ss -ltn` for both after a restart: the macOS domain was first running the old definition on `*:2223`). `vms/README.md` says how to start, stop and rebuild them; `sync-build.sh` now works from any directory. `vms/` is ignored by git (disks, keys). **The connection in Virtual Machine Manager: first claimed, not true, then done.** The session's `gsettings set` had written to a store Virtual Machine Manager does not read (`dconf read` still showed only `qemu:///system`), and the owner opened it and saw nothing. The connection was then added in the program's own window (File → Add Connection → *QEMU/KVM user session*): its list now shows *QEMU/KVM User session* with `macos-sonoma` and `windows-server`, and `dconf` holds both connections with autoconnect. Not done: nobody opened either console in the Virtual Machine Manager window.
- **macOS tests, run in the guest on a clean tree** (an interrupted copy first stalled over the forwarded port; the tree was sent as one archive, without `vendor/`, and `vendor/` and `target/` of the old tree kept): `cargo metadata`, `check`, the tests of the core crates (640 + others pass), the window tests with no display (15) pass; **four fail** — see [macOS evaluation](macos-evaluation.md): not descriptors this time but time; one of them passes when its 20 s limit is raised, which shows slow, not stuck. The product's behaviour with many files on a real Mac is unknown.
- **The window on macOS, seen** (asked again by the owner after this session had said "not launched"): logged in at the guest's console from the host, started from that session, with `FREYA_RENDERER=software`, the application shows its window: first-run name (typed, kept on the next start), Send and Receive of the current tree (built in the guest after copying the two changed files; no numbered labels), the keychain's prompt after the rebuild. Without the setting it aborts on Metal as in E-042. Two earlier conclusions of this same session were wrong and are corrected in the documents: "the display shows only the logo" (pictures taken before the guest had finished starting; the display also lags 20 to 40 s) and "launch not possible in a guest". Details and what was not tried (transfer, pairing, file dialog, Metal, a real Mac): [macOS evaluation](macos-evaluation.md); screenshots `shots/macos_*.png`; how to repeat it: `vms/macos/README.md`.
- **Android.** Rebuilt and installed on NX and ROG, byte for byte verified (after the relogin the phones had to be found again by `adb mdns services`); the Send page on NX was looked at and has no numbered label; ROG only installed.
- **Market research** (two agents, files in `docs/marketing/`, linked from `docs/marketing.md`): competitors with sources (LocalSend, AlterSend, WeTransfer and others), a comparison matrix, a launch plan, a first-day plan, a six-week posting calendar, the website, channels, assets. The one finding that changes the message: AlterSend (a public repository from May 2026) already ships pair-once, end-to-end, LAN and internet, no account, on five platforms; "works over the internet" is not our difference. Read by this session for wording (honest about Peersus's minuses, no unsupported claims in the drafts), not fact-checked source by source.

### E-045 — The keyring's password prompt: the application asked without opening a session, so the service gave it a terminal prompter; fixed in the unlock path — BUILT, tests pass; the prompt itself NOT SEEN by this session; a second identity record is back

- **What the owner reported** on 2026-11-12: *Identity unavailable: The system keyring is locked and its service did not show the password prompt*, while other programs on the same workstation do get the prompt.
- **Cause, read in the service's source at the commit the installed package is built from** (`oo7-daemon` 0.7.0 beta, commit `e91b98a`): `Prompt.Prompt` chooses the prompter from the Secret Service *session* the calling connection opened (`session_from_sender` → `peer_info` → graphical or not). A connection with no session has no peer information, counts as not graphical and is given the terminal prompter, which does not exist under a desktop: *CLI prompter failed*. The application called `Unlock` and `Prompt` on a connection that had opened no session; a program that uses the usual library opens one first, which is why those get the dialog. E-040's reading ("the service picks the terminal prompter for programs started from the desktop") was half of it: it is true only of a caller without a session.
- **Fix:** `identity_unlock::prepare` opens a session (`OpenSession`, `plain`, no secret crosses it, it ends with the connection) before it asks to unlock, and only when something is locked. The stand-in service in the tests now refuses a prompt to a caller without a session when told to behave like that provider; `a_provider_that_picks_its_prompter_by_the_callers_session_is_given_one_before_the_unlock` and `an_unlocked_identity_opens_no_session` are new; the ten unlock tests pass. `cargo fmt --check` and `./dev.sh desktop` pass. **The whole gate was not run again for this change.**
- **On the real service, once:** with both collections locked (read at 05:56), the rebuilt owner was started from a desktop-launched program's scope. It did not report the missing prompt; six seconds later it ended with *System keyring contains multiple matching identity records*, and the collection read unlocked. So the unlock stage was passed. **Not proved: that the dialog was shown.** The owner was at the workstation at that moment and may have typed the password into it, or may have unlocked by hand in the same seconds; this session did not look at the screen. One answer from the owner settles it.
- **Two identity records again.** `SearchItems` (paths and attribute names only, no secret read): `default_keyring/46` (made 11-09 22:18, the one the test phone knows, E-040) and `login/2` (made 11-09 19:50) both carry `service=Peersus`, `username=transport-identity`. E-040 had changed the `service` attribute of the second so that one matched; it reads `Peersus` again and its modified time equals its created time, so that change is gone since the service restarted at the owner's login (why was not found; that the service never wrote it is a guess). A third item under the same service is the development catalog's key, with another `username`, and is not an identity. Nothing was changed in the keyring this time: it is the owner's to allow.
- **Unlock at login is a different matter and is not fixed by this:** the workstation logs in automatically, so no password is typed at login and no PAM module could unlock the keyring with it; the prompt at the first use is what a person gets.
- **macOS guest, said too loosely before:** nothing is *installed* there. The project has no macOS package (`.app`, `.dmg`); what runs is the development build from a launcher file. The owner looked for an application in the guest and found none. `~/Desktop/Peersus.command` was put on the guest's desktop and started; the keychain's dialog is waiting there for the guest's login password (the first time `Allow` was pressed, not `Always Allow`, so it asks at each start).

### E-046 — macOS: a bundle a person opens; the owner's pairing attempts with the guest: what each message was; messages drawn under the header's pieces (fixed); how far the camera's reader follows the drawn code (measured) — BUILT; bundle SEEN in the guest; pairing with the guest NOT achieved

- **What the owner reported** on 2026-11-12, while testing the macOS guest with the phones: nothing called Peersus in the guest; pairing by scanning the guest's code fails with *Connection queue is full*, then *invitation expired* and *Local command failed; command outcome may be unknown*, and "it stays stuck there"; the camera is black and white, stutters and practically never reads the code; "in Settings a shade or colour appears over the notification". Manual pairing was refused by the owner as a proof: the code must be scanned.
- **The bundle.** `scripts/stage-macos.py` (new, with `scripts/test-stage-macos.py`, five tests, in the gate's metadata step; they pass on Linux and in the guest under Python 3.9) stages `Peersus.app`; a release build in the guest took 19 m 41 s; the bundle is in the guest's `/Applications` and was seen running as an application (name in the menu bar, mark in the Dock, bundle identifier, keychain, first-run page): [macOS evaluation](macos-evaluation.md). The launcher files this session had left in the guest are removed. Two things found on the way are written there and not changed: a first start that waits on the keychain longer than 90 s says the application could not start, and the data is not where a Mac keeps application data.
- **Pairing, what was established.**
  - The messages are on the test phone (its owner's list held *Connection queue is full* and *Invalid or expired invitation*; the owner's own phone held none). The guest's owner log holds **no attempt from any phone**: nothing reached it.
  - *Connection queue is full* is the one sentence `runtime.rs` gives for any refusal of `NetworkHost::join`, which refuses only when eight admissions are pending. The counting was read line by line and is balanced; every counted wait is bounded (connect 30 s, the first stream 30 s, each message 30 s) **except `connection.open_bi()` in `handshake::join`**, which had no bound. Whether that is what filled it is **not known**: when it happened and what was pending could not be read, because the test phone's system log gives nothing to the shell (`0 B readable`) and this session had cleared it before looking (a mistake: evidence destroyed). **Bounded afterwards** (`handshake.rs`, the same 30 s as its neighbours): the tests and the lint of `peersus-net` pass; no test makes a peer that grants no stream, so the new bound itself is not exercised by one. **It is in no built artifact yet**: the Android package on the two phones and `Peersus.app` in the macOS guest were both built before it, so a pairing retry does not exercise it until `peersus-net` is built into them. The two other bare `open_bi` calls (`reconnect.rs` probes, the stream of a kept session) hold no admission and were left.
  - *Local command failed; command outcome may be unknown* is what the window shows under the code field for **every refusal by the owner**, not only for a broken connection. Reproduced on the test phone: an invalid code was joined, the owner counted one more *Invalid or expired invitation* (`errors_said` 2 → 3, read from its state) and the field said the line above. `ipc.rs` turns the owner's sentence into an error and the window's bridge, whose result is a fixed sentence (`&'static str`), replaces it. It was so before this session (the committed version does the same). The owner's sentence still arrives as a message at the top. **Not fixed**: the owner's sentence has to be carried through that channel, in two crates.
  - The guest can be reached only through a relay: it is behind the virtual machine's address translation and the owner listens on a port chosen at each start (`NetworkHost::listening_port` says so), so no forward can be written for it. Its invitation lists addresses that are the host's, which lead nowhere.
  - **The join path after the scan works (diagnostic, 2026-11-12 07:22).** The owner of the test phone was handed the macOS application's current invitation over its control socket (not by scanning, and not offered as proof that scanning works): the phone answered at once, and within 3 seconds **both** owners held a pairing request, with no error on either side and the guest's relay connected. Clocks of the host, the guest and the phone were read and agree to the second (so expiry is not it). The request was refused on both sides afterwards and nothing was kept. So what fails for the owner is **before** `join`: the camera does not read the code the guest's window draws.
- **The drawn code now carries only the routes it can show readably (new).** `qr::for_disc` (used by the one place that draws every code, `Drawn::of`) draws the code with every route when that is four pixels a module or more on the disc, as before; when it would be smaller, it draws the fullest code that reaches four, leaving routes out in the order relays, then IPv4, then IPv6 (`Invitation::compact_for_qr_within`, new, on the radio form's own rule; a route of a custom transport is not carried then). If leaving routes out cannot reach four pixels, nothing is left out. What is left out is out of the drawing only: the invitation, the owner and the copied text keep every route, and the scanner expands what it reads as before. The crowded invitation of the measurement (a relay and six addresses, 65 modules, three pixels on the desktop's disc) is drawn with fewer modules at four pixels or more, is read back by the camera's own entry at blur 0, 1 and 2 (it read nothing at blur 2 before), and keeps the relay. A phone's own screen (a far larger disc) draws every route as before. Tests: five (net 1, interface 4); the interface's code tests (70) and the network's invitation tests pass. **Cost:** a device that needs one of the left-out routes (a second LAN address, an IPv6 one) does not get it from the picture; it still reaches the relay, and a desktop with no relay keeps its first IPv4 routes. **Not seen with a camera**: the drawing was checked against the camera's reader with a model of blur, not with a phone looking at a screen.
- **Seen on the real screen, and what is still not seen.** The new code (with the 30 s bound on the join's stream and the shorter drawing) was built into the Android package (both phones, byte for byte verified) and into `Peersus.app` in the guest (release build 5 m 8 s, the old bundle moved aside, the keychain asked again for the new seal and was answered). The guest's own Peers page was captured at the screen's own pixels (`shots/macos_app_pair_code_new.png`), the disc cut out as a grey square and handed to the camera's own entry (`how_far_the_camera_reader_follows_a_frame_taken_from_a_real_screen`, an ignored probe: it reads a file named by `PEERSUS_FRAME` and prints, nothing is asserted): read **as captured**, and read at every turn tried at blur 1 and 2 with the frame as it is; shown 80 % and 65 % as large (a window scaled down) it reads at blur 0 and 1 and not at blur 2; at 50 % only without blur (3 of 4); at 40 % almost nothing. The code the guest drew carried one route fewer than the full 5 (the long form read back is 906 characters against 914). **This is the app's own reader on the pixels of the screen, not a phone's camera looking at a monitor: no phone has scanned a code since the change**, and Virtual Machine Manager's scaling and the lens are the conditions that decide it. Tests of both crates after the changes: peersus-net 240, peersus-ui 661, no failures; the lint (`-D warnings`) of both is clean; the whole gate was not run.
- **Messages drawn under the header's pieces: fixed.** Seen on the test phone (`shots/nx_notice_under_header.png`): the message at the top lay under the mark and under the way to Settings, which covered its close button, so it could not be read whole nor sent away, and a press on its close opened Settings. This is what the owner's sentence about Settings and the notification describes, as far as this session can tell (the other thing seen, the page blurred and darkened under the open Settings sheet with a red message showing through as a smear, `shots/nx_settings_over_notice.png`, is as designed). It came with E-041, which put the pieces over the page's top corners and did not move the messages. `Form::notices_clear_of_header` (in `view.rs`, tested for the three forms) now starts them under the pieces' band.
- **The camera's reader, measured** (`how_far_the_camera_reader_follows_the_drawn_code`, an ignored probe in `qr.rs`: a real invitation with a relay and six addresses, drawn as the portal draws it, handed to the camera's own entry smaller, turned by 0, 5, −8 and 12 degrees and out of focus by a mean of 1 to 3 pixels; white and black ground gave the same where both were run):

  | camera pixels a module | sharp | blur 1 | blur 2 | blur 3 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | 7, 6, 5 | 4 of 4 | 4 of 4 | 4 of 4 | 4 of 4 |
  | 4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 0 |
  | 3 | 4 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
  | 2.4 | 3 | 2 | 0 | 0 |
  | 1.9 | 2 | 0 | 0 | 0 |

  That invitation is 371 characters as drawn, **65 modules a side**, and the desktop's portal (a 384 px disc) draws it at **3 px a module**, the least the code allows itself (`LEAST_SCALE`). The reader needs five camera pixels a module to be indifferent to focus; at three, a blur of two pixels reads nothing. A phone's own screen draws the same code at about eleven pixels a module, which is why phone to phone works. On the owner's phone the camera log of the attempts says the same from the other side: 313 s of looking, 115 s of them with a lit, contrasty centre, **one** read. The guest's window is also shown through Virtual Machine Manager, which may scale it down further (not measured). The preview is grey by design (the camera hands over the brightness plane only; `preview.rs` says so): colour needs the two colour planes carried from Java, a wider change and the owner's choice. The disc itself cannot be made larger on a desktop (`Form::portal_extent` never exceeds the portal's own 420), and a window of 900 by 590 has no room for more; so the lever that was taken is fewer routes in the drawing (the bullet "The drawn code now carries only the routes it can show readably", above). Not taken: a larger disc on a maximised window, and colour.

### E-047 — macOS: the pointer and keyboard of a controlling device replayed on the Mac (new) — BUILT; compiles on Linux and in the guest; NOT RUN on a Mac

- **What the owner reported** on 2026-11-12: the control extension does not work on macOS. **Cause (read in source):** `DesktopInput::system()` had no device for macOS; the fallback made every replay answer *unsupported* (`desktop_input.rs`). Linux (uinput) and Windows (SendInput through `enigo`) had one. Nothing else gates the service: `peersus.input` and its `input.inject` permission are declared for every desktop.
- **Change.** The module that replayed events on Windows is now `injector`, shared by Windows and macOS (`enigo` 0.6.1, already in the lockfile with its macOS dependencies; `Cargo.toml` of the app lists it for both). The replay of motion, a position (a share of the main display), wheel notches and the three buttons is the same code; only the key of an event differs by platform: Windows keeps its extended-key table and set-1 scan codes, macOS maps the Linux key code to a `CGKeyCode` (`virtual_key`, ANSI keys, the Mac's modifiers and arrows, Insert as Help), so typed text follows the receiving Mac's own layout for keys it has and a key without a Mac key is left out. `unsafe` is not used here; `enigo` is the maintained safe interface.
- **Evidence.** `cargo test -p peersus-app --lib desktop_input` on Linux: 29 passed, 0 failed, 5 ignored; clippy `-D warnings` on the crate with its tests: clean; `cargo check --locked -p peersus-app --lib` in the macOS guest: finished.
- **Not verified.** Nothing was replayed on a Mac: whether the system asks for the Accessibility permission when the device is first opened (`enigo` asks by default; refused, the replay says it is unsupported), whether each mapped key types what it should, and whether the control extension then works end to end from a phone. **No macOS arm exists for `displays::current`**, so a Mac publishes no displays and a pointer of a device's own is refused there: the extension's Layout page and pass-through are not part of this change. The extension's README (a local, ignored copy) still says macOS is limited.

### E-048 — The camera in colour with a reader-friendly picture; a code with more room on a small disc; two identity records no longer end the application — BUILT; camera installed on both phones, seen live on NX in a dark room; the rest NOT driven

- **Requested** on 2026-11-12: a camera that is in colour, focuses, and reads more easily, after looking at how others do it. Others (CameraX, ML Kit's own scanner) show a colour preview from the camera's own surface and read the whole frame; ML Kit asks for at least two pixels a module and 1280 by 720 or 1920 by 1080. Here the preview is drawn by the toolkit from the frame, so the colour has to cross to Rust.
- **Changes.** (1) `CodeCamera.java` asks for a stream of the sensor's own shape (4:3 on a phone, not a 16:9 crop that loses a quarter of the height), at most 3.2 million pixels, reuses its brightness array, and sends the colour planes of the centre square at half size, refreshed every third frame (`cameraFrame(luma, u, v, chromaSide, w, h)`; `camera_bridge.rs`; `scanner::Colour`, `Frame.colour`). (2) `preview::disc_raster_coloured`: a pixel of the picture is the mean of at most 4 by 4 samples of the frame it covers, not the one under its middle; BT.601 colour; grey when the planes do not fit. (3) `qr::for_disc` asks for five camera pixels a module first, then four (`COMFORT_SCALES`). (4) The focus code of this session (a search again when the sharpness of the middle falls) is in the same Java file; the mode stays auto driven by triggers, because the comment in `CodeCamera.java` records a measurement on a real phone of continuous-picture settling "not focused, locked" on a near screen (not re-measured here).
- **Evidence.** `cargo test -p peersus-ui --lib`: 664 passed, 0 failed, 5 ignored; clippy `-D warnings`: clean. New tests: a fine pattern averages to grey, colour planes that fit tint and ones that do not stay grey, colour follows the quarter turns, `for_disc` prefers the roomier size. Android development build installed on NX (byte-for-byte verified) and on ROG; NX opened the camera and showed a live picture with a faint colour cast in a dark room.
- **Not verified.** Frame rate, the focus behaviour and how far a code is read (the log of NX returned nothing this time, so the camera's own figures were not read; the room was dark); anything on ROG beyond the install; scanning a code. On the desktop disc a code with relay and one address now loses the address to reach five pixels a module: a device that could have used it must go through the relay.
- **Identity: two records.** Reported 2026-11-12: *Identity unavailable: System keyring contains multiple matching identity records*, a dead end. `identity::resolve_ambiguous` now settles it instead of refusing: the record chosen before (`identity.id` holds only its public key) if still there, else the first by public key, the same on every start; nothing is deleted or written to the keyring. Tested with two records in either order, a remembered choice and the same key twice. **Why two exist is not proven.** A lookup that finds nothing while a collection is locked makes a new record beside the hidden one; for a profile **without pairings** the application does not unlock first (a test, `first_use_does_not_request_unlock_of_unrelated_collections`, holds that decision), which is the candidate cause (a test instance with a fresh data directory shares the real keyring account).
