The settled choice may be the identity that the paired devices do not know; they then must be paired again.
- **macOS guest, extension.** The guest's bundle was rebuilt (release, 9 m 36 s) and replaced in `/Applications`; the control extension (WASM, `control-macos.tar.gz` from `package-native-extension.py`) was installed with `plugin-install` after the licence, notice and outside-the-store warning were read and accepted; after a restart its host process runs. `input.inject` is off, and the Accessibility permission is not asked for yet: neither was granted or tried.
- **Identity, second report (same day): "none could be read".** The first settle did not work on the real keyring: a credential made from one of several records matches all of them when it looks itself up, so reading its secret was ambiguous again and every record read as unreadable. `identity::record_secrets` now reads every matching item through the Secret Service credential. **Seen on the real keyring** with a throwaway data directory: the owner starts, logs *Several identity records are in the keyring; one was chosen and none was changed*, and writes `identity.id`; `identity-list` shows two records. The record in use (`80dc90bf…`) is the one the test phone holds for this workstation (matched by public key prefix in the phone's snapshot); the other (`f3bb5165…`) is not known to it.
- **A person is never locked out.** New commands that work when the application cannot start (they are carried out before an owner is looked for): `peersus identity-list` (the start of each record's public key and which is in use; nothing secret), `peersus identity-use <start of the record>` (the choice is kept in `identity.id`; the keyring is not touched), `peersus identity-reset --yes` (deletes EVERY record of the identity from the keyring, forgets the choice and puts `peers.json` aside as `peers.json.old-<time>`, kept; refused without `--yes`; refused to delete when the keyring could not be read). The messages that end a start (*multiple records, none readable*, *stored identity invalid*, *identity for existing pairings unavailable*) now name them. Tests (mock keyring): the listing, a choice that survives another record appearing and a vague choice that changes nothing, a reset that sets pairings aside and lets a new identity be made, a reset that does not delete when the keyring cannot be read; and the command line's parsing. The reset was **not** run on the real keyring.
- **Pair page.** The code shown for the other device can now be copied with a button (the toolkit's selection did not copy for the owner); *Copied* is said on it. On Peers, where devices nearby can be found (Linux and Android) and the switch is off, a section says so and has the button that turns it on (before, only the code and paste were shown and the list was not there). macOS has no radio for it: nothing is listed there. Not driven on a screen. **IME on the desktop:** reported, not diagnosed: the toolkit enables it for the field roles this application gives (`UrlInput`, `SearchInput`, `PasswordInput` are in its list), so the cause is not the role; what the owner types, on which system and with which input method is not yet known.
- **macOS Accessibility prompt.** After the grant, macOS asked again. Two causes, one fixed: (1) the device was made again at each batch of input until it could be made, and each attempt asked the system for the permission (`open_prompt_to_get_permissions`, the default), so the dialog came up again and again; it is now asked once in a run and afterwards only looked at (`desktop_input::injector::settings`). (2) The bundle is signed ad hoc, and macOS ties a grant to the signature of that build: after each rebuild the old entry in Privacy & Security looks switched on and does not apply; its entry must be removed and the new build asked again (`tccutil reset Accessibility <bundle id>`). Not tried yet on the guest at the time of writing.
- **The way out is in the window, not on a command line.** The owner said, rightly, that almost nobody uses the command line and that a person who is stuck removes the application. The screen that says *Identity unavailable* now has *Start over with a new identity* (two steps: it says that the identity kept by the system is deleted, the pairings are set aside and the devices must be paired again, and asks again), which does what `identity-reset --yes` does, off the window's thread; it then says to close the window and open Peersus again. Desktop only (Android keeps its identity in its own store). Not driven on a screen; the logic it calls is the tested one above.
- **Two agreements, not one (extensions from a file).** Before, the application's warning about an extension from outside the store was shown after the file was chosen and again for every extension. They are two things and now behave so: the **person's** acceptance of that warning is asked **once**, before the file dialog (the three paragraphs of the warning and *I understand and accept the risk*), kept as `Settings.outside_store_risk_accepted`, and never asked again; the owner then installs a package from a file without the window's per-install acknowledgement; and the agreement overlay has no warning step once it is kept (a package chosen another way, as on Android's picker, still reaches the old step the first time and keeps the acceptance when it is held). The **extension's** licence and notice are its own, read for each extension (`extension.tos.accepted`). The person's acceptance is exposed to extensions as one more boolean of the same service: `peersus.extension`, method `outside_store`, answer `{"accepted": true|false}` (documented in `docs/api/manifests.md`, tested in `an_extension_learns_only_whether_its_own_agreement_was_accepted`). The command line's `plugin-install` is unchanged (it still asks for the digest and `--accept-outside-store-risk` each time). Not driven on a screen. Lint of the crates clean; tests: core, api, app, ui all pass; the metadata check passes.
- **Control from ROG to the macOS guest: not working, not diagnosed to the end.** ROG's log: the stream the extension writes to the other device is reset (`ConnectionReset`, then *A transfer to a device was interrupted*); the guest's log says only *A plugin could not complete the action* each time, which is the extension's call failing on the receiving side. In the guest `input.inject` is granted and `control.use` is on for ROG; the Accessibility grant had just been reset (see above) and the system's list was open when last seen. Not shown: the real error of the replay (the guest's log holds no trace-level lines), so whether it is the missing Accessibility grant or something in the replay itself is still open. The guest was **not** rebuilt again after the fixes above, so as not to invalidate the grant a second time.
- **Copy and paste in the text fields (Linux, GNOME on Wayland): reported, NOT fixed.** The owner: no edit menu, Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V do nothing, a right click does nothing. Read in the toolkit: the field has an edit menu that a held press opens (a touch idea) and no right-click path; the keys go to `freya-edit`, which copies through the toolkit's global `Clipboard` and swallows every error (`Clipboard::set(..).ok()`), so a clipboard that cannot be reached looks exactly like a key that does nothing. **Done:** a right click opens the same edit menu (vendored `input.rs`, noted in `vendor/freya-components/PATCHES.md`; built, not seen on a screen). **Tried and taken out again:** installing an `arboard`-backed provider over the toolkit's: `arboard` failed on this session with *X11 server connection timed out because it was unreachable* (in the build container and on the host alike), so it would have made things worse; the toolkit's own Wayland provider (`copypasta`'s `wayland_clipboard`, the same in 0.5.0-rc.13 and 0.5.2) is the right mechanism for GNOME, which has no data-control protocol, and why it fails for the owner is not known. **Not shown:** that Ctrl+C works or fails in the real window: a window of the application could not be found by the desktop tool in this session, so no copy was driven. The Copy button of the Pair page goes through the same clipboard, so it shares whatever the cause is.
- **Toolkit moved from 0.5.0-rc.13 to 0.5.2 (the stable release of 2026-11-11), pinned**, as the owner chose (not `main`: an unpinned branch changes under every build, and the lock would guarantee nothing). `freya`, `freya-core`, `freya-winit`, `freya-android` and `freya-testing` are `=0.5.2`. The two vendored toolkit crates were taken again from 0.5.2 and every local change was re-applied as a patch of the old crate against the new one: all hunks applied with offsets only (`label.rs`, `data.rs`, `accessibility/tree.rs`, `tree.rs`, `lib.rs` of the core; `input.rs` of the components, with the role, the re-press hook, the edit menu and the right click), and the `paste`→`pastey` alias was put in both manifests again (0.5.2 had gone back to `paste`; the lock has no `paste` crate). The Skia bindings are unchanged (0.104.0) and still ours. Evidence: lint of `peersus-ui` and `peersus-app` clean; tests of `peersus-core`, `-app`, `-ui` pass (679, 664 and the rest), including the layout regression of the core patch. 0.5.2 has a clipboard for Android of its own, which the Android entry's provider still overrides; the Wayland path is unchanged. Not done at the time of writing: the strict advisory gate, the Android adapter's behaviour on a phone, the desktop window on a screen.
- **The recovery screen, as the owner worded it.** Nothing may block a person: a keyring prompt that was cancelled or failed is an ordinary outcome, not an end. The screen that says the application could not start now always has **Request unlock** on the desktop (it starts the program afresh, so the owner asks the system for the keyring again and its password dialog comes up again), whatever the reason was; when the reason is the identity it also has **Generate a new identity**, whose second step says that the previous identity is deleted and the pairings are set aside before *Yes, delete the previous identity and generate a new one* is offered. These replace *Try again*, *Start over with a new identity* and *Yes, start over* (the catalogue of translations follows). `identity::system_entry` no longer refuses when the unlock before it fails: listing, choosing and resetting go on and say plainly if the keyring still will not answer. Not driven on a screen.
- **A likely cause of "Cannot access system keyring" while the password dialog was open (not proven).** The connection to the Secret Service was built with a method timeout of 5 s, and the prompt was waited for longer than that: a call still waiting for the person to type could end as an inaccessible keyring. The connection now uses the same limit as the prompt (`identity_unlock::PROMPT_TIMEOUT`, 60 s). The specification was read again for this (a prompt is shown by the service, the client calls `Prompt()`, and `Completed` carries *dismissed*): a dismissed prompt is the person's choice and is treated as one. Whether this was the owner's failure is not shown: it has not been reproduced with the new build.
- **The program the owner runs is the release one.** Several fixes of this entry were built only as the development program (`debug/`), while the owner starts the optimized one (`release/`, `cargo xtask build`): what he saw was the older program. A change is not on his screen until that program is built again and restarted.
- **The Copy button of the Pair page was taken out again.** It was not asked for; the owner removed it. The line above that says the code can be copied with a button no longer holds.
- **Scrolling a page in Settings: two causes, one of them this project's own patch (E-0xx, "Focused nodes are revealed entirely"); seven guesses before were wrong.** The owner's report, on the ROG and the NX: in the Settings page, where the extensions' device lists are, a drag moves the page a pixel and stops, the page flickers up and down with the bar fixed, then jumps to the top or the bottom; a plain text part scrolls well; it was right in toolkit 0.5.0-rc.13. **Found with a log** read from `logcat` while the owner dragged (the scroll view and the controller logged every touch decision; any move of the position over 200 px logged the call): 410 of 420 wheel events the scroll view received came while a finger was down, all of source `Custom` (the accessibility "reveal"), 153 of 189 big jumps of the position came within a millisecond after one (`-6986 → -6714` after `dy=272`: the position plus the delta), and 255 of the wheels were of zero, which make the scroll view set the position it drew last and so undo the move of the drag since the last frame. **Cause 1, this project's patch:** the scroll view asks for the focus on every move of a drag; every request ran `scroll_to` in `freya-core`; the rule revealed a node unless it was *entirely* visible; the Settings panel is partly under the bottom bar, so it never was, and each move sent it a wheel of the hidden part. **Fix** (`freya-core` `accessibility/tree.rs`, `PATCHES.md`): a focused node that is itself a scroll view reveals nothing; a reveal of zero is not sent; the entire-reveal rule applies only to text fields (the keyboard covers those), any other node scrolls its ancestors only when completely out of view, as upstream does (with no finger down, 10 such wheels came 10 ms after a lift and replaced the fling by a small move: "the page stops as if at its end"). **Cause 2, toolkit 0.5.2:** #2528 (11 November) polls the runner once per buffer of input events instead of after each; the scroll views set the page from the position drawn at the last render, so several touch moves in one buffer (frames of 24 ms on the ROG) all started from one stale position. Reverted locally in `vendor/freya-winit` (`PATCHES.md`); it was tried alone first and showed no difference only because cause 1 hid it; with both the owner reported no flicker and no blocking, and 94 % of 1479 steps of a drag followed the finger in the log. The maintainer's reasoning for #2528 (a high-DPI desktop mouse) is for the desktop; the revert is per input event, not limited to touch. **Also fixed** (`freya-components` `use_scroll_controller.rs`, `PATCHES.md`): a touch lift after a pause threw the page (630 px at the lift after 0.8 s of rest): the touch release has the check the touchpad path had. **Wrong turns, kept so they are not repeated:** the scroll views' drag math, the velocity of a drag, the position read when an event is handled, the touch moves merged in `freya-core`, the elements of the extension pages stopping the press (the log shows none), and the nested rows of buttons taking the drag. `adb shell input swipe` floods the application and is no evidence of how a finger feels; the log was. Measured on the instrumented builds; the clean build was judged by the owner by hand. Open: whether the page still stops at the first fling into the device zone after the narrowing above (to be seen).
- **Scrolling in Settings, the cause that remained: two scroll views dragged by one touch. This corrects the entry above, which lists "the nested rows of buttons taking the drag" among the wrong turns: it is the cause of the page that sticks and jumps over the device lists.** After the fixes above the owner still reported the page held in place over the extensions' device lists, then thrown ("8 > 8.10 > 8.70"). **Found with a log** from a diagnostic build on the ROG while the owner dragged (every touch decision of every scroll view logged with its viewport and position): each row of a device list is a horizontal scroll view (`plugin_controls.rs`) inside the page's vertical one. A touch on a row lands on both (`pointerdown` bubbles), both start a drag, both listen to `CaptureGlobalPointerMove`, and the first to handle a movement calls `prevent_default()`, which cancels that event for the other (`EventName::get_cancellable_events` contains the event itself); the order between capture listeners is not defined. In one touch held nine seconds on a row: the row handled 525 of 551 movements and stayed at position 0 (it has nothing to scroll); the page handled 26, about one a second, and at each moved by all the finger had travelled since its previous one (`-6891 → -6675` when the finger went from 337 to 553). Touches that began outside a row moved the page one to one. No reveal and no wheel event came in that log, so the accessibility cause above was not at work here. The same toolkit code is in 0.5.0-rc.13; why the page scrolled there was not established (the order of the listeners is the likely difference; not verified). **Fix** (`vendor/freya-components`, `src/scrollviews/touch_drag.rs`, both scroll views, `PATCHES.md`): the scroll views of a window agree on one of them per touch: each one under the touch registers at `pointerdown` (innermost first) with the axes it can scroll, and when the touch has moved 2 px the drag is given to the innermost that scrolls along the axis the touch moved on most; only that one follows, cancels and takes the lift. A scroll view with nothing to scroll no longer swallows a child's press when the finger slides. Not done: passing a drag on to the parent when the inner view reaches its end. **Verified:** `tests/scrollview_nested.rs` run through `peersus-ui` (`-- toolkit_nested_scroll`): against the toolkit's unchanged scroll views the page stays at 0 at the first movement of a touch on a nested row and the button press is lost (2 of 5 fail); with the change 5 of 5 pass, for both `ScrollView` and `VirtualScrollView`, and the toolkit's own scroll view tests pass. The diagnostic lines are removed from all three files. **Judged by the owner** on 2026-11-12, after the build without diagnostics was installed and compared byte for byte on the ROG and the NX: scrolling works and he found no scroll fault; which of the two phones he tried was not stated. Not rebuilt for the desktop.
- **macOS guest updated to this tree (toolkit 0.5.2, the recovery screen, the fixes above); its window was NOT seen.** Sources synced (348 files), release build in the guest: exit 0 in 31 m 31 s (9 m 36 s the time before; see the disk note below), bundle staged with `stage-macos.py --environment FREYA_RENDERER=software`, the copy in `/Applications` replaced and compared with the staged one file by file (no difference), `codesign --verify --deep` passed, the Accessibility grant reset for the bundle (`tccutil`), and the application opened. **What happened next is the case this entry is about, on a Mac:** the guest's session was locked and its display asleep when the application was opened (the system's own session record said *screen is locked*; no input for 17 minutes; display sleep is 10 minutes there), and macOS shows no keychain dialog in a locked session, so the owner process ended within seconds with *Identity unavailable: Cannot read system identity; existing credentials were not changed* (`owner.log`), and nothing was changed (`peers.json` untouched). The window program stayed up, idle, and the window server lists its window (1100 by 720, on screen). Whether that window shows the recovery screen with *Request unlock* and *Generate a new identity* was **not seen**: unlocking the guest takes its password at its screen, which is the owner's to type. The rebuilt bundle has a new ad hoc signature, so the keychain will ask to allow it once and Accessibility must be granted again.
- **Why the guests crawl: one hard disk.** The project folder, the build's target directory and both guests' disk files are on the same rotational SATA disk, 96 % full. Measured with both guests running: the device busy 99 % of the time at about 80 reads a second (28 ms average wait), tasks on the workstation stalled on disk 20 to 38 % of the time; the Windows guest, started a minute before the build in the macOS guest, was the larger reader. Seen because of it: the macOS guest took about ten minutes to start while a release build ran on the workstation and looked hung at the logo with an idle processor (it was not: its disk counters were moving); a program not yet in memory took about 20 s to start inside the guest; `cargo` there stayed over ten minutes at *Downloading crates* with a working network. Nothing was reset and no priority was changed. A guest that looks stuck is to be judged by its disk counters (`virsh domblkstat`) and the workstation's `/proc/pressure/io`, not by its screen.
- **Starting a guest and seeing it** (`vms/`, not tracked): `vms/macos.sh` and `vms/windows.sh` start the guest if needed and open its console window; `vms/vm` holds the one implementation and lists both. They start the libvirt domains that already describe the machines, not a second QEMU command line. Run one after the other, both windows opened; started in the same instant, the first request was lost once.
- **The nested-scroll fix upstream, and one rule added here (2026-11-13).** The fix was proposed as marc2332/freya#2536 on 2026-11-12. The maintainer closed it the next day and reopened the same commit, attribution kept, as #2538 with three commits of his own (the scroll view is identified by its accessibility id, `press` takes the sizes, the tests are one table): the same rules in less code. #2538 is open, not merged, and #2467 (a descendant taking a touch over from the scroll views) is stacked on it. **The vendored copy now is #2538 at `43b1fb88` plus one rule that is not upstream:** in #2538, as in what was proposed, a scroll view that overflows takes the drag also when it is already at the end the touch pushes towards, so a list inside a page (the transfer list with a fixed height, the relay list above eight relays, the language and appearance pickers) holds a touch it cannot use and the page cannot be moved from on top of it. Here each scroll view registers how far it can still go each way, and the drag goes to the innermost one that can still scroll the way the touch moves; when none can, #2538's rules apply. The owner is still chosen once per touch. **Verified by test only:** in upstream's workspace the added row fails on #2538 (the page stays at -200) and all 136 `freya-components` tests pass with the rule; in this workspace `-- toolkit_ headless::` passes (29 tests). **Not judged on a phone** when this was written, and not proposed upstream.
- **The rule above, on the phone and upstream (2026-11-13).** The build with #2538 and the added rule was installed and compared byte for byte on the ROG; the owner tried it there and reported scrolling smooth with no fault. Which nested lists he tried was not stated. The NX did not get this build: it was not reachable over adb. The rule was then offered to the maintainer as a patch inside a comment on #2538, with the three added test rows; it was not pushed anywhere, and there was no answer when this was written.

### E-049 — P1 — A cleaned /tmp made every extension deaf: the pointer captured and frozen, a paired device uncontrollable, and nothing could take the input back — FIXED; verified by review, tests and inspection; the live fault reproduced as a deletion, not as a blocked pointer

- **Reported by the owner** on 2026-11-14: the pointer on the desktop froze for minutes, and no device of his — the local mouse included, nor a controlling one over the network — could move it. MSI could not control any device either.
- **What was seen in the log and on the machine** (`owner.log`, `peersus_ui`, `peersus` running since 2026-11-12, `/proc`, `ss`, `journalctl`): from 00:08 UTC the clipboard extension's guest refused every data stream, from 01:31 the control extension's did too — `stream-open outcome="refused"`, `elapsed_ms=0` — and every `ForwardInput` call failed with *"A plugin could not complete the action"*. The pointer crossed a barrier at 03:31 UTC and the InputCapture session kept it until 03:44, while the forwarded input went nowhere. `/tmp/peersus-1AVPMz/` — the directory the extensions' socket files live in — was **gone**, though its listeners still showed LISTEN in `ss` (live descriptors on deleted files). The owner had emptied `/tmp` by hand. A listener keeps its descriptor, so its control link survived and the extension looked alive; but a Unix socket can only be *connected to* through its path, so every `data_open` failed instantly and permanently.
- **Two faults, one trigger.** (1) **Socket files under `/tmp` with no repair.** `bin/peersus.rs` put the extension sockets in `tempfile::tempdir()`; deleting the directory broke every extension's data path for the rest of its life, with no detection and no recovery. (2) **A forward failure never gave the pointer back.** `pass_through.rs` queued into `outgoing` and logged the failure, but the `visit` stayed, the capture stayed, and the XDG InputCapture portal kept diverting the whole seat's pointer input to a link that was down — hence the frozen, unresponsive desktop. `desktop_capture.rs::session` only releases on `Order::Release`, `Deactivated`, or stream end; none arrived.
- **Changes.** (a) `bin/peersus.rs`: the sockets now live in `directory.join("run")`, beside `peers.json` and `grants.json`, not in `/tmp`; a cleanup of the temporary directory can no longer orphan them. (b) `peersus-plugin/src/transport.rs::Listener::bind` now creates the socket's parent directory when it is missing, so a bind can recover instead of failing. (c) `peersus-plugin/src/service.rs`: the directory is restored before every bind (`restore_socket_directory`, mode 700), and a 5-second watcher checks both socket paths of a running extension; a missing file triggers `create_dir_all` + `set_permissions` and a rebind of that listener at the same path. The rebound listener **replaces** the old one with `mem::replace` + `mem::forget`, because `Listener`'s `Drop` would unlink the new file (one forgotten descriptor per repair, harmless). The check is `#[cfg(unix)]`: on Windows the endpoint is a loopback port, there is no file to lose, and probing `exists()` on a `host:port` string would rebind every tick. Rebinds happen only when the file is really gone, not on the watcher's suspicion. (d) `peersus-app/src/pass_through.rs`: the forwarder sends the failed peer id back over a `dead` channel; the `follow` select releases the capture at the visit's `from` point, clears the visit, and marks the device `broken` for ten seconds. A barrier entered while its device is marked broken releases the pointer at the entry point instead of capturing it, so a dead device cannot take the pointer again on the next guard window; the mark is lifted after the guard or cleared on route change.
- **Evidence.** `cargo check -p peersus-plugin -p peersus-app`: clean. `cargo test -p peersus-plugin`: 101 passed, 0 failed. The fault was reproduced as the deletion of a live extension's socket directory — the same sequence the log showed — and the repair path was read end to end: `watcher → restore → rebind at the same path → the plugin's `data_open` succeeds on its next try`.
- **Not verified.** The blocked-pointer path itself was not re-driven end to end on a live desktop (it needs a barrier crossing while a device is unreachable); the release-on-failure and broken-guard were verified by reading, not by a frozen pointer. Whether a device that comes back while marked broken then serves again was not tried.

### E-050 — P1 — The pointer stayed captured when a forwarded input failed late; neither desktop could be controlled — FIXED IN CODE; tests and lint pass; installed on both desktops (17:18, byte for byte); NOT observed there

- **Reported by the owner** on 2026-11-14, again after E-049: no control on the workstation nor on the second desktop, repeatedly.
- **What the logs say** (`owner.log` of both, the window 14:57 to 15:01 UTC). Workstation: *"That input was not understood"* four times, *"A plugin did not complete the action in time"* twice (14:58:05.522, 14:59:32.631). Second desktop: *"A transfer to a device was interrupted"* at 14:58:05.538, 16 ms after the first of those, and *"A transfer to a device stopped making progress"* at 14:59:49.997. The second desktop's two lines are about a stream **it** was sending: it was forwarding input to the workstation in the same minutes. Whether that was its own mouse pushed across the edge or something else is **not known**; what it carried is not in the log.
- **Four faults in the capture follower and the queue behind it**, each read in the code. (1) `runtime.rs::forward_input` answers "accepted" when the batch is queued, long before the extension answers; a failure or the 5 s limit that comes later was only said as a notice. The follower never heard of it, so E-049's release never ran for it and the pointer stayed captured. (2) The dead-device branch dropped the visit even when the dead device was another one, without releasing. (3) A capture entered at a barrier with no route kept the pointer. (4) Key codes the input contract refuses (`0x100..0x160`, mouse buttons that arrive as keys) were forwarded, so the whole batch was refused with *"That input was not understood"*.
- **Changes.** `surfaces.rs`: `failed(id)` drops what waited behind a failed call and keeps only an end, so nothing old is replayed late. `runtime.rs`: a failed surface call is also said on a broadcast (`Client::surface_failures`). `pass_through.rs`: the follower listens to it and gives the pointer back; `take_if` instead of `take().filter`; an entry with no route releases; `passed()` checks each input against the contract before it is forwarded. `ipc.rs` and the demo client carry a sender nothing listens to.
- **Evidence.** `cargo test -p peersus-app --lib -- surfaces:: pass_through::`: 14 passed, among them `a_failed_call_drops_what_waited_behind_it_and_keeps_only_the_end` and `a_captured_input_the_contract_refuses_is_not_passed_on`. `cargo clippy -p peersus-app -p peersus-plugin -p peersus-ui --lib -- -D warnings`: clean (two nested conditions of E-049's rebind code in `service.rs` were collapsed for it; no behaviour change).
- **Found while reading**, and taken up in E-054 except (b). (a) The receiving extension lets go of nothing when a stream ends with *continues* and no stream follows; the idle watch never takes a held pointer away (`desktop_input.rs::hide_idle`), and keys held on the shared keyboard have no watch at all. Deduced from the code, not reproduced. (b) An open stream on which nothing moves for 30 s is ended by both hosts (`pump::IDLE_LIMIT`); an idle visit closes its stream after 400 ms, so this only happens while a button or key is held. Open. (c) The sending extension's `close` waits for the acknowledgement with no limit of its own. (d) The guest has no clock; a time limit has to be the host's.
- **Installed.** The release build of 17:12 was staged and put in place of the five programs on both desktops (the earlier ones kept in a backup folder), each compared with the staged one; both applications were started and their extensions answer. On the second desktop `peersus stop` left the old window running with its owner ended; that window was closed by hand.
- **Not verified.** The pointer was not captured and given back by a real failure on either desktop. Why the stream between the two desktops fails in the first place is not known.

### E-051 — Old messages said again by a window that is drawn again — FIXED IN CODE; one test; in the release installed on both desktops on 2026-11-15 and in the Android package built that day (not on the phones); seen in no window

- The owner keeps its last few messages for as long as it runs. A window that opened later, or was drawn again after the connection dropped or the app was left and entered again, showed them all as new.
- `view.rs`: `notices_already_said` (what a window only now drawn takes as said: the owner's own messages; what follows them is how things are, and is said) and `fresh_notices` (new texts, and the newest of the owner's own as many times as its count moved). `peersus-ui/src/lib.rs` uses both. One test holds the rule, the first look and the repeated sentence included. Seen in no window.

### E-052 — The control page with its device offline — FIXED IN CODE (extension); in the package installed on both desktops on 2026-11-15 (E-061 tells how); NOT on the phones, whose page it is

- A device that is not there is how things are, not a failure of each touch: the page shows it, nothing is sent, what was held is let go, and the choice is kept for when the device is back (`ui.rs::reachable`). A failure now carries a code beside its fixed words (`remote.rs::code`: `cancelled`, `unavailable`, …), so the person is told what is missing.
- The four devices still ran the packages of 9 and 12 November when this was written. **Since (2026-11-15):** packaged with `scripts/package-native-extension.py` and installed on both desktops (E-061). The rule "the choice is kept for when the device is back" was replaced there at the owner's request: a chosen device that stops being controllable is unselected and the page says so.

### E-053 — A structure card in the README, drawn by a workflow that never writes `main` — WRITTEN; NOT run (it runs on a push to `main`)

- `.github/workflows/codeflow-card.yml`: a third-party static analyzer (`braedonsaunders/codeflow`, MIT, pinned to commit `b70af01c`) reads the tree on the runner, the vendored toolkit and lock files left out. The picture and its state file are pushed as one replaced commit to the branch `codeflow-card`, which holds nothing else; the README shows it by address. The analyzer's own action was not used because it commits into the branch it analyzes. No checkout keeps credentials; the token reaches the publishing step alone.
- Expected first reading, computed by hand from the analyzer's formula: 67, grade D, most of it from file size (inline test modules) and connections per file. No test was moved for the number.

### E-054 — Three limits a person can rely on: what a silent controller holds is let go, a closed connection is not waited for long, and two keys always bring the pointer back — IN CODE; tests and lint pass; the two host changes installed on both desktops (17:31, byte for byte), the extension's not; NOT observed

- **What a controller gone silent still holds is let go** (`desktop_input.rs`). The host notes when each controller last sent input; after `HELD_LIMIT` (thirty seconds, the time a silent connection is given, so a press that is really held ends no sooner than it did) the idle watch lifts its own pointer's buttons and releases its keys and buttons on the shared keyboard and mouse. Before, a held pointer was never touched and the shared keyboard had no watch at all, so a key pressed by a device that then went away stayed down. The watch now starts with any replayed input, not only with a pointer of the device's own. `rest` and `release` had the same body: one `release` and one `let_go` are left. Test: `what_a_controller_gone_silent_still_holds_is_let_go_after_the_limit_and_only_its_own`.
- **A closed connection's acknowledgement is waited for three seconds, not thirty** (control extension, `remote.rs`, `ACK_MS`). The guest is one thread that also draws its pages and replays other devices' input; it now asks whether the acknowledgement can be read (`poll`) before reading it, keeps the deliveries that arrive meanwhile as a call does (`keep`, shared with `call`), and counts the delivery as not made when nothing comes. Test: `a_peer_that_never_acknowledges_is_given_up_after_a_bounded_wait_and_what_arrived_is_kept`. A link on which an acknowledgement takes longer than three seconds now reports a failure where it used to stand still.
- **Both Control keys held together give the pointer back** (`desktop_capture.rs`, `WayOut`). The capture session releases the pointer itself, where the desktop holds it, and tells the follower as when the desktop takes it back on its own; nothing is asked of the visited device, its extension or the network. The second of the two keys is not forwarded. Chosen because every other key or pair is something a person types on the other device. Test: `both_control_keys_together_ask_for_the_pointer_back_and_nothing_less_does`. Described in `docs/architecture.md`.
- **Evidence.** `cargo test -p peersus-app --lib` with all of it in the tree: 686 passed, none failed, 8 ignored; `cargo clippy -p peersus-app --all-targets -- -D warnings`: clean. Extension: 60 tests passed, lint clean, the guest compiles for `wasm32-unknown-unknown`. The whole gate (`./check.sh`) was not run.
- **Installed.** The release build of 17:30 replaced the five programs on both desktops as in E-050, the ones of 17:12 kept in a second backup folder; both applications started and their extensions answer. The Android package was built (17:30) and installed on neither phone: `adb` sees both phones announce themselves and cannot connect to either.
- **Seen while installing.** `peersus stop` ends the owner and leaves the window running with the owner as a child it does not collect, on both desktops; a new start then needs that window closed first. Not looked into.
- **Idle cost of the build of 17:30, sixty seconds on each desktop with nothing else building, for a later comparison** (from `/proc`: CPU time of the process, context switches of all its threads, resident memory). Workstation: owner 0.50 % of a core, 62 wake-ups a second, 37 MiB, 14 threads; window 0.12 %, 18 a second, 98 MiB; each of three interpreters 0 %, none, 6 to 7 MiB. Second desktop: owner 0.42 %, 36 a second, 38 MiB, 22 threads; window 0.15 %, 18 a second, 92 MiB; each of two interpreters 0 %, none, 6 MiB. Which page each window showed was not noted, and what wakes the owner that often when nothing happens is not known.
- **Not verified.** None of the three was seen on a device. The key codes of the two Control keys are the Linux ones the capture already forwards (29, 97); that the desktop reports exactly these was not watched. The extension is not packaged or installed: the installer refuses a different package under an installed id, so an update means removing the installed one first, which is the owner's decision.

### E-055 — No module of a crate needs another that needs it back; one error path where there were two; the count is in the gate — IN CODE; the lint gate and the tests named below pass; NOT installed, the Android part NOT compiled

- **What was there.** Counted without comments and test modules (the first count of the day was wrong, see A-15): three pairs and three groups. `peersus-app` `desktop_capture` ↔ `pass_through`, and ten modules around `runtime`; `peersus-net` `reconnect` ↔ `transport`; `peersus-ui-android` `bridge` ↔ `extension_clipboard`; `peersus-core` `config`, `model`, `nearby`, `private_store`, `relay`; `peersus-ui` nine modules around `view`. Each group hung on one to three single references, not on its size.
- **The capture and the geometry** (`input_follow.rs`, new, Linux only). `follow` and `passed` drove the capture from inside `pass_through.rs`, the module whose first lines call it pure, and the capture took `Barrier` from it. The driver is now its own module that needs both; `pass_through.rs` needs neither and is what it says. Moved whole, its test with it; nothing in it changed.
- **The endpoint and the reconnect protocol** (`transport.rs`). The endpoint named `reconnect::ALPN` to admit it, and `reconnect` is built on the endpoint. The name is the endpoint's now (`REMEMBERED_ALPN`), and `reconnect::ALPN` is that same value.
- **Android: the activity and the clipboard.** `bridge::remember_activity` announced a clipboard change; the announcement is made by its one caller, `android_main`, when it succeeds. Same moment, same effect. **Not compiled:** the gate does not build the Android adapter and no Android build was made after it.
- **The model and what it carries** (`peersus-core`). `nearby` and `private_store` needed the model for one small type each, and the model needs them. `PeerId` is in `peer.rs` and `SecretText` in `secret.rs` (both new), given out again by `model` under the paths every caller uses. The relay address rule (`valid_relay_url`, `MAX_RELAY_URL_BYTES`) moved from `config.rs` to `relay.rs`, which was asking `config` for it while `config` is built on `relay`; its three callers outside the crate name the new place.
- **The ten around `runtime`: two references.** The demo client took the size of its request channel from `ipc` (16) while the real one wrote 64: both use one constant of the runtime, `REQUESTS_WAITING`. The registry's `info()` asked the runtime what each plugin can be granted; it is now given that rule by its caller, as the other things only the owner knows already are.
- **The nine around `view`: one function.** `view::search_settings` called the settings search, which is built on the view. Nothing but the view's own tests called it; it is a helper of those tests now.
- **One error path.** `Runtime::error` and `plugin_error` each counted, compared with the last and kept sixteen. Both call `say_error`; each keeps its own line in the log.
- **Kept from coming back.** `scripts/module-cycles.py` is the count (a module is a file or folder directly under `src/`; it follows `crate::name`, `use crate::{name, ..}` and, from a top file, `super::name`, outside comments and test modules; it does not follow deeper `super::super::` paths or names reached only through a re-export, so "none" means none found this way). It ends with status 1 on any group and is a step of the `metadata` gate. It reports none in all twelve crates.
- **Verified.** `cargo xtask clippy` (the gate's lint, warnings denied): exit 0 with everything above. `cargo xtask test`: exit 0 in 1174 s with the three pairs and the core moves in the tree. After the last three cuts and the error path: the library tests of `peersus-core` (125), `peersus-app` (686) and `peersus-ui` with its headless feature (702), none failed. The whole gate then passed with all of it (see Gate status); a first run of it stopped at once on a generated `README.md` that was out of date, which `scripts/gen-docs.py` made current.
- **Measured for a later comparison.** `./dev.sh bench` kept as `benchmark-results/before-refactor-20261114.json` (431 s; it measures the window drawing, the replay of states and the transport, not the path of captured input).
- **What wakes an idle owner: narrowed, not found.** A throwaway owner alone (the gate's test build, an identity of its own, its own data folder, no window, device or extension) wakes 9.5 times a second over thirty seconds. The installed one, with a window, three devices online and three extensions, woke 62 times a second in one minute and about 30 in a later forty seconds, unevenly, with a burst about every twenty seconds; every wake-up is on the runtime's worker threads. Its state did not change once in twenty seconds, so it is not the window being told anything. What comes with devices, a window and extensions is therefore most of it; which of them is not known. The installed owner cannot be traced without a setting of the system that is the owner's to change, and a trace doubles the count it reads (91 for the owner that wakes 9.5).
- **Seen on the way.** The installed owner's log file is 235 MB; nothing bounds it.

### E-056 — A pointer that is not kept is let go in from the edge, never on it; a desktop a device is driving does not take a pointer at its edges — IN CODE; tests and lint pass; installed on both desktops (18:47, byte for byte); NOT observed

- **What the owner saw (2026-11-14, 16:23Z).** On the workstation the pointer was held on the strip of the left edge, toward the second desktop: it slid up and down and went nowhere else, and it was not on the second desktop. Restarting the control extension on both desktops did not free it; the extension was then turned off on both (`disable-plugin control`, 16:31Z). The second desktop's window read "Peersus stopped" while its owner process, started at 17:32 local, was alive and answering commands: not explained.
- **What the logs hold.** The installed build logs warnings only. The workstation: at 16:23:06.105Z "That input was not understood" and 0.6 ms later "A plugin could not complete the action", both for the control extension; the same pair at 16:10:56Z. The second desktop: its own "did not complete the action in time" at 15:58:26Z, 16:11:32Z and 16:30:34Z. The first sentence has two sources in the owner, a forwarded batch that is refused and a page event that is refused (`runtime.rs`); which one it was, and why, is **not known**.
- **What the code shows.** When the pointer comes back by being pushed through the edge it is let go 24 to 96 pixels in (`RETURN_DEPTH`), and the comment on that rule says why: let go nearer, a hand still moving or a pixel of jitter pushes it through the barrier again, "which would hold it there, only sliding up and down". Every other way of giving it back ignored the rule and let it go at the point the desktop had stopped it, which is on the barrier: the visited device could not be reached, the device is marked broken for ten seconds, the layout changed during the visit, the barrier leads nowhere. A failed forward followed by ten seconds of a broken mark is therefore the trap the comment describes. That this is what happened at 16:23Z is **an inference** from the two failures in the log and the owner's words; it was not watched.
- **Changed: one rule for every give-back** (`pass_through.rs` `let_go`, `input_follow.rs`). A visit's return point is the least depth in from where the pointer left, and the two places that gave it back at the cursor use the same function. A give-back caused by a device that could not be reached is now one warning in the log, with no device named, so the next such report has a line to go by.
- **Changed: a driven desktop does not capture** (`desktop_input.rs` `replayed_within`, `input_follow.rs` `DRIVEN_GUARD`). Input a device sends is replayed through a device of the kernel, which the desktop cannot tell from a mouse, so a pointer driven from another computer can reach this desktop's own barrier and be taken and carried back to where it came from, each desktop then holding the other's pointer (A-list: the mutual capture; the second desktop had a stream open toward the workstation during the lockout of 14:58Z). A pointer that reaches a barrier within one second of input replayed here is let go, in from the edge, and no visit begins. Any replayed input counts, keys too: for that second the person at this desktop, if there is one, pushes again. That the second desktop did capture a driven pointer today was **not shown**.
- **Removed on the way.** Three direct dependencies no source names: `thiserror` in `peersus-plugin`, `serde` and `serde_json` in `peersus-clipboard-platform`.
- **Verified.** The library tests of `peersus-app` (688, none failed), the gate's lint (exit 0), the module count (none). The release build was installed on both desktops at 18:47 local and compared byte for byte with what was built; both started with the debug lines of the follower on (`PEERSUS_LOG=peersus_app::input_follow=debug`, for this run only), and the control extension was turned on again on both.
- **Not verified.** Nothing of this was seen on a desktop. The cause of the refused input stays open. The whole gate was not run after these changes.
- **Superseded by E-057 (2026-11-15).** The owner was held on the edge again at 23:05Z with this build. Letting the pointer go 24 pixels in did not help, because the desktop does not put it there; the guard that gave a driven pointer back the moment it was taken, and the one for a pointer taken again at once, were themselves the giving-back-at-once that feeds the loop, and are gone. What stays from this entry: `let_go`, the warning, the removed dependencies.

### E-057 — The pointer held on the edge: reproduced, what the desktop does, and what is done about it — IN CODE; the tests named below pass; three crossings seen in the traces of both desktops with the development build (00:09Z); the release build of this tree is on both desktops since 2026-11-15 02:34; NOT yet confirmed by the owner's hand

- **Reproduced without the owner.** A throwaway mouse made through the kernel (`uinput`, a script outside the tree) moves the workstation's real pointer; a second script reads every input device, so it is known what moved and what did not; the development owner writes every step. With the control extension on, three runs (23:16Z, 23:20Z, 23:33Z) showed the same thing each time.
- **What the desktop does (Mutter 51.1), as seen.** After **every** `Release`, the one after a failure and the ordinary one of a pointer pushed back alike, the desktop says the pointer is taken again 10 to 17 ms later, at the point where it was **first** stopped (the same `x = 0`, `y = 555.2310180664063` 231 times in 20 s, while the place asked for was 25 pixels in and at other heights). So the place given with the release is not where the pointer is; it stays on the barrier, and its next motion in any direction is a hit: the capture's barriers hold a pointer that starts on them whichever way it moves. In one run this went on at about a hundred a second for 80 s with **no motion on any input device** and nothing replayed here. The same is reported by another program that shares a mouse through this portal (deskflow issues 10226 "Release cursor_position hint appears not to be applied" and 10248, GNOME 50). Why Mutter does it was **not found** in its source (51.1 was read: `handle_release` releases the barriers, deactivates, then warps); it is not reported upstream yet.
- **A second fault of the desktop, read in its source.** `meta_input_capture_session_process_event` passes on the amounts of a motion from variables it never sets when the motion has no relative part (a pointer the desktop itself placed), and ignores whether there was one. Such a motion reaches the capture as anything: a number no hand makes, or not a number. One of them made the visited pointer's place not a number; the position sent next was refused ("That input was not understood", one input, a position: the line of 16:10Z, 16:23Z and 23:05Z), the device was marked broken, and before the fail-safes of 2026-11-14 every later motion went nowhere with the pointer taken for good.
- **Two faults of ours, read in the code.** (1) A capture session was left open whenever anything failed after it was opened (`?` returned past the one `close`). The desktop keeps the barriers of an open session and holds a pointer that reaches one with nobody to let it go, until the owner process ends: turning the extension off does not end it, which fits the owner's "restarting it did not free it". (2) The word that a taking ended was applied to whichever taking was going on; read after the next one had begun (two streams, read in either order), it cleared the new one, and the desktop went on holding the pointer.
- **Changed: the session** (`desktop_capture.rs`). `session` opens, calls `serve`, and closes whatever `serve` returned. A session lost after it was enabled is made again after five seconds (`Ended::Lost`, one warning), where before it waited for the barriers to change. A deactivation is applied only to the activation it names. A motion whose amounts are not usable numbers is dropped where captured input is translated (`motion`). Three traces for a development build: the pointer taken (where, which activation), given back (where asked), let go by the desktop.
- **Changed: no barrier where the pointer cannot be carried** (`input_follow.rs` `open_edges`). The follower no longer takes a pointer and gives it back at once as a way of refusing. An edge that cannot be entered has no barrier: every edge for 350 ms after any give-back (`COOL`, `give_back`), so that nothing is there to hold a pointer the desktop left on the edge and the hand coming back carries it off; the edges toward a device that could not be reached, for ten seconds; every edge while a device drives this desktop and for two seconds after (`desktop_input::replayed`, `replayed_within`), which is the mutual capture of the A-list. The barriers are set again when the reason ends, which makes a new session; that took 14 ms on the workstation. A visit whose place on the other device is lost is given back instead of kept.
- **Changed: the window closes with an owner that was told to stop** (`peersus-ui` `link.rs` `owner_ended_as_told`, `lib.rs`). The owner said "Peersus stopped" was all the second desktop showed each time the application was restarted from a terminal, and its button did nothing. `peersus stop` ended the owner and left the window on that screen with a finished process nobody had waited for. The window now keeps the owner it started; when the connection ends and that owner ended with success, the application was closed and the window ends too. An owner that ended any other way still gave the screen and its button. Both halves were replaced in E-058: the owner now says itself that it was told to stop, and one that was not is started again by the window.
- **Changed: Request unlock replaces the window in place** (`peersus-ui` `link.rs` `start_afresh`, `lib.rs`). The owner said the button seemed to do nothing on the second desktop. It asked the system for this program's path at the press and started a second process, then ended the first. Read in the code, two ways that does nothing: once the installed file has been replaced (every install here, every update) the system names the path with "(deleted)", the start fails and was not looked at, and the window ended; and a window started as a unit of the service manager takes the new process down with it. Which of the two the owner met was **not observed**. Now the program is the one recorded when the window started (the path an update already uses) and takes this process's place on Unix; when it cannot be started the window stays and says so under the button. **Seen** with the installed release build on a private X display (so the owner's screen was not touched): the file replaced under the running window (`/proc/<pid>/exe` ending in "(deleted)"), its owner ended by `KILL`, the screen "Peersus stopped", the button pressed by a synthetic click: the same process became the new program (path no longer deleted), a new owner and its two extension hosts were running 8 s later, and the window drew the Send page. **Seen** on the second desktop: `peersus stop` ended the owner and the window was gone half a second later.
- **Seen in the run of 23:45Z, development build, synthetic mouse.** One taking, one give-back, the session made again 351 ms later, **no second taking**. The second desktop: "A device drives this desktop: its edges are closed", open again two seconds after the last input. In that run the pointer was already on the edge and the taking came from its first motion **away** from it, which is the desktop's behaviour above seen once more.
- **Seen in the run of 00:09Z, development build on both desktops.** Three takings at the left edge, each followed by one "Pointer pushed back", one release and the session made again 351 ms after it; two of the visits lasted 2.8 s and 2.3 s with 657 and 429 inputs forwarded and as many replayed on the second desktop, which logged "A device drives this desktop: its edges are closed" each time; nothing refused, no taking between the crossings. The owner's own hand was on the mouse during the run as well as the synthetic one, so which motion made each crossing is not known, and "no taking without a motion" is not shown by this run. A run just before it (00:06Z) showed the workstation has a second neighbour on its right edge, and twice a taking on the first motion **away** from the left edge after a give-back: the pointer went to the other desktop for one input and was pushed back 3 ms later. That is the desktop leaving the pointer on the barrier; it costs one bounce and holds nothing.
- **Tests.** `motion` (numbers kept; not-a-number, infinite and absurd amounts dropped), `open_edges` (none while closed, none toward a broken device), `let_go`, `replayed_within`. The capture and follower modules: 44 passed.
- **Mistakes made on the way, kept so they are not made again.** A `PEERSUS_LOG` that names only one module silences every other warning (the default level is added only when the value has no directive at all): the run of 18:47 lost the owner's warnings that way; write `warn,` first. Restarting the second desktop's application with `peersus stop` and then its unit showed the owner the stopped screen each time.
- **Open.** The owner's own hand on both desktops. A release build of this tree on both. The whole gate. The two faults of Mutter reported upstream. Whether the place asked for on release is ever honoured (the pointer comes back where it left, not at the height it reached on the other device, when it is not).

### E-058 — The application starts itself again: a window whose owner ended brings it back, closes with one that was told to stop, and rejoins one that returns — IN CODE; tests pass; SEEN on both desktops with the installed release build (2026-11-15 02:35 to 02:47); the owner with no window open is NOT covered

- **What the owner asked (2026-11-15).** A person must not have to restart the application: it heals itself. Only an extension that crashed leaves the person a choice (its Restart button, which exists). And "Request unlock" on a screen that says the application stopped reads as a keyring being unlocked, which is not what is happening.
- **Changed: the owner says that it was told to stop** (`runtime.rs` `Client::told_to_stop`, `ipc.rs` `TOLD`). Every way of stopping it on purpose (`peersus stop`, a signal, its last window going) is one command through one client, which now marks that it was given. As the owner ends, it writes one byte on every window's lease when the mark is set, and nothing when it is not. The window's bridge reads its lease once the owner has gone and ends with the answer (`attach` and its two siblings return it). This replaces the exit status of the owner as a child, added the day before, which knew nothing about an owner the window had not started; that code and its test are removed.
- **Changed: a window brings a dead owner back** (`peersus-ui` `link.rs` `owner_ending`, `start_again_unasked`, `heal_budget`, `replace_this_program`; `lib.rs`). When the connection ends: an owner restarting for an update is followed as before; one that was told to stop closes the window; any other is started again by the window replacing itself with a new one, which starts the owner as every window does. At most three times in five minutes (the times are handed to the next program in its environment, and anything in it that is not such a time is ignored); after that the window says the owner stopped. Never done by a demo, a panel, a test or a screenshot run: only a window that `Session::open` attached, and not when starting an owner is forbidden.
- **Changed: two windows that both start an owner** (`bin/peersus.rs`, `link.rs` `Session::open`). Windows that heal at the same moment each start an owner and only one can be it. The other used to try to act as a command line client, wrote the whole state into the log or failed, and its window said the application could not start. An owner started for a window that finds another now ends with success and says nothing; a window whose owner ended with success goes on asking for the other one within the same ninety seconds, and one whose owner ended with a failure stops at once with the reason, as before.
- **Changed: a window left on the stopped screen rejoins an owner that is there again** (`link.rs`). Every two seconds it tries to connect where the owner listens; when something answers it replaces itself and attaches. It never starts an owner from there.
- **Changed: the button says what it does** (`lib.rs`). "Request unlock" only when the identity could not be had from the system; "Restart" otherwise. A press that cannot start the program says so under the button (it used to end the window). The sentence of the stopped screen no longer tells the person to close the window and open the application.
- **Tests.** `a_window_learns_that_its_owner_was_told_to_stop_and_not_when_it_ended_any_other_way` (a real owner and service, both endings); `a_second_owner_started_for_a_window_ends_with_success_and_says_nothing` (the real program twice); `a_window_starts_the_application_again_by_itself_only_so_many_times` (the bound, its window of time, hostile values).
- **Seen, second desktop (real session).** `peersus stop`: owner and window gone within half a second. Owner ended by `KILL`: the same window process, a new owner 3 s later with its two extension hosts.
- **Seen, workstation.** Three windows on one owner (the owner's own and two on a private X display so that they could be pictured and pressed). Owner ended by `KILL`: three owners started in the same second, one stayed, all three windows kept their process, both private windows drew the Send page. Twice more the same. The fourth time within two minutes: no owner started, both private windows showed "Peersus stopped" with the button "Restart". The button pressed on one: a new owner, that window on the Send page with its count cleared, and the two others on the Send page by themselves within nine seconds. `peersus stop` after that: the attached window ended with the owner.
- **Found on the way: the install script, not the application.** The second desktop's login shell does not split a variable into words, so the script that restarts the application there signalled only the first of "window owner": the window ended, the old owner stayed, and the new window attached to an owner of an older build. That is one way the stopped screen came up there after an install. The remote half is now run by `bash` explicitly.
- **Not covered, said plainly.** (1) An owner that dies while **no window is open** (the background option) is started by nobody until a window is opened, which starts it. Curing that needs something that outlives the owner (the session's service manager on each system, or a keeper process); it is not built and not designed yet. (2) A **window** that dies is not started again either. (3) An owner that is alive and does not answer is not detected. (4) An owner made before this change says nothing on its leases, so a new window takes its stopping for a death and starts it again; only two builds mixed during an update could show that.

### E-059 — The window carries the application's id; the metadata is filled in; a staged tree can be installed for one user — IN CODE; tests pass; the id READ from the installed window on Wayland and on X11; the dock was not seen by me

- **What the owner saw (2026-11-15).** The dock showed the window with a generic icon under the name "Unknown", while the application list had the icon; and a monitor of his showed no publisher for it.
- **Cause, read in the code.** The window announced no application id, so the desktop could not match it to the installed entry, which is where the name and the icon come from.
- **Changed.** `peersus_ui::APPLICATION_ID` given to the main window (the Wayland application id and the X11 class are the same field in the toolkit's window library); `StartupWMClass`, `GenericName` and `Keywords` in the desktop entry; a test that holds the id, the entry's file name, its `StartupWMClass` and its `Icon` together. The AppStream file now has a description, categories, keywords, the two programs it provides, colours, the controls it supports and a release note; a relation that said the application never uses the internet was written and removed the same hour. `scripts/install-linux-user.py` puts a staged tree in a user's prefix (programs replaced in one step, the entry's `Exec` rewritten to the installed window, nothing started or stopped), with `scripts/test-install-linux-user.py` in the `metadata` step.
- **Verified.** The id test; the validators of the `metadata` step (exit 0); the install test (4 passed); installed on both desktops with the script and the checksums compared. With the installed build on a private headless compositor: `xdg_toplevel.set_app_id("io.github.DarkPhilosophy.Peersus")`; on a private X display: `WM_CLASS` the same id twice.
- **Not done.** Metadata apart for each program (the window, the owner, the extension hosts), which the owner asked for afterwards. What his monitor reads a publisher from was not looked at.

### E-060 — A device's own pointer is drawn by asking the session which display is its own, not only what the owner was started with — IN CODE; tests pass; SEEN with the service's own probe on the workstation; release build INSTALLED on both desktops (04:25, checksums compared) and running since 04:28

- **What happened.** On 2026-11-15 between 02:46 and 03:26 the workstation's owner had been started by a test window of mine on a private X display, and inherited its environment: no Wayland display named, an X display that was not the session's. The owner reported that controlling the workstation did nothing visible; after a restart from the desktop it worked.
- **Read in the code.** The pointer of a controlling device was drawn only when `WAYLAND_DISPLAY` was set in the owner's environment, on the X server its `DISPLAY` named. With neither right, the kernel device is made and nothing is drawn, with no error anywhere.
- **Seen, with `what_a_moving_pointer_costs_this_desktop` (the service's own code, `own`).** Before the change: the session's environment gave the device and the drawn window; a foreign environment gave the device and no window. After it: the device and the window in both.
- **Changed** (`desktop_input/cursor/session.rs`, `cursor.rs`, `desktop_input.rs`). Whether the desktop is a Wayland one: the environment, or a compositor of this user listening in the runtime directory. Which X server: the display the process was told of, then every other local one, each accepted only when it is this user's server with its own authority file, as before; then the plain connection as any program makes it. Two tests (the order of displays; a live compositor against a socket left behind).
- **A claim of mine that was wrong.** I said the fault was reproduced by driving the workstation from the second desktop with a synthetic mouse. It was not: that drive reached the workstation in none of the runs, with or without the fault, and the one run that showed a pointer device was most likely the owner's own test. The probe above is the evidence.
- **Open.** The same dependence on the inherited environment in the offer panel (`offer_panel.rs` reads `DISPLAY`) and wherever the clipboard is reached was not looked at.

### E-061 — Control lists every paired device with where it is, lets only a device that is online and allows Control be chosen, and unselects one that stops being so — IN CODE (application and extension); tests pass; packages built and validated; INSTALLED and running on both desktops; NOT on the phones; NOT seen on a phone

- **Asked by the owner (2026-11-15).** A status dot per device (green, red, grey); a device can be chosen only when it is online and every permission the extension needs there is allowed; one that is offline, sleeping, in another status or lacks a permission is dimmed and cannot be chosen; a chosen device that stops qualifying is unselected, visibly.
- **Application (`peersus.peers` `list`, for every extension).** Each device now also carries `presence` (`online`, `sleeping`, `offline`: the same words `peersus.status` uses) and `allowed` (every permission of the calling extension that is granted per device is granted for that one). `name`, `device` and `online` are as before, so an extension written for the old list reads the new one unchanged. Statuses a person sets by hand are not in the list: they stay behind `peersus.status`, which an extension must ask for. Three tests (the five fields; sleeping and offline; allowed).
- **Extension (Control, `ui.rs`).** One place decides a device's standing (`Standing::of`): ready, not allowed, sleeping, away (a presence with no word here), offline; a host that says neither new field is read as before, by `online`. The dot is green for a device that is there, red for one that is gone, grey for the rest. The open chooser lists every other device, not only the online ones; a device that cannot be controlled is dimmed, says why, and has no id, so the application sends no touch for it; `choose` refuses it as well. When the chosen device stops being ready (or is unpaired) its gesture ends, nothing is chosen, and the page says which device was unselected and why, above the list, until the person chooses again or opens the page anew.
- **Behaviour changed on purpose.** Before, a chosen device that went offline stayed chosen and control resumed when it came back; and one that was unpaired was replaced by the first online device. Now neither happens: what is controlled is only ever chosen by the person (the first ready device is still taken when the page opens with nothing chosen). The two tests that held the old behaviour were rewritten to the new one, not removed.
- **Verified.** The extension's own tests: 63 passed (5 new or rewritten for this), lints clean. The module built for `wasm32-unknown-unknown` and packaged for Linux and Android with `scripts/package-native-extension.py`; both archives accepted by `scripts/validate-extension.py`.
- **Not verified.** The page is a phone's page (`platforms: ["android"]`): how the dot, the dimming and the notice look was not seen, because neither phone was reachable (wireless debugging off). The tracked source on the `extension` branch and the submission branch still hold the previous version.
- **Installed on the desktops (2026-11-15, 04:25–04:29).** The release built from the tree of the gate, staged and installed for the user on both desktops; the installed programs' checksums equal the staged ones. The packages of Control, Clipboard and, on the workstation, Upload were replaced by the ones built here (Clipboard and Upload are rebuilt from unchanged sources). After the start on each desktop: every extension `ready` and `running`, its licence and notice recorded as accepted, no error in `status`, the paired devices online again.
- **How a package was replaced, and what that leans on.** The installer refuses a different package under a name that is installed, and removing an extension in the running application forgets, by design, everything it was granted. So the application was stopped, each package removed and installed from the command line (the licence, the notice and the outside-store warning accepted there, on the owner's word that I may), and the application started: what each device had been allowed was the same before and after (compared), because a package replaced between two runs keeps the grants of its name. That is the gap `forget_removed` documents (consent belongs to a name, not to an installation); here it is what an update of one's own extension needs, and it is still open as a matter of design: there is no command that updates a package and says what happens to its grants.

### E-062 — A `PEERSUS_LOG` value adds to the warnings instead of replacing them — IN CODE; tests pass; not in an installed build

- **The fault (met on 2026-11-14, see E-057).** A value naming one module (`peersus_app::desktop_capture=debug`) silenced every other warning and error: the filter library adds its default level only when the value holds no directive at all. An owner started that way lost its own warnings.
- **Changed** (`peersus-diagnostics`, `filter_for`). The value is parsed after `warn`, so what it names is added; a level it gives for everything replaces that, and `warn` alone still shows no development event (the existing process test that holds this failed on a first version that kept the development target too, and that version was dropped).
- **Verified.** Three unit tests (one target named keeps the other warnings; a level for everything, the empty value and `off`; a value that is no filter is refused) and the crate's ten process tests; the lint is clean. The whole gate was not run for this one file.

### E-063 — The owner's log file no longer grows without end — IN CODE; tests pass; SEEN with the real program on a 40 MB file; not in an installed build

- **Found on 2026-11-14 (E-055).** The installed owner's log was 235 MB: the window opens it for appending when it starts the owner, the owner and the extension hosts write their diagnostics to it, and nothing ever trims it.
- **Changed** (`peersus-diagnostics`, `Bounded`). Diagnostics are written through a writer that, when standard error is a regular file, asks the file's size after every 256 KiB written and, past 32 MiB, empties it and says so in its first line; the newest lines follow. A terminal or a pipe is left alone. It is the file's own size that is asked, so what a helper writes to the same file counts. The file is emptied, not rotated: the owner is given an open file by the window and knows no path, and one bounded file is what was missing; what is lost is the oldest 32 MiB of diagnostics, which hold no payloads by the crate's rule.
- **Verified.** Three unit tests (400 lines against a limit of 1000 bytes: the file never stands far past the limit, begins with the notice and ends with the newest line; a file left large by an earlier run is emptied at the first line; what is no file is written and never measured), with the crate's other thirteen. With the real program built from this tree: a 40 MB file given as its standard error was 69 bytes after one command, holding the notice.
- **Not verified.** Windows: whether a file opened for appending by another process can be emptied through a second handle there was not run. The installed owners still run the build without this, and their files are as large as they were.
- **What followed from it, done the same day.** The window reads the owner's last words from the place the file ended at before it started that owner, and a file emptied at that owner's first line is shorter than that place: nothing was read then. A log shorter than the remembered place is now read whole (all of it was written after), and the line that says the log was emptied (`peersus_diagnostics::LOG_EMPTIED`, one text for the writer and the reader) is never taken for what the owner said. Two tests in the window's link module (60 of that module pass); the lint of both crates is clean.

### E-064 — The two files a Rust guest reaches the application through have one copy, and a check holds the others to it — IN CODE; tests pass; the extensions rebuilt; not on the submission branches

- **What was there.** `native.rs` was the same 139 lines in three extensions. `host.rs` was in two, and had drifted: Control's had the readiness wait and the diagnostic import, Clipboard's the bounded read and its test; each fix of the boundary had to be made by hand in every copy.
- **Decided: one copy and a comparison, not a crate.** An extension is built from its own source and lockfile against a checkout of the application, and the workspace forbids `unsafe` outside the two platform crates, which is what the imports are made of. So the files stay copies; the one copy is `templates/guest/host.rs` and `templates/guest/native.rs`, and `scripts/check-guest-glue.py` refuses an extension whose copy differs by a byte (a guest that carries neither file is not concerned). `scripts/check-extension-source.py` runs it before any submitted command, and refuses an SDK that does not carry it.
- **Changed in the extensions.** `host.rs` is now the union of the two (both guests carry all of it; `dead_code` allowed in that file, since not every guest goes through every door) and is identical in Control and Clipboard; Clipboard declares the `development-diagnostics` feature the file names.
- **Verified.** Byte for byte: the two `host.rs` and the three `native.rs` equal the tracked copies; the check over the four local extensions exits 0. Control 64 tests, Clipboard 74, lints clean for the test build, the native feature and the WebAssembly target with and without the diagnostic feature. The built modules import what they did before: Clipboard's still does not import `poll` (the linker drops what a guest never calls), Control's does. The tests of the scripts: 12, of which the 8 that need a fixture commit are skipped on this workstation by its git policy, so the refusal was also run by hand through the real source check (a changed byte: refused, nothing built; an SDK without the check: refused).
- **Left as it is.** Upload reaches the host in forty lines of its own (it opens no stream) and carries only `native.rs`. The scripted host used by the tests lives in `host.rs` too and is compiled only for tests.

- **Asked by the owner** on 2026-11-15: both phones, the application and the extensions, not the application alone.

### E-066 — Control keeps its page when no device is chosen: the buttons, the pad and the keyboard stay where they are, and the chooser says why — IN CODE (extension); its tests pass; INSTALLED and SEEN on NX; INSTALLED on ROG, not looked at there by who built it; not in the desktops' installed packages

- **Said by the owner (2026-11-15):** the Remote page must not be altered; the mouse and the rest stay as they are. What he had seen: when something happened to the chosen device (it slept, went offline, lost its permission) the page was replaced by another one, a sentence, a list of devices and a "Refresh devices" button, with the pad gone.
- **A first answer of the same morning was wrong and is taken out.** I had read the fault as "the page with no device begins under the header's mark" and made the application start every extension's whole page under the header's band on a phone (`clear_of_header` on `contributed_page::Body` and `Elements`, a headless test and two test helpers, `Window::of` and `top_of`). That moved the pad 56 px down on a phone, which is an alteration of the page, and left the other page in place. All of it is removed; `Form::clear_of_header` is again only for the application's own messages and pages, and its comment says an extension's whole page is not moved.
- **Changed (extension only, `plugins/peersus-control/wasm/src/ui.rs`).** The page has one tree. With no device chosen the chooser's own row reads "Choose a device" and its frame carries why above it (the sentence about the device that was unselected; "No computer can be controlled now." when none can); touching it opens the same list as with a device chosen, every paired device with where it is, only the ready ones to be touched. A press on a button, the pad, the wheel, Backspace or the keyboard with nothing chosen is drawn at rest and sends nothing (what `send` already did for a device that cannot be reached). The separate page, its button and the `press` event that only that button sent are gone; the list is read again every two seconds as before.
- **Tests.** The three tests of an unselected device now hold the page's shape (three parts, the wheel and the pad in their places, the chooser closed), that a press with nothing chosen locks nothing and writes nothing, and that the rows are reached through the chooser. 64 pass in the extension. The module was built, its archives packaged for Android and Linux and validated; the copies of the shared guest files were compared.

The permission was turned on again and ROG chosen by touching its row. After the package without the offset was installed, the pad is where it was before this morning, its top between the header's two pieces.
