The three Android archives were copied to ROG's `Download/` (size and SHA-256 equal) and installed there through the application's socket as on NX in E-065; the new Control archive replaced the one on NX the same way. On both: the three extensions `running`, the agreement recorded, the installed modules equal to the archives', the permissions kept, no message. The owner said it was right on his side before the deploy; what he looked at was not asked.

At every reading of the list the sentence is made again from how that device stands now; when it can be controlled again it is chosen again and the sentence goes. It is the person's own choice coming back: no other device is ever chosen in its place, and choosing another one by hand ends the following. Coming back sends nothing by itself. The permission test now also holds the three steps (not allowed, then sleeping with another device ready and not taken, then back and chosen again with no stream opened); 64 pass, the extension's lint is clean. **Seen on NX:** permission off, the frame as above; permission on, and within four seconds, nothing touched, ROG was chosen again and the frame was the closed chooser. ### E-067 — What did not build cleanly or pass on Windows is corrected — IN CODE; the tests concerned pass in the Windows guest; lint clean on Linux; the two interface tests of eighty files still fail there

- **Found by running the tree of the gate in the Windows guest (2026-11-15).** The contract and the core passed; of the diagnostics tests two failed (the bounded log file: a file opened for appending cannot be emptied there); the plugin host and the application built with warnings for code only Linux uses; the application's 500 library tests passed (4 ignored); the window's passed but the two that hand over eighty files (659 passed, 2 failed, 4 ignored), as before.
- **Changed.** `peersus_diagnostics::open_log(path)` opens the log one way for whoever starts an owner: appending with mode 0600 on Unix, writing from the end on Windows, where the bound can then empty it; the window's `open_log` and the diagnostics tests use it. The plugin host's watcher of its socket files is Unix only and waits for ever elsewhere; `surface_failures` is allowed unused off Linux; of the nearby announcements, what only the Linux adapter reads is gone or gated to Linux and tests (`Air`, `service_data_128`, `section_128` and their tests removed: no adapter on Windows or macOS reads them).
- **Run again there on the corrected tree.** Diagnostics: 6 passed. `check` of the plugin host and the application with all targets: no warning. Plugin host: 95 passed. The application's and the window's tests were not run again there after the change.
- **On Linux:** `clippy -D warnings` over the four crates, clean. No full gate has run on this tree yet.

### E-068 — Two documents are written from the source: the crates with what each builds against, and an index of the extension contract — DONE; the generator's tests pass; the pages generated and checked

- **Why.** The owner asked for documents maintained by generation where they can be. The workspace map in `docs/architecture.md` was written by hand and says nothing of which crate builds against which; the contract an extension is written against had no list of what it declares.
- **Added to `scripts/gen-docs.py`** (which already wrote the marked blocks of the README and the changelog, and which the gate's metadata step runs with `--check`). `CRATES` in `docs/architecture.md`: every workspace member with the `description` of its own manifest, the workspace crates it builds against (dependencies and build dependencies, for every target; test-only ones left out) and the modules directly under its `src/`, lowest crate first. A circle between crates is refused, and so is a member whose manifest does not say what it is (`xtask` had no `description`; it has one). `CONTRACT` in the new `docs/api/contract-index.md`: every public item declared at the top of a module of `peersus-api`, with the first sentence of its own documentation and a link to the file; what is inside a test module, or public only within the crate, is not listed. The hand-written table of responsibilities and boundaries stays above the generated one: a boundary is a decision, not something the manifests say.
- **What the generated table shows that the page did not say:** `peersus-ui` builds against `peersus-app` and `peersus-net`, not only against the core; `peersus-plugin-runtime` builds against `peersus-plugin`; `peersus-diagnostics` builds against the contract. None is a circle. Whether the window should reach the owner through less than the whole application crate is a question for the refactor, not answered here.
- **Tests** (`scripts/test-gen-docs.py`, 10 pass; three are new): the table is what the manifests and folders say, lowest first, and a new module makes the page stale; crates that need each other are refused with the circle named; the index lists a struct, a `const fn` and a constant with the first sentence, and neither a `pub(crate)` item, a private one, nor one inside a test module.
- **Run on the tree:** both pages written; `--check` passes afterwards. The index is 132 lines for ten modules.
- **Not done.** No index of the other crates (their public items are the application's own and change with it). The modules are listed by name only, with no word of what each is: that would have to come from each file's own first line, which not every file has.

### E-069 — P2 (A-04) — Each extension has a rate of its own; one that keeps flooding is suspended for a while and the person is told — IN CODE; tests pass, one through a real extension process; NOT in an installed build; NOT tried on a phone

- **The finding (A-04, E-028):** nothing limited how much one extension could ask of the hub. A flood was only ever stopped by what stops a crash: its queue filled, its connection was dropped, it was restarted, and after five times turned off. The project's own isolation rule asks for a rate, a refusal or a delay over it, and a temporary suspension with the person told.
- **The rule, in the hub** (`peersus-plugin/src/hub.rs`; the hub does no I/O and is given the time, so every part of this is tested without waiting). Each extension has 600 operations a second, 300 at once after a quiet time (`RATE`, `BURST`). A call, a subscription and a published event each spend one, counted before anything else about the operation is looked at; a reply, a cancellation and an unsubscription spend nothing, since they end work. The application itself is not limited. Over its rate an operation is refused with the new `Error::RateLimited` (`rate-limited` on the wire). Six hundred refusals with no quiet second between them (`SUSPEND_AFTER`) suspend the extension for thirty seconds (`SUSPENSION`): everything it starts is refused, and a call to it is refused at once as unavailable instead of waiting out its deadline. The allowance and the suspension are kept by the extension's name, as its permissions are: a restart lifts nothing, a removal (`forget`) forgets both.
- **The delay, in the link** (`link.rs`). After a refusal for the rate the application stops reading from that extension until the hub would take one more operation, or until the suspension ends (`Channel::retry_after`). What the extension writes meanwhile waits in its own connection, so a flood is slowed by the extension's own writes and costs the application one refusal, not one for each operation.
- **Found while testing it, and fixed:** the link read the next operation before the answer to a refused call had been written. An extension whose calls were refused faster than they were answered therefore filled its own queue of 64 and lost its connection, before any rate could apply: that is what E-028 saw as "the same handling as a crash". The reader now yields to the writer after each refusal. The new link test failed on exactly this before the change (the connection was closed at the 65th refused call) and passes after it.
- **The person is told once for each suspension**, in fixed words, by the owner's health check: "An extension asked for too much, too fast, and is paused for a short while" (`Hub::take_suspended`, which names each suspended extension once). The extension stays installed, running and with its permissions.
- **Tests.** Hub (6 new): over the rate one is refused while another participant and a reply go on, and the rate comes back with time up to a burst; one that keeps flooding is suspended, named once, not called meanwhile, and heard again afterwards; refusals with quiet between them never add up; a restart keeps a suspension and a removal forgets it; the application is not limited; the transport is told how long to leave a suspended extension unread. Link (1 new): twice a burst at once is answered call by call, some `rate-limited`, and the connection is whole afterwards. Application (1 new, a real process): a test extension that writes five thousand operations at once is paused and told about within the test's wait, is not turned off, another extension's action completes meanwhile, and the application's own call to the suspended one is refused within two seconds. The broker's 100 tests pass.
- **What the numbers rest on.** Control forwards about sixty batches a second for each controlling device, one call each (E-019); 600 leaves several devices room. No installed extension was measured against the limit: until the three extensions have been driven on the devices with this build, that none of them reaches it is an expectation, not an observation.
- **Not done.** The queue of the application's own endpoint is still one queue for every extension: what bounds one extension's share of it is now its rate, not a share of its own. A control-frame write to an extension that never reads still has no time limit (left from A-01; E-009 tells why a liveness rule can wrongly stop an extension the system has frozen). The suspension is not shown on the extension's card, only said once as a message.

### E-070 — P1 — A device that connects again takes the place of the session it left; only the other connection of the same moment is a duplicate — IN CODE; its tests pass; NOT in an installed build; NOT yet seen on the devices

- **What it answers.** Two things seen on the devices and one read: the owner's "after it is back online it stays sleeping" (2026-11-15); a phone whose application had started again saw both desktops offline for about 100 s while they showed it online (the note of 2026-11-15 below); and the rule itself, in `peersus-net/src/network_host.rs` `install`.
- **Cause, read in the source.** A second connection from a device that already had a session was closed as a "duplicate session" whenever the session held was still believed alive, unless the new one was the preferred one (the connection the device with the lower id dialed) and the held one was not. That rule is for two devices that dial each other at the same moment. But a device dials only when it has no connection of its own (`reconnect` skips every device it has a session with), so a later connection means the session held here is one the device no longer has: it slept through the end of it, or its application started again. Its new connection was refused for as long as this side had not noticed the old one dead, and until then the device was shown as it last was: asleep, or online to a device that could not reach it. Whether the new connection was let in depended on which of the two ids is lower, which is why it was seen with some pairs and not others.
- **A change found in the tree, and what was kept of it.** At 08:02 on 2026-11-15, while this session's gate was running, `install` was changed by an edit that is not one of this session's and that the log did not record: every new connection replaced the session held. It answers the two faults above, and the gate of that hour passed with it. It also lost two things. With both devices dialing at the same moment each side could close a different one of the two connections, leaving none; and by removing the old session before the new one was put in, the files of the old session that waited for consent were no longer withdrawn with it.
- **The rule now** (`takes_the_place`, one function with the reasons written on it): a new connection takes the session's place unless the session held is alive, is the preferred one, the new one is not, and the session is younger than ten seconds (`SAME_MOMENT`). That one case is the same-moment duplicate, and both sides close the same connection as before. A session records when it was installed. The replaced session's waiting files are withdrawn with it, as before 08:02. A session that takes another's place starts awake, as every session does.
- **Tests** (in the file; 10 pass): the decision for each case; and with three real connections between two endpoints: the other connection of the same moment is closed and the session is the one it was; a device that had said it sleeps and connects again later, by the connection that is not the preferred one, takes the session's place, the old connection is closed, and it is awake. The second half fails on the rule as it was before 08:02, the first half on the rule of 08:02. The net crate's whole suite and the lint are run after this entry; see Gate status for the run that covers it.
- **Not verified.** On the devices: a phone that sleeps and wakes, and one whose application starts again, with the installed build on both sides. Ten seconds is a choice, not a measurement: a device whose application starts again within ten seconds of connecting, on the side that does not dial the preferred connection, is still refused until it tries again.

### E-071 — P2/P3 (A-06, A-07, A-08, A-13) — Four bounds on what one extension can take from the application — IN CODE; each test failed first and passes; NOT in an installed build

- **A-07, consent prompts** (`peersus-app/src/extension_prompts.rs`). Four prompts could wait at once and nothing said whose: one extension could take all four for five minutes each and no other extension could ask its owner anything. Now one extension has one prompt waiting; four in all (`MOST_PENDING`), each from a different extension. A second one from the same extension is refused at once with fixed words. Checked against the consumers: the three prompts (configure, authorize, present) are asked by a guest that makes one call at a time, so no installed extension has two at once. Test: an extension with a prompt waiting is refused four more, another extension is asked meanwhile, and the first may ask again once its prompt is gone. The older test of the bound of four now uses four extensions.
- **A-08, the data listener** (`peersus-plugin/src/service.rs`). `let Ok(stream) = accepted else { continue }` asked again at once after a failed accept; an accept that keeps failing (no descriptors left) would take a processor for as long as it lasts. The step is one function (`taken`) that waits 100 ms (`ACCEPT_PAUSE`) after a failure. Test, on the paused clock: a connection costs no time, ten failures cost ten pauses. The control listener was not changed: a failed accept there already ends the cycle, and the restart pause follows. Not reproduced with a real listener out of descriptors.
- **A-06, pointers of their own** (`peersus-app/src/desktop_input.rs`). Every new `owner` name an extension sent made state here and, with `pointer: own`, a device on the desktop; nothing counted them. Now one extension replays for at most sixteen devices at once (`MOST_OWNERS`). At the limit the pointers of its devices that left (not heard from, nothing pressed) are given up first, so a seventeenth device over a long day is not refused for good; a device that comes back has its pointer made again. Over the limit the call is refused with fixed words and nothing is made or remembered. Test: sixteen devices, the next refused with and without a pointer of its own, the ones it has go on, another extension is not counted with it, and after the silence limit a newcomer takes a place. Sixteen is a choice: the layout of devices has no stated most.
- **A-13, captured input.** Read first, and the finding as written was not the fault. The only queue an extension's slowness fills is the one in `surfaces.rs`, and it was bounded before the audit (`MOST_WAITING`, with its test): past it the batch is refused and the pointer is given back. The queues in `input_follow.rs` and `desktop_capture.rs` are between the application's own tasks; the sender waits for the owner's loop, which takes a batch without waiting for the extension (`forward_input` is not async). They were left as they are. What the reading did find: the queue's own words said "a person cannot outpace this" because motion is summed, but the pointer carried across the layout is sent as places (`Position`), which were not merged, so a call out for the time of a few hundred mouse samples filled the queue and the person's pointer was given back with "The extension is not keeping up". Reproduced by a test (twice the queue's size of places behind one call: refused), then fixed: a place that waits is replaced by the newer one, and a press between two places keeps its order. Whether this was behind any pointer given back on the devices is not known: no log of it was looked for.
- **Verification.** Application library suite with fixtures: 695 passed, 8 ignored. Broker crate: 101 + 9 passed. Formatting applied. Lint and the whole gate: not yet run on this tree; see Gate status.

### E-072 — P1/P2 (A-03, A-09, A-10, A-11) — The application names no provider's header and no service for a change; the input service's arguments have one definition; one extension carries input; no grant between two extensions — IN CODE; tests pass, one through the built extension and a local TLS server; NOT in an installed build; the prompt's new line NOT seen in a window

- **A-03, request headers** (`peersus-app/src/platform_services/file_http.rs`; decided under the owner's authority of 2026-11-15). The service allowed `content-type` and one header a single provider's protocol uses, by name. Now an extension names the request headers of its own when it asks to send a file (`authorize`, optional `headers`, at most eight lower-case names); the person is shown them with the file and the address ("It also sends request headers of its own there: …"); they are kept with that authorization, and a request may carry `content-type` and those only. Names the transport or the application writes, or that a server or a proxy reads as where a request came from, refuse the call before the person is asked: the Fetch standard's forbidden request headers, with `authorization` and the forwarding ones (`header_forbidden`). The manifest is not changed: the address itself is given the same way, at the call, and shown at the same prompt. The prompt kind carries the names (`ExtensionPromptKind::Authorize.headers`, absent when empty); its words moved from the component to the display rules (`view::extension_request`), one place for both platforms. The upload extension names its header when it asks.
  - **Compatibility.** A new extension on an older application: the older application ignores `headers` in `authorize` and allows the old name, so it works. An older upload extension on this application: a file small enough for one request is sent as before; a larger one is refused at its first part with "Unsupported request header", before anything of it is sent. The extension must be updated with the application; nothing falls back silently.
  - **The other half of the finding**, a bound "sized for one provider's protocol": the most values kept from responses (128) was explained in a comment by one provider's arithmetic. The number is unchanged and named (`MOST_SECRETS`), explained by the service's own largest file. The API page said 64; it says 128 now.
  - **Tests.** Names taken and refused (shape, count, each forbidden kind); a forbidden name refuses `authorize` and leaves no authorization; the names are kept with the authorization; through a real local TLS server, a header that was not named is refused and the next request the server sees is the allowed one, which carries the named header. The built upload extension run by the real module host against that server (`compiled_upload_wasm_through_real_tls_multipart_and_chunks`, by hand with the three artifacts named): the prompt is given `x-upload-session`, and the upload in one request and the one in parts both end with the link. UI: the words with and without names. Android's adapter was changed the same way and is compiled only for Android: not compiled yet.
- **A-09, the notice of a change** (`runtime.rs`, `platform_services.rs`). When grants, settings or the devices change, the runtime published the clipboard service's `changed` by naming that service, twice. It now asks the table of services that say when they change (`platform_services::watched`, also what the watch tasks at start read) and names none. Nothing changes today, since the table has one such service; a second one would be told without a line in the runtime. Test: the watched services are those of the table, each once, and each is a service with methods.
- **A-10, the input service.** Its argument names (`events`, `owner`, `pointer`, `own`, `show_name`, `label`) were bare strings in the service and in the runtime's name tag. They are declared once beside the service (`platform_services::input_arg`), used by both, and now described in the API page, which had only named two of them in passing. And the carrier: with two extensions declaring an `input` contribution the first found was used and the other ran for nothing. A second one is now not started beside the first, and the person is told ("Another extension already carries input between devices. Turn that one off to use this one"); every start goes through the one function that checks (`start_one`, read: no other call starts a service). Test of the rule (`pass_through::carrier_taken`); not tried with two real extensions, since only one that declares it exists.
- **A-11, guarded methods between two extensions: decided, not built.** A capability is granted by the person to the application; nothing shown at consent says "this extension may use that extension", no extension needs it, and adding it means new consent words on both platforms. So it stays as it is and is said: an extension that imports another's guarded method is offered nothing to be allowed and is answered `denied`; unguarded methods are open to the consumers an export names. Test: such an extension has nothing grantable and holds nothing at the hub whatever was written in the grants. Written in `docs/api/service-messages.md`.
- **Verification.** Application suite with fixtures 859 passed; core 141; the UI tests of this change 12; the upload extension's own 9; lint with warnings as errors clean for core, net, diagnostics, broker, application and UI. The whole gate: not yet run on this tree.

### E-073 — Key bindings (asked by the owner on 2026-11-15 as an extra): what exists, what a desktop allows, and the design — DESIGN ONLY; nothing is built, no document may say key bindings exist

- **Asked.** Extensions register bindings of their own and Settings knows them; examples given: a chord that takes the pointer to a chosen computer, chords that accept or decline an incoming file, a screenshot extension whose picture lands on Send. "Not a priority, an extra"; where it goes was left to this work.
- **Read in the tree (2026-11-15).**
  - An extension's command already exists as data: `peersus_api::contributions::Action` (id, label, the exported method it calls, capability, `needs_device`, `destructive`, `input`, a bounded timeout), and one path runs it: `Command::InvokeAction { plugin, action, peer }` → `runtime.rs` `invoke`. The CLI and the cards use that path. Every contribution type refuses fields it does not know (`deny_unknown_fields`), so a manifest that says something new is refused by an older application, by name of nothing, with the manifest error: explicit, not silently dropped.
  - The application's own answers to an incoming file are commands too: `AcceptTransfer { transfer, directory }` and `CancelTransfer { transfer }`; the folder is chosen by the interface (`peersus-ui/src/lib.rs` `accept`), not by the owner.
  - Keys in the window: one handler at the root (`on_global_key_down`, used only to mark the window awake) and the paste rule of Send (`intake::is_paste`). No registry of chords, no stored chord, nothing in Settings.
  - Keys outside the window: none. The desktop's keys reach the application only while the pointer is taken (`desktop_capture.rs`, InputCapture portal, where both Control keys give it back). `ashpd` 0.14 is already a dependency and has a `global_shortcuts` feature, not enabled.
  - On the workstation (GNOME Shell 51.1, Wayland) the portal offers `org.freedesktop.portal.GlobalShortcuts` version 2 and `InputCapture` version 2 (read with `gdbus introspect`). The second desktop was not asked.
- **What a desktop allows, and what it does not.**
  - Global shortcuts through the portal are the desktop's: the application offers an id, words and a *preferred* trigger; the desktop asks the person, may give another trigger or none, and tells what it gave (`ShortcutsChanged`). So outside the window the application cannot promise a chord; it can show the one the desktop gave.
  - **Taking the pointer by a chord is not something the capture portal offers.** Its session is armed (`Enable`) and the desktop alone starts a capture, when the pointer meets a barrier (`Activated`); there is no request to start one (read in `ashpd`'s `input_capture` interface: create, zones, barriers, enable, disable, release, connect; and known of the portal's definition, not read again today). The owner's first example therefore needs one more thing than a binding, a way for the application to bring the pointer to a device's edge, and the only one in reach is to move this computer's pointer with the virtual device the input service already makes. Whether that reaches a barrier reliably (acceleration, several displays, an edge only partly a barrier) is **not known and not tried**. It is the last step of this work, an experiment on the owner's two desktops that he asked for, and is reported as it turns out.
  - Windows and macOS have global hot keys only through their own interfaces, which the workspace has no safe wrapper for today. X11 sessions are not covered by the portal route everywhere. Android has no such keys.
- **Design.**
  1. **A binding is a person's chord on an action that already exists. No extension code runs in the application, and no new way to run an extension is added.** What can be bound: the application's own short list (accept the incoming files, decline them, show Send, show Receive) and every action of an extension that needs nothing typed or staged (`input: none`) and is not destructive. Each is run the way its button runs it; an extension's through `InvokeAction`, with its capability, its per-device grant and its rate exactly as today.
     - **`input` here is the action's own field, what the person must type or stage before it runs; it says nothing of the pointer or the keyboard.** An action that takes the pointer to a device is not excluded by it: it would be an action of the extension that carries input, with `needs_device` and `input: none`, run through `InvokeAction` like any other, and what moves the pointer stays where it is today (the input service and `desktop_input.rs`, under the extension's grant for that device). The owner's first example is therefore inside this design; what it still lacks is the thing told above, a way to start a capture by request, which no binding supplies.
  2. **Contract (`peersus-api`).** One type, `Chord`: modifiers (control, alt, shift, the system key) and one key of a closed list (letters, digits, function keys, a few named ones), written as text (`"ctrl+alt+1"`), with at least one of control, alt or the system key so that a chord is never something typed. An `Action` gains one optional field, `chord`: the chord its extension *suggests*. Nothing else is declared: the identity, words, device need and capability of a binding are its action's. A separate list of bindings beside the actions was weighed and not taken: it would repeat the action's id and label and need a cross check for nothing a consumer reads. An older application refuses a manifest with the field, as it refuses any field it does not know; an extension that wants to run on both leaves it out.
  3. **A suggestion binds nothing.** An installed extension never makes a key do anything by itself: Settings shows "Suggested: …" with one press to take it, and refuses it if it is taken. An action that needs a device is bound once per device by the person (the chord for one computer is not the chord for another), so a suggestion there is only a starting value.
  4. **One registry (`peersus-core`).** What is bound is the owner's setting: chord → the application's action, or (extension, action, device). One chord has one meaning; a second use is refused with the words of what holds it. A binding goes when its extension is removed or its device is unpaired; it stays, idle, while the extension is off. Set and cleared by one command; published in the snapshot, so a window and a platform adapter read the same list.
  5. **Settings.** One section, "Shortcuts", in the application's settings, a generic list: the application's actions, then each extension's by its name, each row with its chord, record, clear, and for a device action one row per device that allows it. It is in the settings search. The row never names an extension in code: it is drawn from the snapshot.
  6. **Where a chord is heard.** *In the window*, on every desktop, by the root key handler: no platform code. *Outside it*, where the platform offers it: on Linux the owner holds one GlobalShortcuts session with what is bound, behind the platform boundary of the application crate, and Settings shows the trigger the desktop gave when it differs. Elsewhere Settings says "Shortcuts work while the window is in front on this system"; on Android the section is not shown. Accepting a file from outside the window needs a folder the owner knows; if none is set that binding says so instead of opening a chooser nobody sees.
  7. **Limits (the isolation rule).** A chord is refused by the registry, not by an extension; a held key repeats nothing (one run per press, and never faster than the extension's own rate of E-069 allows); an action that fails says its fixed message as it does from its button.
- **Not decided here, said so:** whether the window's chords must be withheld while a text field has the focus on Windows, where the right Alt key is control and alt together (to be read and tried in the Windows guest); the screenshot extension (a new extension and a new host service for a picture of the screen, which is the screenshot portal on Linux and something else on each system: not part of bindings, and not started).
- **Order of work:** the contract with its tests; the registry and the command; Settings; the window; the Linux adapter; Control declaring an action for a device and the experiment of the first example.

### E-074 — The tree of the green gate on the two desktops and the two phones: what was installed, and what the stress of one extension, the presence rule and the upload prompt did there — INSTALLED and byte-verified on all four; SEEN as told below; a real upload NOT made

- **Installed (2026-11-15, from the tree the gate of 1301 s ran on).** Desktop: the optimised programs (built 10:08–10:14) staged and installed for the user on MSI and on HP; the installed `peersus`, `peersus-ui` and `peersus-wasm-host` have the checksums of the built ones on both. The first staging of this session took the programs of 04:24 from the layout's triple directory, because the build had been run without the layout's environment and had written to the bare `target/release`; it was seen in the files' times before the application was restarted, staged again from where the build wrote, and compared byte for byte. `./build.sh desktop` is the entry that keeps the two in one place. Android: the development package built from the same tree (10:22), installed on NX and ROG by the deploy command, byte for byte verified, no data cleared. Extensions: Control, Clipboard and Upload from the archives of 06:11, 07:52 and 09:47, on the desktops by remove and install with the accepted agreement while the application was stopped (per-device permissions identical before and after, by checksum of the list), on the phones through the running application (modules compared with the archive, permissions kept). All three run on all four devices; Upload had not been installed on HP before and has no permission there.
- **The Android build warns** (it is the only build of the adapter's half; the gate does not see it): three constants of `platform_services.rs` (`EVENTS`, `POINTER`, `POINTER_OWN`) and `send_files`, three variants of `appearance.rs` and one `mut` in the window crate are unused there. Not corrected yet.
- **Stress of one extension** (the `test` extension of the local copy, rebuilt against today's contract and with one more button, *Flood events*: five thousand published events and nothing that is answered). Driven through each application's own control channel, one press at a time, while the snapshot and another extension's page were asked once or twice a second:

  | press | NX (phone) | ROG (phone) | MSI (desktop, installed release) |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Flood (2000 calls and 2000 events at once) | the extension was started again within 2.3 s; one message | the same | started again within 1.5 s; one message |
  | Flood events | "An extension asked for too much, too fast, and is paused for a short while" at 2.3 s; not called for at least 64 s, answering again by 75 s; no restart seen | paused at 2.3 s; started again at 10.4 s | paused at 1.5 s; started again at 10.6 s |
  | Nonsense | no change seen (sampled each second) | no change seen | its process was replaced within 1 s |
  | Never answer | "did not complete the action in time" at 5.9 s; answers the next press | stayed running and answered the next press; the same message was already in its list from the press before, so when it came is not known | the same message at 5.0 s; process not replaced |
  | Spin | started again within 2.5 s | (state not seen changing at one sample a second) | process replaced within 4 s |
  | Fill memory | started again within 2.3 s | started again | started again within 1 s |
  | Leave | started again within 2.3 s | started again | started again within 1.5 s |

  On every device, through all of it: the application's process was the same one before and after; no request on the control channel failed (about 190 samples on NX, 80 on ROG, 290 on MSI); the snapshot was answered in at most 0.79 s on NX, 0.26 s on ROG and under 5 ms on MSI; the other extension's page was answered every time, in at most 0.23 s on a phone; the three other extensions stayed running and the three paired devices stayed online. The extension was never turned off for good. This is the acceptance the isolation rule asks for, on three of the four devices; HP was not driven.
- **Read from it, and not verified further.** (1) A flood that contains calls ends as a restart, not as a pause: every refused call is answered, the extension does not read while it floods, and its connection is closed when the answers cannot be delivered. That is bounded and costs the application nothing, but it is what E-069 described as cured; what E-069 cured is the case where the answers can be written. (2) A paused extension that goes on writing is started again about ten seconds later on two devices of three. The candidate is the extension host's own five-second limit on a write nobody reads; it was not traced. The pause is kept by name over a restart (E-069), and the extension was heard again afterwards. (3) A call to a paused extension is refused with "The plugin is not running", which is not what is the matter with it. (4) The pause lasted longer than thirty seconds on NX because what the extension had already written was still over its rate when the first pause ended.
- **Also seen while it ran, cause not known:** "[clipboard] A plugin could not complete the action" once on NX (about 10:37) and once on ROG, with Clipboard running before and after. The stress presses nothing of Clipboard's. It is the rare message already on the list, now with a time.
- **NX's own log cannot be read over adb** (its buffers report 0 bytes readable), so nothing above rests on it.
- **The presence rule of E-070, on the devices.** NX woken: MSI, HP and ROG showed it awake within 1.5 s. Put to sleep: all three showed it sleeping after 17.8 s, the time the phone took to doze. Its application stopped by force: offline on all three after 34 s. Started again: online on all three within 1.5 s, and NX showed all three online 1.9 s after its start. Stopped and started at once, while the three still held the old session: NX showed all three online 4.4 s after the start, and none of them showed it offline at any sample. That last case is the one that took about 100 s before (the note of 2026-11-15); it is answered. A desktop that sleeps and wakes was not tried.
- **The upload prompt on a phone.** Upload was asked to send a synthetic file of 2048 bytes made in the application's own cache on NX. The prompt arrived with the address, the name, the size and `x-upload-session` as the header the extension names, and the window showed "It also sends request headers of its own there: x-upload-session." with Allow and Close (screenshot over adb). **It was closed, not allowed, and the files were removed:** allowing it publishes a file at a third party under the owner's key, which is his to ask for. So a file sent in one request and one sent in parts from a phone are still not seen; the built extension against a local TLS server (E-072) remains the evidence for that path.
- **Found there:** the prompt's first line lay under the header's mark on the phone (E-076).
- **Left as found:** three archives named `control-*.tar.gz` in the application's cache on NX, from an earlier session; `test` is installed and switched off on NX and MSI, and was removed from ROG, as each was before.

### E-075 — An owner that dies with no window open is started again: the program keeps its own owner — REMOVED the same day (E-080); what follows is the record of what was built and seen before that

- **The gap** (phase A, the last open item of "never locked out"). A window starts a dead owner again by itself (E-058). With no window (the owner kept in the background, or started from a command line) nobody did, and a device then stayed unreachable until a person noticed.
- **Weighed.** A unit of the session's service manager for each platform (systemd, launchd, a Windows service): three mechanisms, an installation step the per-user install does not have today, and nothing for an owner started by hand. The owner starting itself again from inside after a failed task: a process that has failed is the wrong one to trust with it, and a death by a signal leaves nothing to do it. **Taken:** the program that is about to be the owner first becomes its keeper, a process that holds nothing of the application's (no lock, no socket, no network, no log of its own), starts the same program once more as the owner, and waits.
- **The rule** (`peersus-app/src/lifetime.rs`, `Keeper`, pure, given the time). The owner ends well: the keeper ends well. It could not start and said why (exit code 1): the keeper ends with the same failure and starts nothing, so a window reads the reason exactly as before. It died while running (a signal that asks nothing, a panic, or the new exit code 3 the owner gives when its own task failed, `DIED_RUNNING`): started again, at most three times in five minutes (`MOST_RESTARTS`, `RESTARTS_WITHIN`); past that the keeper says so and ends with the failure. The keeper asked to end: it passes the request on, waits, and starts nothing again.
- **The process** (`keeper.rs`, Unix; `bin/peersus.rs`). It wakes for three signals only (its child ended, terminate, interrupt) and never on a timer, so it adds no idle wake-up. The owner is marked with the keeper's process number (`PEERSUS_KEPT`) and is kept only if that number is its parent: a mark inherited by anything else is not believed. On Linux a kept owner also asks the system to be told to end when its keeper dies, so a killed keeper leaves no owner behind that nobody keeps. A command (`peers`, `stop`, `plugin-install`, …) is carried out by the process itself as before; only the start with no command is kept. The update's restart replaces the owner in place and stays under the same keeper. The owner's words still go where the program's output goes, since the owner inherits it.
- **What it changes for whoever looks:** a running application is two `peersus` processes, the keeper and under it the owner; the window's parent-and-child reading is unchanged because the keeper ends as its owner did.
- **Not on Windows:** the program is the owner itself there, as it was, and only a window starts a dead one again. On macOS the keeper runs, but a killed keeper leaves its owner running (the system has no such request).
- **Tests.** Rule (6 new): each ending read from the code or the signal; an owner that ended well or could not start is never started again and is not counted; three restarts and then none, and again once the first are five minutes old; deaths far apart never add up; a keeper asked to end starts nothing. Process (2 new): the system's own account of an ended process is read as the rule expects; a request to end reaches an owner that runs and nobody once it was collected. The real program (3 new, `tests/program.rs`, with the in-memory identity): an owner killed outright is replaced by another that answers `peers`, stops when told, and the keeper then ends with success having said once that it started it again; one killed four times is started three times and then left, the keeper ending with a failure and the sentence; a killed keeper's owner ends by itself (Linux). The nineteen older tests of the real program pass with the keeper between them and the owner.
- **Seen on the desktops (2026-11-15, release programs built 11:30–11:33 from the tree of the gate of 1338 s plus E-078, installed for the user on the workstation and on the second desktop, checksums of the installed `peersus`, `peersus-ui` and `peersus-wasm-host` equal to the built ones on both).** After a start from the window each shows the window, under it one `peersus` with a single thread (the keeper), under that the owner, and under the owner its three extension hosts. The owner killed outright (`KILL`): on both, another owner under the same keeper answered `peers` 0.3 s later, with three new extension hosts; the window's process was the same one before and after; the log ends with "The application stopped unexpectedly and was started again". On the workstation a picture taken through the portal afterwards shows the window on Send with the saved devices and its green mark, so it is attached to the new owner; on the second desktop the window was not looked at.
- **Not verified.** With no window open on a desktop (only by the three runs of the real program in the gate); what the window did in the 0.3 s (whether it noticed its owner ending at all, or joined the new one after trying to start its own: its process did not change, and nothing was read from it); three deaths in five minutes on a desktop; on macOS.

### E-076 — Two corrections found while observing: what stands above the page on a phone starts under the header's pieces; the log's bound is applied when a program starts — INSTALLED on both desktops and both phones; both SEEN (the first on NX, the second on the workstation)

- **Above the page on a phone** (`peersus-ui/src/lib.rs`). A pairing request and an extension's question are put above the page, and on a phone the header's two pieces lie over the top corners of whatever is first there: the first line of the upload prompt was under the mark (E-074, picture taken on NX). The first of the two now starts under the pieces' band, by the same measure the floating messages use (`Form::clear_of_header`, E-046); on a desktop nothing changes. The page below keeps its own room for the pieces, so while a question is shown there is that much empty band between it and the page: accepted, since moving the page's own room would lay the page out again each time a question comes and goes.
- **The log's bound** (`peersus-diagnostics`, `Bounded::over`). The size was asked at the first diagnostic line. The owner installed on the workstation at 10:16 ran over a file of 243 MB left by earlier builds and never emptied it: at its level it had nothing to say, so it never asked. The size is now asked when the program starts. The test that held "at the first line" holds "when the program starts, before it says anything" and what the file then contains exactly.
- **Seen on NX (2026-11-15, development package built 11:42, installed on NX and ROG by the deploy command, byte for byte verified, no Rust warning in its build).** Upload asked to send a file of 2048 bytes: the question stands under the header's mark and menu, its first line whole (`shots/nx_prompt_clear_of_header.png`), the page below it with the empty band told above. Closed without allowing; nothing was sent; the file removed. ROG was installed on and not looked at. The owner saw that question on the phone and did not know what it was: it was this test, and he was told.
- **The log's bound, seen on the workstation.** The log there now begins "The log passed its size limit and was emptied; what follows is newer" and holds what came after.

### E-077 — Read, not changed: why a paused extension that keeps writing is started again, and what the Control guest still waits on — READ in the code; the first is an inference that fits what E-074 saw, not a trace

- **The restart about ten seconds into a pause (E-074, untraced there).** While an extension is over its rate the application stops reading its channel for as long as the hub would refuse it (`link.rs`, the sleep after a `RateLimited` refusal; during a suspension that is the rest of the thirty seconds, `Channel::retry_after`). What the extension goes on writing fills its own connection; its interpreter's write then waits five seconds and fails (`peersus-plugin-runtime/src/socket.rs`, the control connection's write timeout), the guest ends with a failure and its supervisor starts it again after its pause. That fits the times on ROG and on the workstation; **it is inferred from the code, no development trace of it was taken**, and it does not say why NX, a phone like ROG and given the same flood, showed no restart. What follows from it and was read: the suspension is kept by the extension's id and survives that restart (`Hub::revoke` leaves `budgets`; only removing the extension forgets it), so a restart buys a flooding extension nothing; an extension that does not flood never meets it.
- **The Control guest's waits (phase B, "the guest never blocks long").** The wait of thirty seconds for a closed connection's acknowledgement is gone since E-054 (`ACK_MS`, three seconds, counted from the last thing that arrived). What remains: a call to the application waits for its answer, and the application itself answers within the four seconds the call asks for (`CALL_MS`; the hub does not let an extension ask for more than its declared action allows). So the longest the guest stands still for one thing is four seconds, when the application cannot reach a device it was asked to open. Not shortened: no measurement says four seconds is the wrong bound, and a shorter one refuses a slow first connection through a relay.
- **Transient or fatal (phase B).** Read in `remote.rs` `next` and `lib.rs` `run`: a failure on one device's connection (read, write, acknowledgement, a line not understood) ends that connection with its fixed answer and the guest goes on; the guest ends only when its own channel to the application fails. The supervisor counts an end after ten seconds of running as a fresh history (`Policy::healthy_after`), so a guest that ran well and lost its channel is started again and is not given up on. The item written on 2026-11-14 described the code before E-054; nothing is left of it to change.

### E-078 — A press held for more than thirty seconds on a visited device no longer ends its connection — IN CODE; compiles, lint clean; the rule is NOT run by any test and was NOT tried on the desktops

- **The fault (phase B, read on 2026-11-14, never reported by the owner).** While the pointer visits a device and something is held (a key kept down, a drag that pauses), the follower keeps the connection open and says nothing until the next input. Both applications end a connection that carries nothing for thirty seconds (`pump::IDLE_LIMIT`), the visited one lets go of what was held (`HELD_LIMIT`, E-054), the next input fails, the pointer is given back and the edge toward that device is closed for ten seconds.
- **Weighed.** Closing the connection while something is held and saying "still held" in the next: the receiver already keeps a hold across a rotation, but for thirty seconds only, so the same end comes later. A longer limit for a connection that holds something: the application cannot know what an extension's bytes hold, and must not. **Taken:** the follower says the pointer's place again every ten seconds for as long as something is held (`input_follow.rs`, `HOLD`, the same timer that closes a resting connection; the place is absolute, so saying it again moves nothing). It is ordinary input of the contract, so the extension, the visited application's limit and its note of when the controller was last heard are untouched, and a controller that is really gone is still let go of after thirty seconds.
- **Held by:** a compile-time check that the repetition comes at least twice within the limit. The follower's loop runs only with a desktop's capture session and has no test that drives it; lint of the application crate clean, its follower and pass-through tests (12) pass. **Not observed:** holding a key for a minute on the other desktop is the owner's to try (no pointer probe is made on his desktops without a fault reported).
- **Cost.** One wake-up every ten seconds, only while something is held on a visited device.

### E-079 — Where the site is published today and how it got there — FOUND by asking the host and the record of 2026-11-06; nothing changed there; the build that publishes it is NOT in the tree yet

- **Asked (2026-11-15), because the site is to be finished and published and no document said where it lives.** The pages workflow is started by hand and only keeps an artifact.
- **Found.** The relay's own name answers over HTTPS with the site: the nine files of `site/` (the four pages, the style sheet, the script, `supporters.json`, the two marks) were fetched from it and each is byte for byte the tree's file of today. `catalog.json` and anything else are 404: **no extension catalog is published there**, so the store section of the site's page has nothing to read. The application does not depend on that: it reads its catalog from the repository's `extension` branch (`peersus-store-fetch`), not from the site.
- **How it is served.** The program on the HTTPS port of that server is the relay itself (`iroh-relay` 1.4.1 by its own word, installed 2026-11-06, run by its service unit with a configuration that names no folder of pages), and the pages are inside the program: its file contains their text. So what runs there is a relay built with this site embedded, and publishing a changed site means building that relay again with the new pages and replacing the program, which restarts the relay every device of the project uses.
- **The source of that build.** Not in this tree (the workspace uses the relay crate only in tests), not on the server, in no document or script. It was found in the record of the work session of 2026-11-06: a copy of the published relay crate 1.4.1 in a temporary folder, its server changed to answer the site's paths from files embedded at build time and its content policy widened to the site's own scripts, styles and images; built in a Debian container so that it runs on the server's system library; the program copied over, the old one kept beside it, the service restarted. The temporary folder is gone; the steps and the changes were taken out of the record and kept (outside version control, under the build folder). **To do with the site work:** put the change and its build in the tree as one reproducible step (a patch against the published crate and a script), so that publishing the site does not rest on a folder that is cleaned away.
- **Read and not printed anywhere:** the relay's configuration and environment hold an access token; only the names of its settings were read.

### E-080 — The keeper of E-075 is taken out: one `peersus` again — REMOVED in code; lint clean, the program's 19 tests pass; INSTALLED on both desktops and SEEN there as one owner under the window

- **Why.** The owner saw two `peersus` processes, asked what the first was for, and judged it rightly: it was not asked for, a process of the application's own that restarts the application can die as easily as what it watches, and it made things worse in one way (a keeper that ended took its owner down, and an owner asked to end closes its window, so its death closed the whole application). It covered one case only, an owner that dies while no window is open, which has never been seen on his devices; with a window open the window already starts a dead owner again (E-058). A problem that had not happened was answered with a part that added one. He also said he wants no service of the system registered for this and no guard for the window: a crash there is accepted.
- **What it did badly even in its own case (found on the second desktop before the removal).** With a window open, the owner the keeper started again was still marked as started for a window; the window never noticed the change (its requests were answered by the new owner 0.3 s later) and so never took a lease on it; that owner ended after its fifteen seconds of grace for a first window, and only then did the window start another. Two restarts of everything within about seventeen seconds where the window alone makes one.
- **Removed:** `peersus-app/src/keeper.rs`; the rule and its six tests in `lifetime.rs`; the start as a keeper, the exit code for "died while running" and the type that carried it in the program; the three runs of the real program and their helpers in `tests/program.rs`; the section in the architecture page. The window's own recovery (E-058) is untouched.
- **Evidence.** Lint of the application crate with its test features: clean. `tests/program.rs` with the in-memory identity: 19 passed, none failed. Release programs built 12:04–12:05, installed for the user on both desktops, checksums of the installed `peersus` and `peersus-ui` equal to the built ones. After a start from the window, on both: the window, under it one `peersus` (the owner, started 0.01 s and 0.03 s after the window), under it the three extension hosts. The whole gate was not run again over the removal. The Android package is unaffected in behaviour (the phone has no such program) and was not rebuilt.
- **Not recovered by anything, as before E-075 and by the owner's word:** an owner that dies while no window is open (the next window starts it), and a window that dies.
- **Seen again by the owner afterwards, and why (12:06–12:15).** On the workstation he still saw the extra process and that ending it ended everything. What ran there was not the installed programs but the development ones (`target/ui/<triple>/debug`), built at 11:10 with the keeper still in them and started at 12:06; read from the processes' own program paths and file times. Only the release programs had been rebuilt after the removal. The development programs were built again from the tree without it (`./dev.sh desktop`, exit 0, 12:14–12:15); the running window was not restarted for him. **A removal is in every build that can be started from this tree, not only the installed one.**
- **The owner's rule, said with it:** the window starts the owner; the owner never starts the window, because two programs that start each other can start each other for ever. Read in the tree: the owner starts the window program in one case only, as the small panel of an offered file (`offer_panel.rs`, with `--offer-panel`, while an offer waits and no window is in front, at most once in five seconds); that panel is not a window that keeps or starts an owner. Nothing else in the application, core or broker crates starts it.

### E-081 — Key bindings, step 1: a chord in the contract and an action's suggested chord — IN CODE; the contract's tests pass, lint clean, inside the green gate of 1929 s; NOTHING reads it yet, no chord can be bound

- **Added (`peersus-api`).** `chord.rs`: `Chord` (the modifiers control, alt, shift and the system key, and one key of a closed list: `a`–`z`, `0`–`9`, `f1`–`f12`, enter, space, tab, escape, the four arrows, home, end, delete), built only through `Chord::new` or from its text, so one that exists is one the rules allow. It always holds control, alt or the system key: never something typed. It has one spelling (`"ctrl+alt+1"`: lower case, the modifiers in the order ctrl, alt, shift, super, each once); anything else is refused, at most 32 bytes. It crosses as text and can name an entry of a map. An `Action` gains one optional field, `chord`, the chord its extension suggests: only for an action a key can run as it is (`input` none, not destructive), and one manifest does not suggest one chord twice. An action without it is read and written as before.
- **Updated.** Every literal `Action` of the workspace's tests (nine) says `chord: None`; `docs/api/manifests.md` has "Suggested chords", which says the application does nothing with the field yet; the contract index was generated again.
- **Tests.** Three for the chord (every chord of the 14 allowed sets of modifiers and 59 keys is written one way and read back; 25 misspellings and 6 keys out of range refused; text and map-key round trip) and one for the action's field (where it is allowed, the four refusals, duplicates, unread spellings). The count of the first was wrong when written (12 sets instead of 14) and failed; the test was corrected, not the code.
- **Not done:** the registry, the command, Settings, the window's handler (steps 2–4 of E-073). They wait behind the notification and Upload work the owner put first (E-082).

### E-082 — What the owner saw of Upload on 2026-11-15, what was true of it, and what he ordered: notifications first — FOUND by reading and from two devices' traces; NOTHING changed in code yet

- **What he saw and took for something else.** A question of the Upload extension above the Receive page of a phone (11:44), with a test file: the test of E-076, not announced to him. He took it for a second, new upload extension or a debugging path that pulls files from a phone. Neither exists: there is one Upload extension on every device, with the provider it had in `main`; the question is the consent the HTTP service has always asked before a file leaves, and it was already drawn above the page in the commit of 2026-11-09. Told to him; he read the extension himself and agreed it was not changed.
- **His judgement of that band, and his order.** A message inserted above a page is the old way and alters a page it has nothing to do with; the application has notifications, in the window and outside it, and the question belongs there. The notifications cannot carry buttons today, which is why the band stayed. Ordered, before any work on Upload: an idiomatic rework of notifications with two distinct kinds (in the application, outside it), buttons whose words the sender chooses, agnostic so that an extension has every option; then the band and its code deleted and the upload question shown as an in-app notification.
- **Read: what there is today.** In the window: floating cards of text that go after seven seconds (`Notices`, from the owner's sentences); bands above the page for an extension's question and for a pairing request; the bar of an incoming offer. Outside the window: on Linux the offer's bar in a window of its own, started by the owner while an offer waits and no window is in front (`offer_panel.rs`, `offer_window.rs`); on Android the transfer notifications and an offer panel written in Java. Each decides for itself what it shows. No service lets an extension post a notification.
- **Found while looking, both real.**
  1. **The key check of the HTTP service never runs.** `check` is answered by the service (`file_http.rs`) and imported by the Upload extension, but is not in the table of methods the application offers (`platform_services.rs`, `METHODS`): it is not exported and has no deadline of its own, so the extension's call, which asks ten minutes like its others, is refused at submission. Seen in the traces of a phone and a desktop as the call after `configure` ending `timeout` in the same millisecond. The extension takes any failure of the check for "unverified" and answers that the key was saved. The tests of the check call the service directly and never pass through the broker, which is why they pass.
  2. **On one phone the application wrote "A local frame could not be encoded" 44 times** (class Data), all on 2026-11-15 from 12:25, in bursts about 200 ms apart while the owner was typing in Settings; none in that phone's log of the three days before. A request for the state made from the workstation does not cause it (tried). Which value it is, is not known.
- **Also asked by him, for after the notifications:** "Upload selected file" offered only once an API key is saved, and the extension's settings saying whether the key works.
- **Not verified:** a real upload with the builds of today on any device by this work (it is the owner's key); what he meant by "totally broken" at 12:33 on the workstation, where the action stood at 0 of 35105 bytes waiting for his answer to the question and the owner had no error.

### E-083 — Notifications, step 1: one shape, one list, one answer — IN CODE; lint clean, the unit suites pass (contract 125, core 85, application 706, window 706); NOT installed, seen on no device

- **Why.** The owner's order of E-082: two distinct kinds (in the application, outside it), buttons whose words the sender chooses, the same for the application and for an extension.
- **Added (`peersus-api`, `notification.rs`).** `Notification`: `text` (one paragraph, at most 500 characters), `reach` (`inside` the window, or also `outside` it), `stays`, and up to three `buttons`, each an `id` the sender is told and a `label` of at most 24 characters. It has no size, place or colour: those are the application's. One that stays or has a button waits for the person; any other goes by itself. `Answer` is what the sender is told: `pressed` with the button's id, or `dismissed`. Anything the rules do not allow is refused whole; nothing is cut on the way.
- **Added (`peersus-core`).** `PostedNotification` (the owner's number, who it is for: the application or an extension by id, the notification), `Snapshot.notifications`, and one command, `answer-notification` (a number, and a button's id or none to close it).
- **Added (`peersus-app`, `notifications.rs`).** The list: it numbers what is posted, publishes it in the snapshot, tells the sender the answer, and takes away after seven seconds what does not wait. At most eight of the application's and two of one extension are on show; an extension's own requests are also bounded to six a minute (`admit`, for the service of step 3); everything of an extension leaves when it stops. A sender that stops waiting takes its notification off the screen with it.
- **Added (window).** The cards that already floated over the page for the application's passing sentences now also draw the list: the sender's name for an extension's, the text, its buttons in the sender's order, and a way to close it. At most three are drawn at once and a question keeps its place. One asked for `outside` is drawn in the window too; nothing draws it outside yet (step 4).
- **Updated.** `peersus status` prints the list; `docs/api/local-control.md` has "Notifications".
- **Tests.** The contract's bounds and its wire shape (3); the list: an answer reaches only its sender and only once, a passing one leaves, an extension's limit and rate, everything of an extension leaves with it (4); which cards are drawn (1).
- **Not done, not verified:** nothing was built for a device or seen in a window; the Linux panel and the Android panel still show only offers.

### E-084 — Notifications, step 2: the Upload question and its link are notifications; the band above the page is deleted — IN CODE; lint clean, the same unit suites pass; NOT installed, seen on no device

- **Updated (`platform_services/file_http.rs`).** The service that sends a file asks for consent itself, the same on every platform, through the list of E-083 under the calling extension's name: the file's name and size, the address, that the file goes as it is, the request headers the extension named; buttons `Allow` and `Don't allow`. Every word is the application's. Only `Allow` authorizes: `Don't allow`, closing it, no answer within 300 s, or the extension stopping all end as `cancelled`. The link a destination answers with is a notification with one button, `Copy link`; it is copied only when that is pressed. A long name or link is shown with its middle left out and used whole. A question that would not fit a notification is refused and nothing is sent.
- **Removed.** The consent and link halves of the platform adapters' interaction (the trait is now: choose a file, the key form, read the key, copy); the band above the page in the window, with `plugin_controls::HostRequest`, `view::extension_request` and its test; `ExtensionPromptKind` (the list of forms an extension opened now holds only the key form: number, extension, address).
- **Updated.** Desktop and Android services create the list and hand it to the owner, which publishes and answers that one. The tests of the service, of its transport and of the built Upload extension's pipeline answer through the list. `peersus answer` is only for the key form; a consent is answered with `peersus call '{"command":"answer-notification","id":N,"button":"allow"}'` (the command's help says so). `docs/api/service-messages.md` says how consent and the link are shown.
- **Tests added.** What the consent says (name, size, address, headers, a long name shortened, one that does not fit refused); a consent is the extension's notification and only `Allow` authorizes.
- **Visible change, to be said before it is installed:** the Upload question no longer pushes the page down; it is a card over the page, like the passing sentences, and stays until answered.
- **Not done, not verified:** not seen in any window; a real upload is the owner's to make. The words of the consent and of the link's button are the application's but are written when posted, in English: in another language they are shown in English (a card translates only what is the application's own notification, and these are shown under the extension's name). Whether the deleted band translated them was not checked.

### E-085 — Notifications, step 3: any extension can ask for one (`peersus.notify`) — IN CODE; lint clean; proven through the real owner and hub with a real extension process; NOT installed, seen on no device

- **Added.** One method of the application, `host` / `peersus.notify` / `1` / `show`, guarded by a permission of its own, `notify.show` ("Show you notifications under its name, with its own words and buttons, in the window and outside it"), granted once and not per device. Its arguments are the contract's notification as it is; nothing in the service knows any extension. One that passes is answered at once; one that waits is answered with the person's answer; the call's deadline, its cancellation or the extension stopping takes it off the screen. Refusals show nothing: `refused` (not a notification the contract allows), `rate-limited` (more than six requests a minute, refused ones counted), `busy` (two already on show), `cancelled`, and `denied` from the hub without the permission.
- **Where.** Declared in the one table of the application's methods (`platform_services.rs`), so its export, its permission's words and its deadline are derived like every other; answered by the owner's own list (`Notifications::show`), on every platform, with no adapter. `withdraw` as a method was not added: ending the call is the withdrawal.
- **Proven with.** The test extension (`plugins/peersus-test`): two buttons on its page ask for a passing notification and for a question that reaches outside, and a line says what was answered (it redraws by itself while one waits). The test process of the integration tests gained a general mode (`ask`: one call to a named service once a file exists). **Versions:** this work first raised the test extension's and the Upload extension's version numbers; the owner ordered on 2026-11-15 that nothing is versioned while the project is in development: every package and extension stays at 0.1.0 until he says the project leaves development. Both were put back to 0.1.0 (the test extension had stood at 0.1.1 since the update trial of E-029).
- **Tests.** The service on the list (shape, the answer's wire form, unknown fields refused, the rate with refused requests counted, the limit on show, an extension that stops); the extension's own (3, one of them this); and the whole way in `tests/plugins.rs`: two real processes import the service, only the one granted is shown, under its id, with its buttons; a button it does not have changes nothing; the pressed one comes back to the process as `{"answer":"pressed","button":"drop"}`; the other gets `denied`. 18 tests of that file run, all pass.
- **Updated.** `docs/api/service-messages.md` has "Notifications an extension asks for"; the permission's words are in the catalogue in the eleven other languages (the test that every permission of the application is translated failed without them), written by this work and not reviewed by speakers.
- **Not done, not verified:** the rebuilt test extension was not packaged or installed; nothing seen in a window.

### E-086 — Notifications, step 4, first part: the Linux panel shows what reaches outside; the pairing request is a card of the stack, its band is deleted — IN CODE; NOT installed, seen on no device

- **Updated (owner, Linux).** The panel that announced only a waiting offer is started also while a notification that reaches outside is on show and no window is in front (`offer_panel.rs`); it draws those notifications under the offer with the window's own card and answers with the same command (`offer_window.rs`, `posted_card`, `view::outside_cards`). A press at the end of carrying the panel is not an answer, as for the offer.
- **Updated (window).** A pairing request is drawn as a card of the floating stack, before the posted notifications, with what it had: the three numbers, Refuse, the clock, its words in the person's language. **Removed:** the band above the page that held it and pushed every page down.
- **Decided, to be said to the owner:** the pairing request and the incoming offer are still the application's own components and are not entries of the list. As plain notifications a pairing request would lose its running clock and its translated words (a notification's text is fixed when posted, and has at most three buttons: the fourth answer, Refuse, would be the cross), and an offer would lose the choice of which files to take and its time left. Not done without his word.
- **Not done:** Android shows a notification that reaches outside only in the window (its system notification and panel are written for offers, in Java); Windows and macOS have no announcement outside the window at all.
- **Tests.** Which cards the panel draws (only those that reach outside, named as in the window). The rule that starts the panel has no test of its own: a snapshot cannot be built in that crate's unit tests without all of its fields.

### E-087 — "A local frame could not be encoded" on a phone (E-082): the Send page's action on the chosen file cannot leave an Android window — FOUND by reading; NOT reproduced, NOT fixed

- **Read.** An Android window is attached to its owner through the local socket like a desktop window (`link.rs` `open_at` → `ipc::attach`), though both are in one process: every command it sends is written as a frame. On Android the button of an action that works on the file chosen on Send (`plugin_controls.rs`, `SelectedControls`) sends `InvokeSelectedAction`, which is marked never to be written to a frame (`#[serde(skip)]`, "trusted composition only"). Writing it fails in `write_frame` with exactly that warning and class (a refusal of the serializer, `Data`), the window's request ends as "Local command failed", and the owner never hears of the press. The committed tree of 2026-11-09 is the same.
- **What follows, if the reading is right.** "Upload selected file" pressed on a phone does nothing and says nothing. How the uploads from a phone made earlier by this work were started was not looked up again; if they were started by a command from the workstation naming a path (which is serializable), that is why they worked. Inferred, not checked.
- **Not verified.** The log of that hour is gone from the phone's buffer (looked: it now begins at 13:23); the 44 lines were not tied to presses of that button, and the owner said he was typing in Settings then, which this does not explain. To verify: press the button on a phone and look for the line.
- **The route that exists for the like.** Text shared to an extension from Android (`InvokeSharedText`, also never written to a frame) is sent by the Android adapter through the owner's own in-process client (`peersus-ui-android/src/plugins.rs`). The selected-file action can go the same way through an ability the platform gives the window, which also removes the `cfg(target_os = "android")` branch in the shared window code. Part of the Upload work.

### E-088 — Notifications, step 5: the application's red sentences are NOT moved onto the list — DECIDED from reading, nothing changed in code

- **What the step was.** When something fails the owner says a fixed sentence; the window shows each new one as a red card that goes after seven seconds. The question was whether those should be entries of the notification list of E-083, so that there is one path and not two.
- **Read.** The owner keeps the last sixteen sentences and a count of how often it spoke (`runtime.rs` `say_error`); both are in the snapshot. Two things read them: `peersus status`, which prints the list so a script can see what failed after the fact, and the window, which works out which sentences are new and times them itself (`view::fresh_notices`, `Notices` in `lib.rs`). The window puts two more kinds of sentence through that same timing which the owner never says: what is wrong with an installed extension, worked out by the window in the person's language (`view::extension_health_notices`), and a file that was not delivered.
- **Why not.** Moving the owner's sentences onto the list would not leave one path. The list of sixteen has to stay for `status`, because a notification that passed is gone and a script reads later. The window's own timing has to stay for its two kinds. So there would be three places instead of two. It would also cost something seen: a failure is drawn red and a question blue, and a notification has no field that says which; adding one to the contract every extension uses, only so the application can colour its own failures, is not justified by any extension's need.
- **What would change the answer.** If `status` no longer needs the history, or if extensions come to need a notification that reads as a failure, the step becomes a removal and is worth doing then.
- **Not verified by running:** this is a reading of the code as it stands with E-083 … E-086 in the tree.

### E-089 — Pairing: the two devices show the same three numbers, never in the same places — IN CODE (owner's request of 2026-11-15); tests and lint running when written; NOT installed, seen on no device

- **Asked by the owner.** Both devices listed the three numbers in one order, so two people could each press "the first one" without reading anything. He asked for different orders (his example: 1, 2, 3 on one and 2, 3, 1 on the other), with nothing made harder for the person.
- **Updated (`peersus-core`, `pairing.rs`).** The numbers are derived from the session as before, the same on both devices. The device that answers the invitation now shows them turned by one or two places, which of the two derived from the session like the numbers. A turn of one or two moves every number, so no number is at the same place on both devices: the same place pressed on both is always two different numbers and is refused, in every session. What is committed, sent and compared is the number and never its place, so the exchange between devices is unchanged, and a device made before this pairs with one made after (it shows the unturned order).
- **Updated (words).** The card said "Pick the number the other device shows", which was not what the ceremony is (both show three, and both choose). It says "Pick the same number on both devices"; the hint under the devices nearby says "the same number" where it said "the same circle". Both in the eleven other languages, written by this work and not reviewed by speakers. `AGENTS.md` ("Contractul de asociere") and `docs/nearby-send.md` say the same.
- **Tests.** The ceremony's own: the two lists hold the same numbers and differ at every place; over 256 sessions both turns occur and the same place on both devices always ends as a mismatch. Four tests of the network crate chose "the second number" from each device's own list and meant the same number: they now name the number.
- **Offered to the owner, not built.** A simpler ceremony for the person would be the one phones use to confirm a sign-in: one device shows one number, the other offers three and the person presses the one shown. One press instead of two agreed ones. It changes the exchange between devices (old and new builds would not pair) and the contract above, so it waits for his word.

### E-090 — Upload: the key check can run, the upload is offered only with a key, the settings say what the provider thinks of it, and a phone's window can start an upload — IN CODE; the extension's own tests pass; lint and the engine's tests running when written; NOT installed, seen on no device

- **Fixed (the defect of E-082).** `peersus.http` `check` is declared in the table of the application's methods, so the hub lets an extension call it. Covered by the test that every method of that table is refused without its permission and admitted with it.
- **Added to the application, for any extension (nothing in it names one).**
